22
23    /// <summary>
24    /// Deletes a market by ID.
25    /// </summary>
26    void DeleteMarket(MarketId marketId);
27 }

So, enough of the interfaces. We'll see how markets are used. The market concept affects almost
every aspect of Episerver Commerce.

      · As from previous chapter, we've learned that an entry might or might not be available in a
        market. This is defined by a special metafield named _ExcludedCatalogEntryMarkets. This
        contains list of MarketId which the entry is not available in _ExcludedCatalogEntryMarkets
        metafield.

      · Markets also control the prices: a price must belong to a market. Warehouses and inventories,
        however, are not tied to any markets (which is reasonable because a warehouse might serve
        more than 1 markets)

      · Orders must belong to specific markets. Shipping methods and payment gateways also need
        to be available in a specific market, however, they are still grouped by languages. This was
        because of backward compatibility reasons, and in the future versions shipping methods and
        payment gateways might be changed to be grouped by markets.

                                               A market must always be set for an order

All market-specific information are determined by MarketId. It's important to determine which
market a customer is currently in - this is handled by ICurrentMarket:

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 3.1 Markets                                                          80

1 public interface ICurrentMarket

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets the current market.

5     /// </summary>

6     IMarket GetCurrentMarket();

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Sets the current market.

10    /// </summary>

11    void SetCurrentMarket(MarketId marketId);

12 }

      ICurrentMarket is indeed a bad name for an interface. It should have been
      ICurrentMarketService or something like that.

    The only method which needs to be implemented is GetCurrentMarket - you can simply "ignore"
    SetCurrentMarket - for example if you determine the market of customer based on the geography
    location, then you don't want the users to set the market themselves. You can find a simple
    implementation of ICurrentMarket in both Commerce Sample site (MarketStorage) and QuickSilver
    site (CurrentMarket). Both use cookies to store the selected MarketId.

                         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
  Chapter 3.2 Application-level settings

    Beside Market, there are several other important setting which are shared across an Episerver
    Commerce site. Those settings are available in SiteContext, IApplicationContext and AppContext
    objects.
    SiteContext and AppContext are both accessible in singleton objects: SiteContext.Current and
    AppContext.Current. IApplicationContext is registered via ServiceLocator as singleton but there
    is no property available.

                     This is of course not optimal structure, but it comes with history reasons. SiteContext and
                     AppContext have been there since Mediachase-era, and while SiteContext was intended
                     to be used in site and AppContext was for both site and other applications (console, for
                     example), the distinction has never been clear. ApplicationContext was introduced later in
                     EPiServer.Commerce to handle content-related settings. There should only be one global-
                     level settings object, however doing such thing will certainly result in breaking changes. We
                     will have to live with these three objects, at least for now.

    The most important setting in SiteContext is CurrentCurrency. If your site allows customers to
    select billing currency, this is the place to go

1 SiteContext.Current.CurrentCurrency = selectedCurrency;

                                          Selecting currency, simple implementation in QuickSilver

    There're some things to remember when you set the CurrentCurrency setting:
Chapter 3.2 Application-level settings                                                        82

· This setting is stored by cookie. Other places only see the new CurrentCurrency in the next
  request. So it's required to refresh the page after setting the value.

· Prices are currency-dependant (We'll talk more about this in next part), so make sure to fresh
  prices of all items, especially items in the cart.

AppContext comes with several settings. The one you need to use the most is ApplicationId, a Guid
to construct the CatalogKey which is used in many features of Episerver Commerce:

1 var catalogKey = new CatalogKey(AppContext.Current.ApplicationId, entryCode);

    However, ApplicationId is going to be obsolete soon - which is planned for Commerce 11. It's a
    good thing - it has not been serving its true purpose, to allow multi-tenancy in Commerce. One
    less class to care about, in Commerce 11 you will be able to use Code of the entry directly. Another
    setting in AppContext is Azure-specific. AzureCompatible is a boolean flag to indicate if this site
    is compatible with Microsoft Windows Azure environment. It's not entirely true, however, it only
    shows if the metafield encryption is disabled or not in the database - because at this time of writing,
    SQL Azure Database still does not support encryption. Both ApplicationId and AzureCompatible
    are read-only, they're determined automatically by the state of the site.

At this time of writing, AzureCompatible is already obsoleted. It was introduced in Com-
merce 8 to mark if the database should use column encryption or not. At that time, SQL
Azure Database did not support column encryption, so to upload a database to the Azure, the
encryption must be removed. Now SQL Azure Database does support it, and it's up to site's
admin to decide if they want to use it or not. There is a new flag named IsEncryptionEnabled
which serves the same purpose (but with reverse meaning).

IApplicationContext is probably the most important of all. It has two properties:

   · HasContentModelTypes - indicates if the current site context contains the catalog content
      types, in other words - it separates the front-end site and anything else (Commerce Manager,
      ServiceAPI, unless you have your catalog content types in your ServiceAPI site). This setting
      is used by several parts of the system - for example, Find.Commerce, to see if the it should
      index the content or not. HasContentModelTypes is read-only and can't be set - it depends on
      the assemblies in the bin folder.

   · DisableVersionSync - we've learned in previous chapter how it works and how can it be
      configured in ecf.catalog.config, the other way is via IApplicationContext.

The default implementation of IApplicationContext - ApplicationContext has another extra flag -
SkipContentModelCheck. It allows the site to safely ignore the HasContentModelTypes flag - in other
words, a workaround to set a read-only flag.

                                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Part 2: Inventories and Prices
Chapter 4: Prices.

In Commerce R2 and earlier, prices were a part of entries. If you remember the structure of
CatalogEntryDto in first chapter, you can still see the SalePrice table there. At first, it seems to
be reasonable as the prices seems to be naturally belong to SKUs. However, this design has a serious
flaw, it makes it harder to integrate to an external system, such as ERP, to update prices regularly
and independently (Updating entries will fire certain events, which we will discuss later).

                                                   Price editing in Commerce Manager

Commerce R3 came with a new design - the prices are now separated and no longer tightly coupled
with the entries. Of course, they still tie to the entries in certain way - by matching codes.

                 There are three ways to identify an entry (or a node): An Id (int), a code (string) or an
                 unique identifier (Guid, introduced in Commerce 7.10). An Id is used internally within the
                 catalog, and a Guid is used to communicate with Episerver CMS (if you have worked with
                 Episerver CMS, you might know the concept of permanent link), and a code is used to work
                 with external systems, such as ERP or PIM. Almost every feature which might work with
                 external system works with code.

The new design also came with two interfaces - IPriceValue and IPriceService. The former, as its
name might suggest, is for the price value itself, and the latter provides actual methods to work with
those values.
Let's see what an IPriceValue has:
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                             85

1 public interface IPriceValue

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets the catalog entry key.

5     /// </summary>

6     CatalogKey CatalogKey { get; }

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Gets the market.

10    /// </summary>

11    MarketId MarketId { get; }

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// The customer pricing.

15    /// </summary>

16    CustomerPricing CustomerPricing { get; }

17

18    /// <summary>

19    /// The date and time in UTC when this price data became or becomes active.

20    /// </summary>

21    DateTime ValidFrom { get; }

22

23    /// <summary>

24    /// Gets the valid until date and time in UTC. Null means no end date.

25    /// </summary>

26    DateTime? ValidUntil { get; }

27

28    /// <summary>

29    /// The minimum quantity that this pricing applies to.

30    /// For example, if MinQuantity = 4 then customer must by at least 4 to

31    /// get the UnitPrice.

32    /// </summary>

33    decimal MinQuantity { get; }

34

35    /// <summary>

36    /// The price per unit of quantity.

37    /// </summary>

38    Money UnitPrice { get; }

39 }

    Almost all the properties explain for themselves. A price for a specific market (MarketId), has a price
    in a currency (UnitPrice) and will be valid for a minimal quantity of purchase (MinQuantity, so only
    purchase with at least MinQuantity will be eligible for that price) in a range of time (ValidFrom and

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                                              86

    ValidUntil, ValidFrom must be present, while ValidUntil can be null, indicating that the price
    will be valid until further notice). However, there are two things which might be interesting here:
    CatalogKey and CustomerPricing.

    As we mentioned earlier, the code is the way to identify an entry when you work with external
    systems. CatalogKey is a little more than that - it has one ApplicationId as Guid and a Code as
    string. You can construct a CatalogKey by this:

1 var catalogKey = new CatalogKey(AppContext.Current.ApplicationId, entryCode);

    CatalogKey is an important little class and is used intensively in price and inventory system.

       What is an ApplicationId and why is it here ? In the early days of Episerver Commerce,
       there was an concept of multi-tenant in just one Commerce database, meaning you can have
       multiple "Applications". Each application is identified by an ApplicationId, which why if
       you look at most important tables in Commerce database, such as Catalog, CatalogEntry
       or CatalogNode, you will see that column there. However, the idea has never been fully
       implemented, and it has never really worked, so in your database, it is always one
       ApplicationId everywhere. It's safe to always use AppContext.Current.ApplicationId.
       The concept of multi-tenant was removed in Commerce 11, and CatalogKey now contains
       only the entry code.

    The second interesting thing is CustomerPricing. What is it for?

    You might have different prices for markets or currencies for an SKU, but you might also want to
    have different prices for different customers (or types of customers): corporate customers might get
    better prices than personal customers. CustomerPricing allows you to set such prices. It's a simple
    class with just two properties.

1 public class CustomerPricing : IEquatable<CustomerPricing>

2{

3   public static readonly CustomerPricing AllCustomers = new CustomerPricing(PriceT\

4 ype.AllCustomers, string.Empty);

5

6   public enum PriceType

7   {

8      /// <summary>

9      /// Gets prices valid for all customers. PriceCode is supposed be an empty s\

10 tring when AllCustomers is specified.

11     /// </summary>

12     AllCustomers = 0,

13

14     /// <summary>

15     /// Gets prices valid for a specific customer. PriceCode is supposed be the \

                           Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                                                    87

16 customer's username.

17       /// </summary>

18       UserName = 1,

19

20       /// <summary>

21       /// Gets prices valid for a specific price group. PriceCode is supposed be t\

22 he price group identifier.

23       /// </summary>

24       PriceGroup = 2

25    }

26

27    /// <summary>

28    /// Gets the price type id.

29    /// </summary>

30    public PriceType PriceTypeId { get; private set; }

31

32    /// <summary>

33    /// The price code. The meaning of this depends on the value of PriceTypeId.

34    /// </summary>

35    public string PriceCode { get; private set; }

36 }

    You can have either price for AllCustomer (which is the default value), for a specific customer
    (PriceType = UserName and PriceCode is the username), or for a specific group (PriceType =
    PriceGroup and PriceCode is the name of that group, usually a name of an organization).

    Almost every API from Episerver Commerce will take an IPriceValue (or a collection of them),
    and will return an IPriceValue (or a collection of them). It's worth noting that those will be read-
    only (if you look back a bit you can see that IPriceValue only defines get on properties, but
    set). The default implementation will use ReadOnlyPriceValue, which is an immutable internal
    class from Commerce. If you want to construct or update an value, you can use PriceValue from
    Mediachase.Commerce.Pricing. It has a constructor which takes an instance of IPriceValue and
    copy all the properties, so you can start editing them.

         For any data classes which are used in caching - like prices - it's a best practice to make the
         data immutable. That will avoid the chance of data in cache being changed unintentionally.

    Now we move to next part - the interface for managing prices: IPriceService. Let's see what
    IPriceService has to offer:

                               Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                              88

            IEnumerable<IPriceValue> priceValues);

      In my opinion - this interface is too "thick". The interface should have "minimal viable"
      signature, and other methods which can be extension methods instead. For example,
      the interface should contain SetCatalogEntryPrices(catalogKeys, priceValues), but
      SetCatalogEntryPrices(catalogKey, priceValues) should be in an extension method.
      It is nothing more than creating a list with only one CatalogKey (and unsurprisingly, it's
      the way it is implemented in the default implementation). The overload which takes only
      one CatalogKey is added there for convenience, but it does not really add any critical
      functionalities to the system. Bigger interfaces take more time to implement, so it is harder
      to override something. Sadly, this trend can be seen across the price and inventory systems,
      where many interfaces contain way more methods than they should.

The methods are quite clear of what they do (Except ReplicatePriceDetailChanges which we
will talk about shortly). There is one important characteristic of the default implementation of
IPriceService - it optimizes prices before saving. The prices loaded from the system might not
be the prices you saved. What does "optimize" mean in this case? It prioritize "best prices". Let's
take an example:
Assuming you have those prices, all have the same market, min quantity and currency:

   · Unit Price 100$, Valid From: 1/1/2016 to 12/31/2016

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 4: Prices.                                                                            90

· Unit Price 80$, Valid From: 4/1/2016 to 6/30/2016
· Unit Price 60$, Valid Form: 5/1/2016 to 5/31/2016

Input those prices in the price editing view in Catalog UI. Guess what will you get after you click
Back (will return you to the read-only prices view, which use IPriceService under the hood)?

It might be surprising for you to find out, those are the results you get:

· Unit Price 100$, Valid From: 1/1/2016 to 3/31/2016
· Unit Price 80$, Valid From: 4/1/2016 to 4/30/2016
· Unit Price 60$, Valid Form: 5/1/2016 to 5/31/2016
· Unit Price 80$, Valid From: 6/1/2016 to 6/30/2016
· Unit Price 100$, Valid From: 7/1/2016 to 12/31/2016

If there are overlapped periods, the lowest price will win. This applies to the Min Quantity, too. The
lower price with lower Min Quantity will override the higher price with higher Min Quantity (If
a price is lower, but has higher Min Quantity, then both will be kept). In short, the most beneficial
prices for customers will be the saved, the other will be "discarded".

Well, that's the behavior of the default implementation. It might be confusing at times when
you don't get the prices you imported (obviously higher prices can be removed, and prices
time can be changed). This behavior, however, can be changed, which we will discuss later.

Another important thing about IPriceService: it is optimized for batch processing. It works really
well when you want to load the price to display to customers, but it comes with a caveat. When
you save, you actually save the entire prices of a SKU, you can't just save exactly one price, because
there is no way to identify it. You will have to load all prices, update the unit price of specific market
and currency, then save all of them back. This is of course less than optimal. But you don't have to
use IPriceService - another one comes to rescue.

When Episerver Commerce 7.5 was being developed, there was a requirement to edit price inside
the new Catalog UI. Prices need to be edited individually. To solve that problem, IPriceDetailValue
and IPriceDetailService were introduced.

                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 4: Prices.                                                                                 91

                                         Price Editing in Catalog UI

    The price editing in Catalog UI is actually saved as long as you left a field. So a price can be
    saved multiple times in one edit.

   There are two price views in Catalog UI. The first one shows the price values of a variant, which is
   equivalent with the price editing tab in Commerce, but readonly. This one - as we mentioned earlier
   - uses IPriceService internally (strictly speaking, it uses ReadOnlyPricingLoader, which calls to
   IPriceService. The second one actually allows you to edit the prices, and it uses IPriceDetailValue
   and IPriceDetailService.

                    Strictly speaking, we have 3 price views in Catalog UI. The third one is the view for entire
                    prices in a Category (Node), or a Product: it will list prices of all variants in that Category
                    or Product. This third view is also read only.

   What is the difference between IPriceValue and IPriceDetailValue, and between IPriceService
   and IPriceDetailService?

   Well, between two data classes, not much.

1 public interface IPriceDetailValue : IPriceValue

2{

3   long PriceValueId { get; set; }

4}

   Yay, IPriceDetailValue is just IPriceValue, with an Id. This will allow you to identify a specific
   price to edit or delete it individually, without saving the entire set price of an entry. Let's see what
   IPriceDetailService provides:

                       Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                          92

1 public interface IPriceDetailService

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// Gets a value indicating if this implementation can modify prices.

5   /// </summary>

6   bool IsReadOnly { get; }

7

8   /// <summary>

9   /// Gets a specific price value.

10  /// </summary>

11  IPriceDetailValue Get(long priceValueId);

12

13  /// <summary>

14  /// Gets all price details of a catalog content.

15  /// </summary>

16  IList<IPriceDetailValue> List(ContentReference catalogContentReference);

17

18  /// <summary>

19  /// Gets all price details for a catalog content with paging support.

20  /// </summary>

21  IList<IPriceDetailValue> List(ContentReference catalogContentReference,

22         int offset, int count, out int totalCount);

23

24  /// <summary>

25  /// Gets all price details for a catalog content with paging

26  /// support and filter for market, currencies and CustomerPricing:s.

27  /// </summary>

28  IList<IPriceDetailValue> List(ContentReference catalogContentReference,

29         MarketId marketId, PriceFilter filter, int offset, int count, out int totalC\

30 ount);

31

32  /// <summary>

33  /// Saves the specified price values.

34  /// </summary>

35  IList<IPriceDetailValue> Save(IEnumerable<IPriceDetailValue> priceValues);

36

37  /// <summary>

38  /// Deletes the specified price values.

39  /// </summary>

40  void Delete(IEnumerable<long> priceValueIds);

41

42  /// <summary>

43  /// Sets the price detail values to reflect changes in IPriceService.

                              Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 4: Prices.                                                              93

44    /// This should ONLY be used by implementations of IPriceService>.
45    /// </summary>
46    void ReplicatePriceServiceChanges(IEnumerable<CatalogKey> catalogKeySet,
47
48 }        IEnumerable<IPriceValue> priceValuesList);

There's one thing to remember about the List method. If the catalogContentReference is a variant
or a package, it will return the its prices - as usual. However if it is a product or bundle, the combined
prices of all children variants will be returned. This is also true if it is a category, but only prices of
direct children entries(no recursive).

There are three important characteristics of the default implementation of IPriceDetailService:

      · First, it saves whatever you tell it to save. So no optimization at all, you'll get what you set.
        As-is. Even if you have duplicated prices, it will happily save it. (Actually, the price editing in
        Catalog UI allows you to duplicate a price, so you can easily create a new one)

      · Second, it does not use cache. For a system designed for editing, it's less than effective to have
        the cache floating around when you won't hit it the next time.

      · And last one, it does sync with IPriceService. So if you save prices by IPriceDetailService,
        it saves the values as-is, but it also synchronizes the changes to IPriceService. Of course, next
        time you load prices by IPriceService, it will still return optimized prices.

                 The syncing works both way, so if you save prices using IPriceService, it will
                 also sync all the changes to IPriceDetailService. That's why both IPriceService
                 and IPriceDetailService both have IsReadOnly property, and both have quite simi-
                 lar ReplicatePriceServiceChanges/ReplicatePriceDetailChanges methods. The property
                 lets the caller (for example, IPriceDetailService instance, knows if it should sync prices
                 using the IPriceService, if yes, the method will actually do the work.

So there are some rules of thumbs when you're working with prices:

   · If you are loading prices to do calculation or to show to customers, use IPriceService.
   · If you are saving prices, use IPriceDetailService.

Those are not absolute rules, nothing prevents you to use IPriceService to save prices, but it will
be less effective. There is also nothing prevents you from adding cache to your implementation
of IPriceDetailService so you can use them for loading, but that would mean you have to
filter duplicated prices every time (not to mention that loading by the default implementation of
IPriceDetailService is not backed by cache).

                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                                                      94

             You can argue that the two price system can cause confusion, and I can agree to some
             extend - it might be not the best design. The argument is that was for better performance.
             For other interfaces, such as for inventories which we will exam shortly, the updates
             usually follow the reads, but for pricing, the updates are much less frequent than the reads.
             But still, the two interfaces are confusing. Perhaps the better design would have been
             following CQRS - Command Query Responsibility Segregation pattern more strictly, and
             make IPriceLoadingService and IPriceSavingService instead.

    Enough of the theory, now let's get our hands dirty with a simple example: How to store the MSRP
    (Manufacturer suggested retail price), usually known as list price for SKU. This price is unlikely to
    change (it'll be changed, like 1 or 2 times in the entire lifetime of the product). If you remember from
    chapter 1 - Catalog system, there was a Display price attached to each variation. However, this is
    not enough, because a product can be sold in multiple markets and for each market, there should be
    a list price. This is a pretty frequently asked feature from Episerver World forums, and we're going
    to build it today.

    How to store the MSRP can be a little tricky - the price system - as well as many parts in the system
    - is extendable in a API:s level, not in database level. So if we want to store a special price, we need
    a small hack - use PriceGroup for what it was not intended for. We will store those prices with
    PriceType = 2 and PriceGroup = "MSRP".

    To make the new prices easy to access, we will try not to implement the interface ourselves, but
    instead, use a very useful feature in C# - extensions methods. Let's start with saving the price:

1 public static void SaveMsrp(this IPriceDetailService priceDetailService, ContentRefe\

2 rence contentLink, MarketId marketId, Currency currency, decimal amount)

3{

4   int totalCount;

5   var priceFilter = new PriceFilter();

6   priceFilter.Currencies = new List<Currency>() { currency };

7   var msrp = (PriceDetailValue)priceDetailService.List(contentLink, marketId, pric\

8 eFilter, 0, int.MaxValue, out totalCount).

9      FirstOrDefault(p => p.UnitPrice.Currency == currency && p.CustomerPricing.Pr\

10 iceTypeId == CustomerPricing.PriceType.PriceGroup && p.CustomerPricing.PriceCode == \

11 "MSRP");

12

13  if (msrp != null)

14  {

15     msrp.UnitPrice = new Money(amount, currency);

16  }

17  else

18  {

19     msrp = new PriceDetailValue();

20     var contentRepository = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<IContentRepositor\

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                              95

21 y>();

22        var entryContent = contentRepository.Get<EntryContentBase>(contentLink);

23        msrp.CatalogKey = new CatalogKey(new Guid(entryContent.ApplicationId), entry\

24 Content.Code);

25        msrp.CustomerPricing = new CustomerPricing(CustomerPricing.PriceType.PriceGr\

26 oup, "MSRP");

27        msrp.UnitPrice = new Money(amount, currency);

28        msrp.MarketId = marketId;

29        msrp.MinQuantity = 0;

30        msrp.ValidFrom = entryContent.StartPublish.Value;

31    }

32

33    priceDetailService.Save(msrp);

34 }

    The code is pretty straightforward - we try to load the existing MSRP, if any, otherwise we create
    one. Perhaps the most notable part is when we set the ValidFrom. This field is required to be not null,
    and the value should be the time when the entry becomes available. However with the abstraction
    of entryContent, StartPublish can be null. In this case we use the current time instead.

    We needed to convert the existing price to PriceDetailValue because the values returned by
    IPriceDetailService.List are IEnumerable<IPriceDetailService>, and IPriceDetailService is
    read-only. And then we can extract that long statement to an extension method on its own - to load
    the MSRP:

1 public static IPriceDetailValue LoadMsrp(this IPriceDetailService priceDetailService\

2 , ContentReference contentLink, MarketId marketId, Currency currency)

3{

4     int totalCount;

5     var priceFilter = new PriceFilter();

6     priceFilter.Currencies = new List<Currency>() { currency };

7     return priceDetailService.List(contentLink, marketId, priceFilter, 0, int.MaxVal\

8 ue, out totalCount).

9         FirstOrDefault(p => p.UnitPrice.Currency == currency && p.CustomerPricing.Pr\

10 iceTypeId == CustomerPricing.PriceType.PriceGroup && p.CustomerPricing.PriceCode == \

11 "MSRP");

12 }

    And then the SaveMrsp become a little shorter (and cleaner):

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                              96

1 public static void SaveMsrp(this IPriceDetailService priceDetailService, ContentRefe\

2 rence contentLink, MarketId marketId, Currency currency, decimal amount)

3{

4     var msrp = (PriceDetailValue)LoadMsrp(priceDetailService, contentLink, marketId,\

5 currency);

6

10    }

14        var contentRepository = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<IContentRepositor\

15 y>();

18 Content.Code);

20 oup, "MSRP");

21        msrp.UnitPrice = new Money(amount, currency);

22        msrp.MarketId = marketId;

23        msrp.MinQuantity = 0;

24        msrp.ValidFrom = entryContent.StartPublish.Value;

25    }

    However, this is not entirely optimal. As we talked earlier - we should use IPriceDetailService for
    editing and IPriceService for loading. We did with editing, but we should implement an extension
    method for loading:

    Of course, there are still improvements can be made - such as making "MSRP" a constant that shared
    across the methods, or you can pass an EntryContentBase parameter instead, so you won't have to

    be able to edit a list price Catalog UI, because the price editor there only allows three predefined
    PriceGroups ("Customer", "Partner" and "Distributor") and we can't change it back in the Catalog
    UI.

    Now let's consider another example of pricing. Prices can be changed quite frequently - on a supply
    and demand basis, or because the items are near EOL (End of Line), or because simply you get a
    better deal from suppliers. In such case, it's not technically promotions, but you still want to show
    the discounted price to the customer. It's pretty strong encourage to say "Was $1200, only $1000"
    now. This is a pretty way to make customers decide to buy.

1 public static IPriceValue GetPreviousPrice(this IPriceService priceService, ContentR\

2 eference contentLink, MarketId marketId, Currency currency)

3{

4     var contentRepository = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<IContentRepository>();

7 nt.Code);

10    var previousPrice = prices.FirstOrDefault(p => p.MarketId == marketId

11       && p.UnitPrice.Currency == currency && p.ValidUntil.HasValue && p.ValidUntil\

12 .Value < DateTime.UtcNow);

14       && p.UnitPrice.Currency == currency && p.ValidFrom < DateTime.UtcNow && (!p.\

                               Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 4: Prices.                                                     99

15 ValidUntil.HasValue || p.ValidUntil.Value > DateTime.UtcNow));

16    if (previousPrice.UnitPrice.Amount < currentPrice.UnitPrice.Amount)

17    {

18       return previousPrice;

19    }

20    return currentPrice;

    The last line is a small but important thing to check - if the price was increased, it's better to silently
    ignore the difference. We only show information if the item is cheaper.

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 5: Inventories

Inventory is another "detachable" system in Episerver Commerce, and yet as important. In real world
scenario, Inventories are usually updated from external systems, such as ERP. So they were designed
with "interface" approach in mind - they are easy to decouple from the system and be replaced by
your own implementation.
Let's examine two concepts: warehouse and inventory.

Warehouse

Warehouse is the place you store the goods, it might be used as a distribution center, or a pickup
location.

                                              Editing a warehouse in Commerce Manager

Same as entries, the code of warehouses are supposed to be unique, and it's used to map between
warehouse and inventories. You'll work with Warehouse via IWarehouse and IWarehouseRepository.
There is nothing special about these interfaces - you can check the documentation at http://world.episerver.com/docu
library/?documentId=commerce/9/8BCAE8CB and http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-
library/?documentId=commerce/9/4A181B5. Normally, you we work with Mediachase.Commerce.Inventory.Wareho
the default implementation of IWarhouse.
This, however, comes with a special note. When you use IWarehouseInventory to get a warehouse,
you get an IWarehouse implemented by ReadOnlyWarehouse - an internal class. Yes, the instance you
get back is readonly and you can't directly edit it. You will need to create a "writable" clone of the
instance, like this
Chapter 5: Inventories                                          101

1 var warehouse = warehouseRepository.Get("warehouseCode");
2 var writable = new Warehouse(warehouse);
3 //edit writable warehouse
4 warehouseRepository.Save(warehouse);

    Warehouse has an implementation that takes an instance of IWarehouse and copies the properties
    to create a new writable instance. If you come from a CMS background, you might find this to
    be strange - it's different from what CMS APIs usually do. In fact, this can be fairly obscene and
    might not be easy for you to find the right way to edit a warehouse. This will be fixed in Commerce
    12. IWarehouse will inherit IReadOnly interface, and to create a writable clone of an IWarehouse
    instance, you can just call CreateWritableClone on it. (This is, however, a breaking change, that's
    why it needs Commerce 12.)

Inventory

While you will hardly work with warehouse API:s, you are supposed to work with inventories, a
lot. Unlike warehouses which are rarely changed, the inventories are adjusted continuously - when
a customer places and order, when a supply comes to a warehouse, etc.

There are again - two API:s for manipulating inventories in Episerver Commerce. The first - and
older one is IWarehouseInventoryService, provides methods to work with IWarehouseInventory.
The newer one is IInventoryService, provides method around InventoryRecord. You might ask,
why.

IWarehouseInventoryService has been there since Commerce 7.5. Its concept is simple: if a SKU
is stored in a warehouse, a record of IWarehouseInventory will be saved for it. The record itself is
quite self-explanatory:

                 In Commerce 9.24, (which is under development at the time of this writing),
                 IWarehouseInventory and IWarehouseInventoryService will be obsoleted. However they
                 are not going to "disappear" anytime soon. Episerver Commerce has a policy of supporting
                 obsoleted APIs for at least 12 months since the version the [Obsolete] attribute is added.
                 This section will be kept in this book for reference - but you are recommended to switch to
                 new inventory API:s.

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                          102

1 public interface IWarehouseInventory

2   {

3      CatalogKey CatalogKey { get; }

4

5      string WarehouseCode { get; }

6

7      decimal InStockQuantity { get; }

8

9      decimal ReservedQuantity { get; }

10

11     decimal ReorderMinQuantity { get; }

12

13     decimal PreorderQuantity { get; }

14

15     decimal BackorderQuantity { get; }

16

17     bool AllowBackorder { get; }

18

19     bool AllowPreorder { get; }

20

21     InventoryTrackingStatus InventoryStatus { get; }

22

26  }

    This maps to the Warehouse Inventory tab in Commerce Manager

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 5: Inventories                                                                                    103

                                      This might not be the best UI in the world, but it works

However it's not editable in Catalog UI:

                                                             This WAS read-only

                 A new UI for editing inventories in Catalog UI was released in Commerce 9.24. Now the
                 final piece of catalog content editing is completed - and you should move to Catalog UI
                 editing entirely. The Catalog Management part in Commerce Manager would be obsolete
                 soon.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 5: Inventories                                                     104

If you are new to Commerce, perhaps it's worth spending sometimes to explain the terminologies
with the inventory: the inventory is for a SKU at a specific warehouse:

· InStockQuantity is the quantity you have in stock - it's the amount of goods that your
  warehouse can fulfill, minus ReservedQuantity. For example, if you have 100 items in store,
  but the reserved level set to 5, you can only sell 95 items.

· ReorderMinQuantity is the minimum quantity for re-order. Once you your instock quantity
  reaches this level, the entry will be shown in the "Low stock quantity" report.

· AllowBackorder is a flag indicating if you allow customers to order this item, even if it's
  currently out of stock, if the BackorderAvailabilityDate, if set, is passed. The orders will
  be fulfilled later when the goods arrive. The number of items ordered by back-order will be
  added up to BackorderQuantity.

· AllowPreorder is a flag indicating if you allow customers to order this item before the day
  it's available, if the PreorderAvailabilityDate, if set, is passed. For example, if you set
  AllowPreorder to true, then your customer still to wait until PreorderAvailabilityDate to
  pre-order. The order will be fulfilled later - the number of items ordered by pre-order will be
  added up to PreorderQuantity.

· InventoryTrackingStatus is a flag indicating if the workflows - should care about the
  inventory at all. If this is set to Disabled, then the workflows won't check if there is any item
  left in store when a customer adds it to cart, or won't adjust the inventory when they place
  the order. Don't be confused with the TrackInventory property of a VariationContent - even
  thought they serves the same purpose. TrackInventory works on SKU level, so if it is set to
  false, it will override InventoryTrackingStatus. InventoryTrackingStatus is at ware-house
  level.

Then how the "old" inventory system work?

If you use a vanilla Episerver Commerce system - meaning you don't have any customization to
the workflows, then usually you work around IWarehouseInventoryService - a pretty big interface
which provides CRUD operations to IWarehouseInventory, as you can find its full documentation
[^here](http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-library/?documentId=commerce/9/56465654).

· You can use IWarehouseInventoryService.List overloads to load the information for entries
  shown to customers. Depends on your website's policies, you might want to show something
  like "7 available", "Less than 5 left", etc. However, it's generally a good practice to at least
  indicate if an item is out of stock or not, and if it should be refilled soon.

· When a customer add an item to cart, you'll normally call CartValidate workflow to do some
  checks (applying promotions etc), and the system will verify if the inventories have enough
  quantity for the goods. In cases when the quantity can't be fulfilled, the quantity might be
  reduced, or the item even removed from the cart. This is done in CheckInventoryActivity.

These steps are not changed in the new system. However the next step was.

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                      105

    · When the customer finally places an order, the value of InStockQuantity (and in some
      cases, PreorderQuantity, if it's a pre-order, or BackorderQuantity, or a combination of
      InStockQuantity and BackorderQuantity) will be updated to subtract (or add, in case the
      warehouse is refilled.) the quantity has been ordered.

    This design works to a point. It serves basic cases quite well, however, it has some major limitations
    which led to the new inventory system:

    · The quantities are set to a calculated value, such as if it was 10 and a customer places an order
      of 2, then it's set to 8. Normally this is non-issue, but if your item is being sold like hot cake
      (and of course, you would want it to be), it's possible that two or more customers are placing
      orders at the same time - then all the threads will try to set the InStockQuantity to 8. This is
      a race condition and causes serious problems.

    · After a purchase order is placed, there is no way to know the status of inventory - is it ready
      for packaging, is it released to shipping, how should we do if a customer cancels the order -
      should we simply revert back the change because the goods were still on the shelf, or should
      we record them as outlet because they were packaged and were ready for shipping? Once the
      changes are made, there are no real way to "revert" them.

    Then come the new inventory system - which is built around IInventoryService - it's supposed
    to be smarter and more flexible than the old one. It was introduced in Episerver Commerce 7.19,
    but was sitting there silently until 8.7, when it was fully integrated into the workflows. (Before
    that, the new APIs were there but the new features were not used by any parts of the system. Any
    inventory operations were done in a "compatible layer", the calls to IWarehouseInventoryService
    are forwarded to work with IInventoryService internally.)

    The new inventory system is much more sophisticated and powerful than the old one, so we will
    examine it piece by piece. Firstly, by the basic "InventoryRecord" class to represent a record of an
    inventory:

    This is more or less map to IWarehouseInventory, which some differences. It's the easiest to explain
    is to look at how an InventoryRecord is converted to an IWarehouseInventory:

1     private IWarehouseInventory RecordToInventory(Guid applicationId, InventoryRecor\

2 d source)

3     {

4          return WarehouseInventory()

5          {

6              CatalogKey = new CatalogKey(applicationId, source.CatalogEntryCode),

7              WarehouseCode = source.WarehouseCode,

8              InStockQuantity = source.PurchaseAvailableQuantity + source.AdditionalQu\

9 antity,

10             ReservedQuantity = source.AdditionalQuantity,

11             ReorderMinQuantity = source.ReorderMinQuantity,

12             PreorderQuantity = source.PreorderAvailableQuantity,

13             BackorderQuantity = source.BackorderAvailableQuantity,

14             AllowBackorder = source.BackorderAvailableQuantity > 0,

15             AllowPreorder = source.PreorderAvailableUtc > new DateTime(1900, 1, 1, 0\

16 , 0, 0, DateTimeKind.Utc),

17             InventoryStatus = source.IsTracked ? InventoryTrackingStatus.Enabled : I\

18 nventoryTrackingStatus.Disabled,

19             PreorderAvailabilityDate = source.PreorderAvailableUtc,

20             BackorderAvailabilityDate = source.BackorderAvailableUtc

21         };

22    }

    We can see that:

      · InventoryTrackingStatus only has Enabled and Disabled now as the other flag - Ignored is
        obsolete. This is mapped to IsTracked.

      · InStockQuantity equals to PurchaseAvailableQuantity plus AdditionalQuantity, while AdditionalQuantity
        is the ReservedQuantity.

                               Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                          108

       · AllowBackOrder will be true if BackorderAvailableQuantity is greater than 0, and AllowPreorder
          will be true if PreorderAvailableUtc is greater than a specific time (the beginning of 20th
          century, only 116 years ago)

    So the way data is stored is changed, but not much. The biggest change is in how it works.

                     When the new inventory system was introduced, the data from old system was migrated to
                     new one, and the old interface was moved to a new proxy class (WarehouseInventoryProxy),
                     and the old class WarehouseInventoryServiceDatabase was obsoleted - and it's not just
                     obsolete in term of API:s - the old class works with the old way of storing data and that
                     means those data are not used anywhere else. This is why you should avoid using concrete
                     class from Episerver unless absolutely necessary. Sticking with interfaces is always the safer
                     way.

    The new APIs are built around the InventoryRecord as we see above, and IIventoryService
    interface. As we mentioned earlier, IInventoryService is a quite big interface, as it provides
    everything you needs to work with the inventory system. Here's just a peak of it:

1 public interface IInventoryService

2   {

3      /// <summary>

4      /// Gets a single inventory record for a catalog entry in a warehouse

5      /// </summary>

6      InventoryRecord Get(string catalogEntryCode, string warehouseCode);

7

8      /// <summary>

9      /// Lists all inventory records.

10     /// </summary>

11     IList<InventoryRecord> List();

12

13     /// <summary>

14     /// Lists all inventory records of requested entry codes.

15     /// </summary>

16     IList<InventoryRecord> QueryByEntry(IEnumerable<string> catalogEntryCodes);

17

18     /// <summary>

19     /// Lists all inventory records matching requested entry codes

20     /// sort by the entry and warehouse codes, with paging.

21     /// </summary>

22     IList<InventoryRecord> QueryByEntry(IEnumerable<string> catalogEntryCodes, i\

23 nt offset, int count, out int totalCount);

24     ...

25  }

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 5: Inventories                                                                       109

I'll say it again: this interface has same issue as IPriceService - it's too thick. It should have been a
small interface with just CRUD operations. Other methods, such as query inventories by warehouse,
should have been in a separate interface, or to extension methods.

It's very important to know that all methods are not equal in term of performance.
Some methods, such as List() is very heavy (it returns all records in your inventory,
which can be a million or even more), and should be avoided. Other methods, such as
QueryByPartialKeys, are not effectively cached. If you want to have best performance, stick
with QueryByEntry. Unlike prices where prices are not frequently updated, the inventories
can be changed continously (when a customer places an order, it's a change already), so
effective and smart caching is very important. Actually QueryByEntry is the most common
operation you'll ever use.

Commerce 10.7 drastically improves the caching of inventory system.

    Now let's try a simple example. When a customer visits a product detail page, you probably want
    to display the number of item in stock. (You might also to do the same with product listing pages).
    Depends on your business requirements, you might want to display the exact number in stock, or
    you only display it when the stock is low (one trick to urge the customer to buy!).

1 var inventoryRecords = _inventoryService.QueryByEntry(new [] { entryCode });
2 var inStock = inventoryRecords.Sum(i => i.PurchaseAvailableQuantity);

    Simple, eh?

                     Yes, it is a simplified sample. In real case you would check and display information if the
                     item is available for pre-order, or if it is currently out of stock, but can be back-ordered and
                     the shipment will be shipped as soon as possible. You can get everything from the collection
                     of IInventoryRecord you have.

Checking inventories.

When an item is added to cart, normally you would like to check if it is still in stock. If it is not, but
it can be pre-ordered, or back-ordered. And it's not just that. For a SKU you can set the MinQuantity
and MaxQuantity, which decides how many items of that SKU can be in a cart - you can either set
the MinQuantity to decide that the customer by at least by batch of 3, or 4 - or set the MaxQuantity
so they can't buy more than that, or a combination of both.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 5: Inventories                                          110

You would not want to do all of those works yourself - that will be a hell of if. Luckiky for us, that
is something the framework provides out of the box. We will look in more details in chapter about
order, but let's have a glimpse here.

To do such task, you can go old-fashioned with workflow (ActivityFlow) like this:

1 OrderGroupWorkflowManager.RunWorkflow(Cart, OrderGroupWorkflowManager.CartValidateWo\
2 rkflowName);

    It's the ValidateItemsActivity which does the work (and some extra).
    Or you can be "modern" and go with the new abtraction APIs:

1 cart.ValidateOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => validationIssues.AddValidationIssues\
2 (item, issue), _lineItemValidator);
3 cart.UpdatePlacedPriceOrRemoveLineItems(_customerContext.GetContactById(cart.Custome\
4 rId), (item, issue) => validationIssues.AddValidationIssues(item, issue), _placedPri\
5 ceProcessor);
6 cart.UpdateInventoryOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => validationIssues.AddValidatio\
7 nIssues(item, issue), _inventoryProcessor);

    These three lines are equivalent with the old fashioned way above, but to check and validate
    inventory, you just need the last line.

    However, checking for inventory is just the first part of solving problem. When an order is placed,
    or when it's handled, you have to let the inventory system know how you want to handle stocks.
    And that can be done by "requests".

Into requests

Checking inventories is just one part of the problems. As a developer, you will have to adjust the
inventories. In the old system, as we know, it's "Setting the value". In the new inventory system, it's
"requesting a change". Whenever you need to adjust quantities, you send a request to the system -
you no longer tell how should the system do it, you only tell the system what you want to do and it
will figure out how to do it (remember, it's supposed to be smarter!). This information is contained
in an InventoryRequest:

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                         111

1 public class InventoryRequest

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets or sets the request date, in UTC.

5     /// </summary>

6     public virtual DateTime RequestDateUtc { get; set; }

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Gets or sets a collection of <see cref="InventoryRequestItem"/> values.

10    /// </summary>

11    public virtual IList<InventoryRequestItem> Items { get; set; }

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Gets or sets additional provider-specific data for the request.

15    /// </summary>

16    public virtual object Context { get; set; }

17 }

    This class is quite simple and easy to follow: RequestDateUtc is of course the time of the request
    in Utc, while Items is a collection of items being requested (customer might be placing an order
    with several items, and you only have to send one request for the entire order. Context is an extra
    object for customization - this property is not used by Episerver itself, but it is reserved for custom
    implementation of inventory system. So what do we have in an InventoryRequestItem?

    Each InventoryRequestItem maps to a LineItem in an order, and beyond that, there are several
    properties which might interest you:

      · ItemIndex: So when we get the response back from the system, we will know which response
        is for which request.

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                      113

    · OperationKey: This is a specialized string which contains almost everything about the request:
      the catalog entry code, the warehouse code, the values of requested purchase/preorder/back-
      order/ quantities, the values of available purchase/preorder/backorder quantities. This value
      plays a very important role in how the system should react in case there is another request.
      For certain RequestType(s), you must have the OperationKey set in InventoryRequestItem.

    · RequestType: A flag indicates how the system should treat the request. There are several types
      of request:

1 public enum InventoryRequestType

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// The request is for a normal purchase.

5   /// </summary>

6   Purchase,

7   /// <summary>

8   /// The request is for a preorder.

9   /// </summary>

10  Preorder,

11  /// <summary>

12  /// The request is for a backorder.

13  /// </summary>

14  Backorder,

15  /// <summary>

16  /// The request is for a purchase or a preorder. The system will compare the ord\

17 er date

18  /// with the purchase and preorder availability dates to determine the appropria\

19 te operation.

20  /// </summary>

21  PurchaseOrPreorder,

22  /// <summary>

23  /// Complete an existing allocation. The operation keys of an existing

24  /// allocation must be specified.

25  /// </summary>

26  Complete,

27  /// <summary>

28  /// Cancel an existing allocation. The operation keys of an existing

29  /// allocation must be specified.

30  /// </summary>

31  Cancel,

32  /// <summary>

33  /// Splits an existing allocation into two parts. The operation keys of an

34  /// existing allocation must be specified.

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                         114

35      /// </summary>

36      Split,

37      /// <summary>

38      /// Executes a custom, provider-specific operation. The operation should be furt\

39 her

40      /// described in the context property.

41      /// </summary>

42      Custom,

43 }

    If you look into first 4 InventoryRequestTypes (Purchase, Preorder, Backorder, and PurchaseOrPreorder,
    you can see they are for placing order (aka they are the "first requests"). The next three ones are
    for subsequent requests, when you make some changes to previous requests, such as commit it
    (Complete), or cancel it (Cancel), or make it into two shipments (Split). The last one is reversed for
    custom implementation, it is not used by default.

      · Context: same as InventoryRequest, it's for the custom implementation.

    So how do the requests work?

    When a customer places an order, the workflow (specifically AdjustInventoryActivity activity)
    will create InventoryRequestItems for each lineitem in the cart:

1 private InventoryRequestItem CreateRequestItem(int lineItemIndex, InventoryRequestTy\

2 pe requestType,

3                                               string entryCode, string warehouseCo\

4 de, decimal delta)

5{

6       return new InventoryRequestItem(index, requestType, entryCode, warehouseCode, de\

7 lta, string.Empty, null);

8}

    requestType can be InventoryRequestType.Preorder, InventoryRequestType.Purchase or InventoryRequestType.
    depends on the type of order. Note that it does not have to be strictly one "request item" per line
    item. By default, the activity might create two request items for one item if it must be fulfilled
    with both order and backorder. Now we have request item(s) for each LineItem, time to add up an
    InventoryRequest and make the request to the system:

1 var request = new InventoryRequest(DateTime.UtcNow, requestItems, null);
2 var inventoryService = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<IInventoryService>();
3 var response = inventoryService.Request(request);

                             Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                                     115

    With the old system, when you're done with adjusting the inventory, you are ... done. No other
    operations needed. However, the new system requires extra processing - it processes your request
    and returns something. You'll need to act on that `response' somehow - so the next time you want
    to make a change on the request you made earlier. Let's see what in that InventoryResponse:

1 public class InventoryResponse

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets or sets a value indicating if the overall operation was a success.

5     /// </summary>

6     public virtual bool IsSuccess { get; set; }

7     /// <summary>

8     /// Gets or sets the request date, in UTC. This will be the same request date as\

9 specified

10    /// in the request.

11    /// </summary>

12    public virtual DateTime RequestDateUtc { get; set; }

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Gets or sets a collection of responses for each item in the request.

15    /// </summary>

16    public virtual IList<InventoryResponseItem> Items { get; set; }

17    /// <summary>

18    /// Gets or sets additional provider-specific data for the response.

19    /// </summary>

20    public virtual object Context { get; set; }

21 }

    So we only interest with what're in InventoryResponseItem. It's a rather big class, which you can
    find its definition here². However, we only need to care about a special property - OperationKey. It
    contains almost every thing for later use.

    What is an OperationKey? Here's an example of an operation key:

    ZZZSZPAU7GKDBPAPWK29X4KW8WQQ7VS7MPJ99Y6SWJT9XBKW6W99SVAMZZA98KYXMLB9ECZ3SARTX

    It might look cryptic to you (and you don't really have to care about its value), but it's actually
    simple than you would think - It's just a serialized string contains information about a request, and
    is handled by OperationKeySerializer:

      ²http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-library/?documentId=commerce/9/D5082996
                                                   Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                      116

1 public class OperationKeySerializer

2{

3     public virtual string Serialize(InventoryRequestType requestType, InventoryChang\

4 e change);

5     public virtual bool TryDeserialize(string value, out InventoryRequestType reques\

6 tType,

7         out InventoryChange change);

8}

    So an OperationKey contains information about an InventoryRequestType, and an InventoryChange,
    and it can be converted back and forth from those values. InventoryChange is a rather simple class,
    contains the code of the entry, the code of the warehouse and the "changes" of inventory values:

1 public class InventoryChange : InventoryKey, IReadOnly<InventoryChange>

2{

3     public virtual string CatalogEntryCode { get; set; } //from InventoryKey

4     public virtual string WarehouseCode { get; set; } //from InventoryKey

5     public virtual decimal PurchaseAvailableChange { get; set; }

6     public virtual decimal PreorderAvailableChange { get; set; }

7     public virtual decimal BackorderAvailableChange { get; set; }

8     public virtual decimal PurchaseRequestedChange { get; set; }

9     public virtual decimal PreorderRequestedChange { get; set; }

10    public virtual decimal BackorderRequestedChange { get; set; }

11 }

    So OperationKey is a changeset for a specific request. When the InventoryResponse is returned to
    the request, the order system needs to store the information - OperationKeys somewhere. Naturally,
    it belongs to the Shipment. Yes Shipment has a "secret" field OperationKeys, and here's it looks like:

    0:1#ZZZSZPAU7GKDBPAPWK29X4KW8WQQ7VS7MPJ99Y6SWJT9XBKW6W99SVAMZZA98KYXMLB9ECZ3SA
    1:1#ZZZS7PAU7GKDB2VQMPTQ8KYD7M92XEYNWG9964NLLPGXEEYAWM926RA27FT2EEYDMCDTZGV3MP

    This value can be accessed by Shipment.OperationsKeyMap property. OperationsKeyMap is of
    type ConcurrentDictionary<int, IEnumerable<string>>, so for each line item index, we have a
    collection of OperationKey attached to it. Normally, we have only one OperationKey per line item,
    - but there are cases, like when you send a split request, which returns two OperationKey. Those
    OperationKeys are required for later processing, such as completing or cancelling a shipment.

    And then it comes to the time when we actually ship the order, or in the bad case, when the
    customer cancels the order. We just need to send requests with the saved OperationKey and
    InventoryRequestType - depends on the type of action we want to do, either Complete or Cancel.

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 5: Inventories                                                        117

1 private void SendRequests(Shipment shipment, InventoryRequestType requestType)

2{

3     var itemIndexStart = 0;

4     var requestItems = shipment.OperationKeysMap.SelectMany(c => c.Value).Select(key\

5 =>

6           new InventoryRequestItem()

7           {

8                           ItemIndex = itemIndexStart++,

9                           OperationKey = key,

10                          RequestType = requestType

11          }).ToList();

12

13    if (requestItems.Any())

14    {

15       InventoryService.Request(new InventoryRequest(DateTime.UtcNow, requestItems,\

16 null));

17       shipment.ClearOperationKeys();

18    }

19 }

                     Note that after sending the request, we need to call ClearOperationKeys. The data stored in
                     OperationKeys is no longer needed in the future, and we should prevent any wrongdoing
                     with that data.

    Normally, we changes all the items in batch for each shipment. But you can of course change an
    item individually, for example if the customer no longer wants to remove specific item from the
    order. In such cases, you can simply send a Cancel request for that item, and then remove it from
    the OperationKeyMaps.

                     It's a bit trickier if the customer does not want to cancel the item, but to change quantity.
                     The "standard" way to it is to cancel the original line item and request a new one with new
                     quantity.

    So what actually happens under the hood?
    When a request is made to the inventory system, depends on the type of the request, let's say
    in the most common case, Purchase, the values stored in InventoryService table are updated.
    PurchaseAvailableQuantity is decreased, while PurchaseRequestedQuantity is increased. (In cases
    multiple requests are made, for example, both Purchase and BackOrder, the respected values will be
    all updated). There is a sustainable different compared to the old inventory system here. In the old
    system, the values are set by the computed values from application layer. As we mentioned earlier,
    this will cause issues in concurrent cases where two or more threads want to set the values at the

                                                     Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 5: Inventories                                                                            118

same time. In the new system, the values are "adjusted" - the API:s only let the database know which
changes are needed, and the database will handle concurrent itself. I would doubt that we can write
better, more robust concurrent handling code than SQL Server engine itself.

When a shipment is shipped, the Complete request will make sure the change is permanent - the value
in PurchaseRequestedQuantity will be decreased. However if the shipment is cancelled before it is
shipped, the change will be reverted back -

When should you cancel a request? It only makes senses to cancel when the shipment has
not been shipped, or even the goods have not been leaving the shelf. In case the goods have
been packaged, or shipped, you will need to handle it separately - to treat the goods as outlet,
for example. That's the reason when we ship the goods, we use the Complete request: once
the shipment is shipped, the inventory system has done its parts. It entirely depends on your
business rules on how would you treat the shipped-but-cancelled goods.

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Part 3: The order system -
selling is what (really) matters

No matter how shiny your website is, no matter how cool your URL is, and no matter how fancy
the technologies you used to build it - in the end, it's the orders you take that really matter - the
most important measure to a success of a e-Commerce website.
This part will be focus on how the order system works, and how you can do better.
Chapter 6: The Order system.

                 This chapter talks about internal implementation of order system. You'll see quite many
                 APIs which can be considered as "legacy" here (Such as CartHelper). If you are using newer
                 version Commerce (10+), you should be using the new abstraction APIs, which we will
                 discuss in later chapter.

Overview

Order system, as same as the catalog system, is based on the MetaDataPlus system. That means,
you can extend your classes with additional attributes to match with your business need. By
default, it can be seen as even more complicated than the catalog system. With Catalog system,
you only have two base classes (CatalogEntry and CatalogNode, you can consider CatalogEntryEx
and CatalogNodeEx are default classes, but they are rarely used), but for the order system, there are
quite many. Here's the list of default order metaclasses from the default installation:

                                                         Default order metaclasses

How stuffs are structured.

At the center of the Order system, is the OrderGroup. It's base class of three most important
classes in the system: PurchaseOrder, PaymentPlan and ShoppingCart (Well, it's a little inconsistent
here, the class itself is Cart, but the corresponding metaclass is ShoppingCart. We'll use the terms
exchangeably). OrderGroup is a big class - like most of other classes in order system - and here are
some of the most important properties and methods:

    You can refer to this link²¹ for detail information of properties and methods.
    Those classes share almost the same major characteristics:

      · They all have a CustomerId (which maps to a customer, either registered or anonymous), cost-
        related properties (Total, SubTotal, HandlingTotal etc.) and a collection of addresses.

      · They all have OrderForm(s). In most of the cases, there will be only one OrderForm, but
        in some B2B systems, you might see two or more. Each OrderForm will have information
        about payments, promotions attached to that form. Each OrderForm might have one or more
        Shipments, a list of OrderAddress(es):
