    · As the unit to be shipped, each shipment will have one Shipping address (It'll be the address
      of the store, in case it's an in-store pickup shipment), shipping method information, shipping
      cost and so on. Each Shipment has a list of LineItems belong to it.

            This is, however, not entirely true. The lineitems collection belong to the OrderForm, and the
            lineitems in a shipment is only a subset mapped to that shipment. This will be explained in
            later section.

       · Each LineItem has information about the the entry it represents, the quantity, the price
          when it was added to cart (PlacedPrice) , the price considering quantity and discounts
          (ExtendedPrice), and then discounts for itself.

    PaymentPlan is a little different, because it has cycle information attached - we will talk more about
    that later.
    However, those classes are different in the places they are used, and therefore, they are processed
    with different workflows, resulting in different behaviors. You can save a Cart in Commerce
    Manager without any payments attached, but you will need to have a valid payment amount (which
    match the order's total) to be able to save a PurchaseOrder.
    And as OrderGroup and its derived classes are all MetaObject, you can always call .AcceptChanges()
    on an instance of them, to get it saved to the database. And what if you want to delete it? .Delete()
    followed by .AcceptChanges() will do the job.
    Let's explore those classes one by one.

    Cart

    A (shopping) cart is supposed to be unique per market, name and customer.

                                                     Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                           124

           If the customer already has another cart in same market, same name in the system, then
           when he or she try to save another cart, it would result in an exception. Episerver Commerce
           has a check to prevent such thing from happening - the default behavior when a customer
           browses the site is, the existing cart will be loaded if he already has one, a new one is only
           created if there is none in existent. Of course, PaymentPlan and PurchaseOrder have no such
           restriction. It would be very sad - but also funny if I can only buy from your website exactly
           once!

    Cart extends OrderGroup by adding certain const strings for the name of the carts:

1 public class Cart : OrderGroup

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets the default name for the cart.

5     /// </summary>

6     public static string DefaultName { get; }

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Gets the name for the Wishlist.

10    /// </summary>

11    public static string WishListName { get; }

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Gets the exchange name for the cart.

15    /// </summary>

16    public static string ExchangeName { get; }

17

18    ...

19 }

    Those names are important and you should stick with them, unless you have a very good reason not
    to. Parts of system rely on those names, so a custom name might break certain APIs which assume
    you are using the default names.

    When a customer browses your website, he or she would want to add items to cart. We need to
    create a cart first. This is an interesting topic, and sadly, there is no way such as:

1 var cart = new Cart();

    Instead, this is the way we had done since the first version of Commerce until very recently:

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                                             125

1 var cartHelper = new CartHelper();

                     CartHelper is finally obsoleted in Commerce 12. Even if you are in an older version - 10 or
                     11 - I urge you to stop using it and move the abstraction APIs instead.

    CartHelper - as its name might suggest - is a helper class, provides convenient way to access and
    manipulate Cart. It's open sourced in Mediachase.Commerce.Website package. For a very long time,
    it's the way to create and update the shopping cart. CartHelper has another constructors which
    take the name of the cart, the customer Id and the market he is in. The parameter-less constructor
    above will use all default values - the default cart name (Can you guess it? It's "Default"), the current
    customer Id (in form of a Guid - even anonymous customer has one) and the current market:

1 var cartHelper = new CartHelper(name, userId, market);

                     What is so bad about CartHelper? Mostly, because it had become, over the time, the GOD
                     class which does everything about the Cart, new functions had been added without much
                     thinking about patterns - it is doing too much. CartHelper is almost impossible to mock, so
                     it's painful to write unit tests for cart-related functionalities. But hold on, we will talk about
                     new way, very soon.

    And then, we can access the Cart by:

1 var cart = cartHelper.Cart;

    CartHelper constructor will return the existing, or create a new one if none existed.

                              Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.  126

                                                The cart implementation in QuickSilver

Another special form of Cart is wishlist - it is just a Cart with defined name by Cart.WishListName
constant ("WishList", if you wonder). Again, you can choose a different name for your wishlist, just
make sure to stick with it.

                        The wishlist implementation on QuickSilver, note that it displays no quantity

There are several known "best practices" for wishlist, such as you should not care about the quantity
of items in a wishlist, or you should not make a check for inventories when an item was added to
it, or only registered, logged in users have wishlists, when a customer adds an item from wishlist
to cart, it should be removed from wishlist. However, these are not definitive rules, and you can of

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                          127

    course implements your wishlist as your business requires.

           By default, when a customer logs in, his current carts will be merged with his existing carts
           in the system (assuming both carts have line item(s)). We will look into how to change that
           behavior later.

    PaymentPlan

    PaymentPlan is a special form of OrderGroup where you can create and manage the cycle of recurring
    order. For simplest example, think about a subscription. Your customer wants to order a magazine to
    be delivered to his home the 5th of every month, for six months. Instead of having manually create
    an order for him every month (trust me, it's a tedious work, even if you have only few customers),
    you can create a PaymentPlan to handle such requirements. As we said earlier, PaymentPlan is with
    the properties to handle cycles:

1 public class PaymentPlan : OrderGroup

2{

3     public virtual PaymentPlanCycle CycleMode { get; set;}

4     public virtual int CycleLength { get; set;}

5     public virtual int MaxCyclesCount { get; set;}

6     public virtual int CompletedCyclesCount { get; set;}

7     public virtual DateTime StartDate { get; set;}

8     public virtual DateTime EndDate { get; set;}

9     public virtual DateTime LastTransactionDate { get; set;}

10    public virtual bool IsActive { get; set;}

11    ...

12 }

    PaymentPlan is really flexible in term of cycle, it allows you to set whatever cycle your customers
    would like:

1 public enum PaymentPlanCycle

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Unknown Plan Cycle

5     /// </summary>

6     None = 0,

7     /// <summary>

8     /// Daily Cycle

9     /// </summary>

10    Days = 1,

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.                                          128

11    /// <summary>
12    /// Weekly Cycle
13    /// </summary>
14    Weeks,
15    /// <summary>
16    /// Monthly Cycle
17    /// </summary>
18    Months,
19    /// <summary>
20    /// Yearly Cycle
21    /// </summary>
22    Years,
23    /// <summary>
24    /// Custom1 Cycle, use your own time frame.
25    /// </summary>
26    Custom1,
27    /// <summary>
28    /// Custom2 Cycle, use your own time frame.
29    /// </summary>
30    Custom2
31 }

                              Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.  129

                                    Cycle settings for payment plan, it is simpler than it seems

                 Note that if CycleMode is None then the PaymentPlan will only happen once. It will behave
                 like one-time PurchaseOrder.

What if you want an order to be created every 3 days, or every 2 weeks - that's when CycleLength
comes to help - you can set CycleMode to Days and CycleLength to 3, or CycleMode to Weeks and
CycleLength to 2, respectively. Combine CycleMode and CycleLength and your payment plans can
fit into even most complicated subscriptions. You can control the number of subscription to be made,
by setting the value of StartDate and EndDate, and MaxCyclesCount.

                 StartDate and EndDate can be used to set the limit of the cycle if you know the time it would
                 end (when calculating the number of cycle is not trivial, like having an order every week, for
                 6 months), while MaxCyclesCount can be used when you know exactly the number of times
                 you should convert the PaymentPlan to purchase order (having 30 orders, every other week).
                 Those are not exclusive, they can be used in conjunction and which condition is reached first
                 will be used.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                              130

              At this point of writing, there is a bug with LastTransactionDate (and it has been there for a
              little long than you might expect). So basically it can't be null (DateTime type, you know the
              rule). It can't be DateTime.MinValue because it does not fit to database (The minimal value
              for SQL Server DateTime type is 1/1/1753 at 00:00AM, while DateTime.MinValue goes all way
              back to 1/1/1 - it's like millennia ago.). It can't also be SQL Server DateTime minimal value,
              because it would make NextTransactionDate returns wrong value. So to be safe, when you
              create the PaymentPlan, set the current date time to LastTransactionDate.

    You will usually not create a PaymentPlan by code directly, but to create a Cart first, and then convert
    it to a PaymentPlan:

1 var paymentPlan = cart.SaveAsPaymentPlan();
2 paymentPlan.AcceptChanges();

    To turn a PaymentPlan to a PurchaseOrder, it's equally simple:

1 var purchaseOrder = paymentPlan.SaveAsPurchaseOrder();

    However, you normally don't have to do it - the point of PaymentPlan is to make sure it can be
    automated once it's created. Episerver Commerce comes with a scheduled job, named "Subscription
    Payment Plans", which will convert any PaymentPlans which statisfies the conditions (.IsDue returns
    true) to PurchaseOrder. You can set this scheduled job to run daily, and let it does its job.

    PurchaseOrder

    PurchaseOrder is what you want an OrderGroup to be - it's the cart (or a payment plan) which
    has been converted successfully to an order. PurchaseOrder extends OrderGroup with some notable
    properties:

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// Gets or sets the parent order group id. This can be used to relate purchase \

5 orders to a payment plan or other order.

6   /// </summary>

7   public int ParentOrderGroupId { get; set;}

8

9   /// <summary>

10  /// Tracking number. Automatically generated or can be generated using provided \

11 function.

12  /// </summary>

13  public string TrackingNumber { get; set;}

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                 131

14    /// <summary>
15    /// Expiration date can be used for subscription type of orders.
16    /// </summary>
17    public DateTime ExpirationDate { get; set;}
18
19          /// <summary>
20    /// The collection of Return merchandise authorization (RMA).
21
22          /// </summary>
23    public OrderFormCollection ReturnOrderForms { get; }
24 }

    There are several ways to create an order, but normally you will call SaveAsPurchaseOrder method
    on a Cart or PaymentPlan. For example, this is the normal way to create a PurchaseOrder:

1 OrderGroupWorkflowManager.RunWorkflow(Cart, OrderGroupWorkflowManager.CartCheckOutWo\

2 rkflowName, true, dic);

3 Cart.CustomerId = PrincipalInfo.CurrentPrincipal.GetContactId();

4 var po = Cart.SaveAsPurchaseOrder();

5

6 if (_currentContact != null)

7{

8     Cart.CustomerName = _currentContact.FullName;

9}

10 purchaseOrder.ProviderId = "FrontEnd";

11 OrderNotesManager.AddNoteToPurchaseOrder(purchaseOrder, "An order was placed from fr\

12 ont end site", OrderNoteTypes.System, PrincipalInfo.CurrentPrincipal.GetContactId())\

13 ;

14 po.AcceptChanges();

15

16 // Remove old cart

17 Cart.Delete();

18 Cart.AcceptChanges();

    Note that this code has been simplified. But the idea is simple: You run the CartCheckout workflow,
    as it will process payments, adjust inventories, and calculate the totals of the order. Then you save
    the Cart as a PurchaseOrder, add some more information to it (in this case, adding an OrderNote),
    then save it again. Finally, you delete the old Cart.

      It's worth noting that .SaveAsPurchaseOrder() (as well as .SaveAsPaymentPlan()) does not
      really "transform" the Cart into a PurchaseOrder, instead, it creates a new PurchaseOrder
      and copy the data over. Now we have both the Cart and the PurchaseOrder existing in the
      system. That's why we need to call the last two lines - the old Cart needs to be deleted.
      There is no hard requirement on that, but it's a common practice to do so.

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.                      132

As you might see, we set the ProviderId of the purchase order. What's it for? In simplest scenario,
you can use it to distinguish between orders placed by front-end site (end customers) and backend
(Commerce Manager - by editors/managers). It also can be used to see which site customer placed
orders on, in case you have multiple sites. Certain activities, such as ValidateLineItemsActivity,
will validate the prices based on this value - it only allows price of an item to be set manually if
ProviderId is not "FrontEnd". Only merchandisers in Commerce Manager can set specific prices for
an item - for example, to give a discounted price (without any matching promotions) for an angry
customer. For an order with "FrontEnd" ProviderId, the prices of lineitems will be "corrected" to
the default values.

Order notes

In the previous example we had this little line:

1 OrderNotesManager.AddNoteToPurchaseOrder(purchaseOrder, "note...", OrderNoteTypes.Sy\
2 stem, PrincipalInfo.CurrentPrincipal.GetContactId());

    What does it mean? It adds a note into the order for later reference. OrderNotesManager.AddNoteToPurchaseOrder
    takes 4 parameter: the order it is adding the note to, the content of the note, the type of the note, and
    finally, the originator of the note. A common scenario is that you might want to let the customers
    to add notes to the orders - if any. You can use this pattern and only change the note content for
    that.

OrderForm - Shipment - LineItem

We learned about OrderGroup, about PaymentPlan and PurchaseOrder. However, there was a lie
when you save a cart as a purchase order or a payment plan. If the cart is "empty" - aka it does not
contain any items, the saving will fail with an InvalidOperationException. Therefore, we will have
to add LineItems to cart.

                 Only LineItem(s) are needed. If the cart has no shipment by default, the default implemen-
                 tation will add it for you. However, you might want to add shipments to your cart, as we
                 discussed later.

                 There is usually one OrderForm per OrderGroup, however for B2B scenarios, it's possible to
                 have multiple OrderForms. It's possible from APIs perspective, but it's worth noting that
                 Commerce Manager only supports one OrderForm per OrderGroup.

A LineItem represents an item in the order. When you add an item to the cart - it is turned to a
LineItem. As we are using the old order system, we'll use CartHelper for that:

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                              133

1 var cartHelper = new CartHelper();

2 var wishlist = new CartHelper(Cart.WishListName).Cart;

3 var entry = variationContent.LoadEntry();

4 cartHelper.AddEntry(entry, 1, false, new Cart[] {wishlist});

    If you're new to Episerver Commerce, we have plenty thing to talk about. Here we are loading two
    carts: The first one is the default cart ("Default") and the second one is wishlist ("WishList"). We
    are using an overload which removes the item from other carts (wishlist) when it is added to a cart.
    Simplify things a lot for us, right?

    It's also worth noted that CartHelper does not work with neither CatalogEntryDto nor VariationContent,
    but Entry. Entry is a POCO object represents an entry. It has been there since the first version of
    Episerver Commerce, and was supposed to be an object-approach alternative of CatalogEntryDto.
    It's a big class - you can find the full properties and methods of it here²². Outside of being used by
    CartHelper for adding items to cart like the example above, it's hardly useful for anything else - with
    the fact that an Entry is read-only. If you are using Commerce 7.5 and later, you should be using
    EntryContentBase - we're lucky that there are plenty of extensions methods for EntryContentBase
    to load the corresponding Entry.

    There are several overloads of AddEntry- you can have the full list here²³. In this example, we are
    adding an entry with quantity = 1, with no fixed quantity, and we are also removing the item from
    wishlist, if it is already in that list.

                     Fixed quantity means if you want to set the quantity of the item to be a new value. So if you
                     have the quantity to be 3, and fixed quantity is set to true, then the quantity of the cart will
                     be 3, no matter the quantity you currently have the the cart. In the above example, we have
                     fixed quantity = false, so the quantity (1) will be added to the current quantity of the item.

So what's in a LineItem? As other classes in order system, it's a big class with a lot of properties
which have been added over versions - you can find the full class definition here², but here the most
important properties:

²²http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-library/?documentId=commerce/9/A421552
²³http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-library/?documentId=commerce/9/8324683B
²http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-library/?documentId=commerce/9/F1B0EB53

                                             Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                          134

1 public class LineItem

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// The catalog entry code.

5     /// </summary>

6     public string Code { get; set;}

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Display name, usually the display name of the entry.

10    /// </summary>

11    public string DisplayName { get; set;}

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// The quantity of the item.

15    /// </summary>

16    public decimal Quantity { get; set;}

17

18    /// <summary>

19    /// The price per unit of the item when it's added.

20    /// </summary>

25    /// </summary>

    Here's an example of lineitem in cart in Quicksilver:

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.  135

                                                                    A lineitem in cart

    You can see clearly how a lineitem is presented:

        · DisplayName is shown to customer - however, it's the Code (which is also the entry's code)
          which is used by the system.

        · The PlacedPrice is 441.50kr, but there are discount(s), so each item only costs 353.20kr. With the
          quantity of 2, so we have the ExtendedPrice of 706.40kr. The total of LineItemDiscountAmount
          and OrderLevelDiscountAmount is 176.60kr.

    Normally, value such as DisplayName, ExtendedPrice, LineItemDiscountAmount and OrderLevelDiscountAmount
    will be set by other parts of system: DisplayName is copied from the DisplayName of the entry, while
    other values are set by the "calculators" when workflows run. You should only have to care about
    Code and Quantity.

                     It's important to keep in mind that all quantities in Episerver Commerce are (treated
                     as) decimal: lineitem's quantity or values of the inventories. However, depends on your
                     implementation, it might make sense to treat the cart's items quantities as integer. For
                     example, if your site sells clothes, the ViewModel for items in cart should use integer for
                     quantities. It's funny to allow customers buying 3.6 shirts, right?

    A lineitem is not shipped on its own. It's shipped via Shipment. It's time to create a Shipment and
    add it to the OrderForm.

1 var shipment = cartHelper.Cart.OrderForm[0].Shipments.AddNew();

    This will create a shipment and add it to the Shipment of the OrderForm - we are working in a normal
    B2C case so there should be only one OrderForm. Until now, the lineitems we added to cartHelper
    belong to the OrderForm, and can be accessed via OrderForm.LineItems. Time to give them to the
    shipment. How?

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                         136

1 var index = orderForm.LineItems.IndexOf(lineItem);
2 shipment.AddLineitemIndex(index);

    This seems ... harmless. But it can be dangerous than it looks!

The quirk of Shipment and LineItem relationship

At its core, it's the OrderForm which owns the collection of LineItems. Assume we have lineitem
A, B, C with the quantity of 3,4,2, respectively. The indices of A, B, C in the OrderForm.LineItems
collection are 0,1,2. In case you have only one shipment, then the LineItems collection belong to that
shipment entirely, all is good. Let's see when you have more than one shipment, which is a quite
common scenario.

The collection of LineItems is still there, and the OrderForm still owns it. However, to distribute the
lineitems across the shipments, there is the concept of index-quantity mapping. The distribution in
the first shipment is A:1, B:2, C:1, and in the second shipment is A:2, B:2, C:1.

To store that information, the indices of the lineitems was used, so they will be 0:1, 1:2, 2:1 and 0:2,
1:2, 2:1, respectively. All is still good, at this point.

The problem arises when you want to remove one lineitem, change it quantity or move it around (to
another shipment, for example). That's when the index-quantity max shows it true face: fragile. You
can easily get the wrong quantity, or even worse, get an IndexOutOfRangeException just because a
lineitem was removed but the map was not updated.

It's something in design and you just can't avoid it entirely. But you can still avoid those problems by
using methods provided in PurchaseOrderManager, instead of manipulating the LineItems collection
your own.

                 Trust me, a good amount of power brain has been spent to make sure those methods work
                 correctly and reliably.

    So if you want to remove a lineitem from the cart, this is NOT the correct way:

1 var lineItem = orderForm.LineItems.FirstOrDefault(l => l.Code == code);
2 orderForm.LineItems.Remove(lineItem);
3 lineItem.Delete();

    but instead:

1 var lineItem = orderForm.LineItems.FirstOrDefault(l => l.Code == code);
2 PurchaseOrderManager.RemoveLineItemFromOrder(orderGroup, lineItem.LineItemId);

                              Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                          137

    PurchaseOrderManager.RemoveLineItemFromOrder will do a lot of things: it will remove the lineitem
    from shipments which contain it, then remove the lineitem from the order form, finally, it will re-
    index the lineitem index-quantity mapping, to make sure everything is in the correct and stable
    state.

    RemoveLineItemFromOrder is not the only thing you can use in PurchaseOrderManager, there are
    other useful methods, such as RemoveLineItemFromShipment or UpdateShipmentLineItemQuantity.
    Just let me re-say this: use PurchaseOrderManager to manage your shipment-lineitems relations, do
    not do it yourself.

    Extending order system metaclasses.

    As I mentioned before - there is a big difference between how MetaDataPlus system is used for
    catalog, and for order. For catalog, you add more metaclasses - because the base ones are too bare.
    For order, you will mostly add metafields to existing metaclasses (you can, of course, add more
    metaclasses extending the default ones, but that's not something you usually do - I haven't seen one
    in real life, but I might not have seen enough.).

    Now is where the problem appears. You might create the metafields, and add them to the metaclasses
    in Commerce Manager, but it might not be enough. You would want to nice, automated way to make
    sure every environment has the same metaclasses and metafields. And for order system, we don't
    have the nice, intuiative APIs for creating metaclasses and metafields as we do for catalog system
    (We don't even create metaclasses and metafields, we were defining conten types). Friend - I have
    been trying to fence off - but now the time to get to know some MetaDataPlus APIs.

    To creat a metafield, we can just use MetaField.Create.

                     It's very important to remember that we have two MetaField classes in the system.
                     Make sure to use the one in Mediachase.MetaDataPlus.Configurator namespace, not the
                     BusinessFoundation one.

1 public static MetaField Create(

2   MetaDataContext context,

3   string metaNamespace,

4   string name,

5   string friendlyName,

6   string description,

7   MetaDataType dataType,

8   int length,

9   bool allowNulls,

10  bool multiLanguageValue,

11  bool allowSearch,

12  bool isEncrypted)

    And then attach it to a MetaClass, by

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                    138

1 var metaClass = MetaClass.Load(metaDataContext, metaClassId);
2 metaClass.AddField(field);

    In real world scenarios, you might want to make sure your site has correct metafields always.
    This should be done in an IInitializeModule so your code will be run every time your site starts.
    However, it's not allowed to have duplicated metafield. MetaField.Create will throw an exception
    if a metafield with same name exists, and metaClass.AddField will also throw an exception of the
    metaclass already contains that metafield. To avoid such exception, you can (and should) be more
    defensive - check first before creating and adding things:

1 var metaClass = MetaClass.Load(metaDataContext, metaClassId);

2 if (!metaClass.MetaFields.Any(f => f.Name.Equals("fieldname", StringComparison.Ordin\

3 alIgnoreCase)))

4{

5     var metaField = MetaField.Load(metaDataContext, "fieldname");

6     if (metaField == null)

7     {

8        MetaField.Create(...);

9     }

10    metaClass.AddField(field);

11 }

    And that's it, my friend.

    Setting metafield precision

    Some of the metafields you will be adding to the system will be of type decimal or number. And
    it's important to set their precision correctly. By default, decimal has the precision of (18,0), which
    would mean you will lose some of the data.

    Of course, that should be avoided. The best way to do it, is to set the precision of the metafield. That
    can be done by setting the attributes:

1 if (field.DataType == MetaDataType.Decimal || field.DataType == MetaDataType.Numeric)

2{

3     field.Attributes[MetaFieldAttributeConstants.MdpPrecisionAttributeName] = precis\

4 ion;

5     field.Attributes[MetaFieldAttributeConstants.MdpScaleAttributeName] = scale;

6}

    You might recognize it, it is more or less the same as DecimalSettingsAttribute - actually they do
    the same thing. Because we have no place to put the attribute on a metafield in order system - we
    have to use direct APIs. Yes it's not something we want to do, but as long as it gets the job done.

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                            139

           Now what if you forgot to set the decimal precision, and you only realize that after filling
           data to your new metafield? That'd be an "Oops!" moment. For the catalog content, the
           decimal values are stored in (38,9), and it's only the attributes of the metafield get changed,
           so there is no data loss what so ever. However, for the order system, the precision get burned
           in the column precision. You would have to change that by getting your hands dirty with
           some direct SQL manipulation. Yes - that's generally a bad idea and we should avoid doing
           it, but there are times when we need to do it. But proceed with great caution, and as always,
           back up first.

    Addresses and Shipping methods

    So we added LineItems to the cart, then add them to the shipment. To make the shipment ship-able,
    we still have works to do: to know where and how to ship it!

    Addresses.

    Each OrderGroup has a collection of addresses - which can be accessed via OrderGroup.OrderAddresses.
    Usually, you have one or more shipping addresses and one billing address.

1 public class OrderAddress

2{

3     public string Name { get; set;}

4

5     public int OrderGroupId { get; set;}

6

7     public string FirstName { get; set;}

8

9     public string LastName { get; set;}

10

11    ...

12 }

    Manipulating addresses in an order can be a complicated task. Here's how the Episerver Commerce
    works to resolve that:

      · If the current user is registered and logged in, then at checkout page, he or she can select from
        CustomerContact.PreferredBillingAddress and CustomerContact.PreferredShippingAddress,
        if those settings have values. Otherwise, the customer can choose from address book, or input
        a new address.

      · If the current user is anonymous, then he or she must input at least one address (if shipping
        and billing addresses are the same)

      · If customer choose In Store pickup (Handoff) shipping method, then the address of selected
        warehouse will be copied to an OrderAddress, then add this OrderAddress to OrderGroup.OrderAddresses.

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                                     140

1   public static OrderAddress ConvertWarehouseContactInfoToOrderAddress(IWarehouse \

2 warehouse)

3   {

4      OrderAddress address = new OrderAddress();

5      address.City = warehouse.ContactInformation.City;

6      address.CountryCode = warehouse.ContactInformation.CountryCode;

7      address.CountryName = warehouse.ContactInformation.CountryName;

8      address.DaytimePhoneNumber = warehouse.ContactInformation.DaytimePhoneNumber;

9      address.Email = warehouse.ContactInformation.Email;

10     address.EveningPhoneNumber = warehouse.ContactInformation.EveningPhoneNumber;

11     address.FirstName = warehouse.ContactInformation.FirstName;

12     address.LastName = warehouse.ContactInformation.LastName;

13     address.Line1 = warehouse.ContactInformation.Line1;

14     address.Line2 = warehouse.ContactInformation.Line2;

15     address.Name = OrderHelper.GetSafeAddressName(warehouse.Name);

16     address.PostalCode = warehouse.ContactInformation.PostalCode;

17     address.RegionName = warehouse.ContactInformation.RegionName;

18     address.RegionCode = warehouse.ContactInformation.RegionCode;

19     address.State = warehouse.ContactInformation.State;

20     return address;

21  }

    · The address will then be saved to OrderGroup.OrderAddresses. If the address was an CustomerAddress,
      it will be copied over to an OrderAddress (the simplest way to do so is to use CustomerAddress.CopyCustomerAdd
      customerAddress, OrderAddress orderAddress)),then added to OrderGroup.OrderAddresses.

    · OrderForm.BillingAddressId, Payment.BillingAddressId and each Shipment.ShippingAddressId
      will point to Name of one OrderAddress in OrderGroup.OrderAddresses. At checkout page,
      here's what our code should look like:

1 var shippingAddress = new OrderAddress();
2 //Set the fields of shippingAddress
3 purchaseOrder.OrderAddresses.Add(shippingAddress);
4 shipment.ShippingAddressId = shippingAddress.Name;

                     During the checkout, the customer can create many addresses, however, only the selected
                     shipping addresses and billing address will be saved, all other unused addresses will be
                     deleted (If you recall from previous section, the cart gets deleted, a new purchase order gets
                     created, so un-references objects like the addresses will be deleted).

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.  141

Shipping methods

The only crucial information missing for Shipment, is the shipping method customer selected to ship
the goods. We'll talk about the shipping methods in depth in Part 8 - Extending the system, but now
we'll only limit to how to get the shipping methods and how to set that information to the shipment.

                                         One way to show available shipping methods to customers

1 ShippingMethodDto shippingMethodDto = ShippingManager.GetShippingMethodsByMarket(Cur\
2 rentMarket.MarketId.Value, false);
3 //Construct the model to display shipping methods to end user

    Here we're getting the shipping methods available in the current market. ShippingMethodDto is an-
    other DataSet, but we should only care about its ShippingMethod table now. Each ShippingMethodRow
    has a ShippingMethodId, which we can use to bind to the radio buttons like the screenshot above.
    Now when the customer selected the shipping method, we need to set it to the shipment:

1 ShippingMethodDto shippingMethodDto = ShippingManager.GetShippingMethodsByMarket(Cur\
2 rentMarket.MarketId.Value, false);
3 var selectedShippingMethod = shippingMethodDto.ShippingMethod.FirstOrDefault(m => m.\
4 ShippingMethodId == selectedShippingMethodId);
5 shipment.ShippingMethodId = selectedShippingMethod.ShippingMethodId;
6 shipment.ShippingMethodName = selectedShippingMethod.Name;

    Then you can just save the cart/order as usual.

                     We are just talking about setting the shipping method to the shipment. How to actually
                     implement a shipping provider will be discussed later in the book.

   Payments

    Now it's time for customer to ... pay. (If your site does not require that, please send me the link, I'd
    like to buy something). Payments are one of the centric parts of order process, and should not be
    taken lightly.

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 6: The Order system.                                        142

   We will discuss about configuring payments later, but Episerver Commerce, as a framework, is very
   flexible in this regard. Authorize.net is the builtin payment method comes with default installation.
   Episerver provides PayPal, DIBS, DataCash as free open source examples. Klarna provides their
   Episerver Commerce addons. It's also possible to develop your own provider to support the payment
   gateway you want your customers to use.

   First, we need to show the available payment methods to the customer:

1 var payments = PaymentManager.GetPaymentMethodsByMarket(_currentMarket.MarketId.Val\

2 ue, SiteContext.Current.LanguageName, false).PaymentMethod.Select()

3 foreach (var paymentRow in payments)

4{

5   var paymentMethod = new PaymentMethod(paymentRow);

6   //Create the Model for the payment

7}

   Here we are getting the active payment methods in the current market and current site language
   (the false parameter indicates that we don't want inactive payment methods). Then for each
   PaymentMethodRow found, we create a PaymentMethod. The most important thing in a PaymentMethod
   is the PaymentMethodId, which must be used for identifying the payment method.

     The simplified payment methods selection in Quicksilver. Shhh, please don't tell anyone my credit card number

   then add the selected one to the OrderForm.

                                                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 6: The Order system.                                          143

1 var payment = selectedPayment.PreProcess(orderForm);

2 if (payment != null)

3{

4   payment.PaymentMethodId = paymentMethodRow.PaymentMethodId;

5   payment.PaymentMethodName = paymentMethodRow.Name;

6   payment.BillingAddressId = orderForm.BillingAddressId;

7   payment.Amount = orderForm.Total;

8   orderForm.Payments.Add(payment);

9}

                     It's required to call CartValidate workflow before adding payment(s) to the OrderForm -
                     it does all the hard jobs - apply all applicable promotions, calculate the taxes and shipping
                     costs. The amount of money you charge your customer must perfectly match the total of
                     the order.

    selectedPayment is a component (User control, Partial view) which implements IPaymentOption
    interface. In PreProcess, you can do some checks to verify that the information customer provided
    is valid (for example, the credit card is not expired), and return a Payment object. The final thing
    is to set some properties to that Payment object and add it to OrderForm.Payments. Those payments
    will be finally processed in later stage (hint: when you run CartCheckout workflow).
    Like shipping methods, we'll discuss morre about payment methods in Part 8. See you there!

   AcceptChanges()

    It's important to understand how saving in order system works. As we mentioned in above section,
    to save an OrderGroup, including Cart, PurchaseOrder or PaymentPlan, simply call AcceptChanges()
    on the object. What does it save? Everything.
    So this is not necessary (in fact, it does not work, the order of saving here is not correct, the "parent"
    objects must be saved first):

1 lineItem.AcceptChanges();
2 shipment.AcceptChanges();
3 orderForm.AcceptChanges();
4 cart.AcceptChanges();

    this is already enough:

1 cart.AcceptChanges();

                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.                                          144

Calling AcceptChanges() on an OrderGroup will also save all the children properties, including col-
lections. It's also pretty smart - only changed objects (object with status of MetaObjectState.Added,
MetaObjectState.Deleted, MetaObjectState.Modified) are saved. Unchanged objects, well, they
remains unchanged.

It's also important to remember that calling AcceptChanges() on an OrderGroup is an expensive
operation. Many calls to database will be done: for each and every object inside an OrderGroup,
there is at least one separate save to database. That's why you should postpone saving Carts as
late as possible, and save as few times as possible. If your site is doing multiple cart saves in one
HttpRequest - it's time to review the code to remove unwanted saves. You probably need only the
last one.

Workflows

When a customer places an order, there are several steps that require extra processing - those can
be done by calling certain workflows. Workflows, or more precisely, ActivityFlows, have been an
essential part of Episerver Commerce since the first version, and play an important part in checkout
process.

                 However, with the raise of new promotion system, it's not a mandatory thing to run
                 anymore. For websites which sell downloadable contents and use the new promotion system,
                 there is almost no reason to use workflows. We will learn more about workflows in Part 8,
                 but here we are talking about the basic of workflows - what are they, and how can they be
                 used.

When a customer adds an item to cart - you should call CartValidate. This workflow will check
if the items in cart can be fulfilled (if this is a normal purchase order, if this is pre-order, or back-
order, then the inventories must have special settings to allow that) and if the cart is entitled to
any promotions. If an item does not satisfy the check, it will be removed from the cart. If there is
a promotion which can be applied, discounts will be made to the price and/or gift items will be
added to cart. CartValidate also records those discount usages - so the system can keep tracks of
promotion redemption.s

There are several ways to call the workflow. The most common way in this stage is probably:

1 cartHelper.RunWorkflow("CartValidate");

    the other way is to use OrderGroupWorkflowManager:

1 OrderGroupWorkflowManager.RunWorkflow(cartHelper.Cart, OrderGroupWorkflowManager.Car\
2 tValidateWorkflowName);

                              Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                              145

When they finish adding items to cart, and proceed to checkout, it's time to call another workflow
CartPrepare. It's almost as same as CartValidate, except it will calculate taxes. CartPrepare also
calculate discounts, but it does not record the promotion usage again.

Finally, when they added shipping/billing addresses and payment information, the final step is to
run CartCheckout workflow. It will process the payments, make requests to inventory system, and
finally record the promotion usage as "Used".

There are most important workflows which are used on the frontend site. There are several others,
but you rarely use them directly by code - they are mostly used by functionalities in Commerce
Manager.

There is a small exception - if you're using your own system to process order, you might
need to use CompleteShipmentWorkflow workflow.

     Here's the full list of all builtin workflows in Episerver Commerce, defined by names:

 1 public const string CartValidateWorkflowName = "CartValidate";
 2 public const string CartPrepareWorkflowName = "CartPrepare";
 3 public const string CartCheckOutWorkflowName = "CartCheckout";
 4
 5 public const string OrderRecalculateWorkflowName = "RecalculatePurchaseOrderWorkflow\
 6 ";
 7 public const string OrderSaveChangesWorkflowName = "SaveChangesWorkflow";
 8
 9 public const string OrderCompleteShipmentWorkflowName = "CompleteShipmentWorkflow";
10 public const string OrderSplitShipmentsWorkflowName = "SplitShipmentsWorkflow";
11 public const string OrderCalculateTotalsWorkflowName = "RecalculateTotalsWorkflow";
12 public const string ReturnFormCompleteWorkflowName = "ReturnFormCompleteWorkflow";
13 public const string ReturnFormRecalculateWorkflowName = "ReturnFormRecalculateWorkfl\
14 ow";
15
16 public const string CheckAndReserveInstorePickupWorkflowName = "CheckAndReserveInsto\
17 rePickupWorkflow";

     It's important to remember that a Cart, or a PurchaseOrder, needs to be saved prior before calling
     workflows. Otherwise, some activities will try to load the information from the database and the
     results will not be correct. In other hands, it does make senses to save as little as possible. Each call
     to .AcceptChanges on an OrderGroup will save everything on that instance - OrderForm, Shipment,
     LineItem, etc. to database, then duplicated calls will result on performance hit. As a best practice,
     try to save only once per request - for example, when a user add or remove one item from the cart,
     it should only be saved only once, nothing more.

                              Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                          146

   Handling order statuses

   If you are using Commerce Manager to handle orders, it does not really make sense to understand
   order statuses, programmatically. However, if you want to to handle orders by external systems, it's
   a must.

   Here's the list of available statuses for orders. Remember that enum is not extensible in C#, so you
   have to stick with those values

1 [Flags]

2 public enum OrderStatus

3{

4          OnHold = 1,

5          PartiallyShipped = 2,

6          InProgress = 4,

7          Completed = 8,

8          Cancelled = 16,

9          AwaitingExchange = 32

10 }

           Technically, you can. Those values are stored as a string column in database, so you can set
           any values you want. However, the existing parts of system won't be able to handle those
           custom values.

                                                                                                                         .
   Let's examine the statuses, one by one.

                                                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                                             147

   In this image above, we can see that the order is in InProgress state. This is the default status when
   a purchase order is made. You can see that there are button to put this order to be OnHold and
   Cancelled.

   To put an order to OnHold state, we can call:

1 OrderStatusManager.HoldOrder(purchaseOrder);

    When an order is in OnHold state - it's read only, for example when you are waiting for customer's
    confirmation - no changes can be made to the order until you release it from hold.

1 if (OrderStatusManager.GetPurchaseOrderStatus(purchaseOrder) == OrderStatus.OnHold)

2{

3   OrderStatusManager.ReleaseHoldOnOrder(purchaseOrder);

4}

   ReleaseHoldOnOrder will throw an exception if purchaseOrder is not already OnHold - so it's best
   for check for the status before actually releasing it from holding.

            Althought it's not required, it's best practice to add a note to the order whenever you change
            the order status, to save history of why did the order get changed. Here's how we should do
            it

1 OrderStatusManager.HoldOrder(purchaseOrder);

2 OrderNotesManager.AddNoteToPurchaseOrder(purchaseOrder,

3 "Order put on hold to wait for customer's confirmation", OrderNoteTypes.System,                           \

4                                               PrincipalInfo.CurrentPrincipal.GetConta\

5 ctId());

   While holding a purchase order only makes it read-only - canceling an order does a lot more: it does
   not only set the status of the order to Cancelled, it also cancels all the shipments in the orders -
   so any inventories reserved for the order, if the shipments haven't been shipped, will be reverted
   back, payments made by the orders will be processed, this time with TransactionType.Credit flag
   (to refund the money, if possible, to the buyer).

   However, as the seller, you won't want the order to be put on hold or be cancelled - you want the
   order to be completed - the goods to be shipped and the customers to be happy with their purchase.
   In such case, you need to dig into OrderShipmentStatus:

                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 6: The Order system.                                             148

1 [Flags]

2 public enum OrderShipmentStatus

3{

4          AwaitingInventory = 1,

5          Cancelled = 2,

6          InventoryAssigned = 4,

7          OnHold = 8,

8          Packing = 16,

9          Released = 32,

10         Shipped = 64

11 }

    You can guess when some statuses are used - OnHold is when the entire order is put on hold, and
    Cancelled is when the order is cancelled. However, in case the shipment has multiple shipments,
    each shipment can be cancelled separately.

                                  Each shipment can be cancelled separately

    To cancel a shipment:

1 OrderStatusManager.CancelOrderShipment(shipment);

    In case the order has only one shipment, or if all other shipments in the order have been cancelled,
    it does make sense to cancel the order itself:

                                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 6: The Order system.                                                  149

1 OrderStatusManager.CancelOrderShipment(shipment);

2 if (purchaseOrder.OrderForms[0].Shipments.All(c=>c.Status.Equals(OrderShipmentStatus\

3 .Cancelled.ToString()))

4{

5   OrderStatusManager.CancelOrder(purchaseOrder);

6}

   By default, when an order is placed, the shipment is in AwaitingInventory state. We must now
   release it from inventory to make it ready to delivery:

1 if ((!ShippingManager.IsHandoffShippingMethod(shipment.ShippingMethodName) ||

2   OrderStatusManager.IsShipmentReleaseable(shipment)) &&

3   OrderStatusManager.IsOrderPaid(purchaseOrder))

4   {

5      OrderStatusManager.ReleaseOrderShipment(shipment);

6   }

   Here we are doing some additional checks - the shipment should not be "in store pickup" delivery,
   it should be "releasable", and the order has been paid. Lucky for us, OrderStatusManager provides
   all required methods to do those checks. Once released, the shipment will be in Released state (of
   course), and ready to be picked up for delivery.

                                           Released shipments, ready for picked up for delivery

   Here we can add shipments to picklists. By the code, it's equivalent to set the status of the shipment
   to Packing:

                                                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 6: The Order system.  150

1 OrderStatusManager.PickForPackingOrderShipment(shipment);

    If you are new to Commerce, picklists are the list of goods ready to be delivered - so the delivery guys
    can pick up the packages from warehouses and deliver it to awaiting customers. After a shipment is
    picked up, it can be completed (finally!)

                                                         Complete shipments, the final step

1 OrderStatusManager.CompleteOrderShipment(shipment);

                     In fact, you are not required to set a shipment to Packing state to make it "complete-able".
                     After a shipment is released, you can complete it directly.

    Completing a shipment is an important step. It does not only set the status of the shipment to
    Shipped, but also it finalizes the payments. For some payment gateways, such as PayPal, when an
    order is placed, the payment is holding - it is subtracted from buyer's account, but not yet transferred
    to your site's account. Only when you the customer acknowledges that he or she received the goods
    - the payment is "released" aka, completes.
    If the order has only one shipment, or all other shipments have been shipped, completing that
    shipment will effectively completes the whole order.
    We've been examining the normal case when the goods need to be delivered. However, it's not
    always the case. When the customer chooses to get the goods directly at the warehouse (in store

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 6: The Order system.                                               151

   pickup), it does not really make senses to "release shipment". When the customer picks the shipment
   up, that action completes the shipment directly.

                                 Pick up shipment and complete it directly

1 if(ShippingManager.IsHandoffShippingMethod(shipment.ShippingMethodName) &&

2  (OrderStatusManager.IsShipmentReleaseable(shipment)

3  || OrderStatusManager.IsShipmentPackable(shipment)

4  || OrderStatusManager.IsShipmentCompletable(shipment))

5 && OrderStatusManager.IsOrderPaid(purchaseOrder))

6  {

7     OrderStatusManager.CompleteOrderShipment(shipment);

8  }

                    In this section we talked about purchase order - but all methods mentioned here also have
                    overloads to work with PaymentPlan. Remember? They are all OrderGroup!

   Of course, if you are selling digital contents, when the purchase order is placed, you are free to make
   it completed directly.

                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 7: New Order system - the
new promise

Why a new system

You might ask - why a new system. The order system is not so bad.
But it is not really good, neither. And here are somethings it is not great at:

   · Performance: The design of PurchaseOrder and ShoppingCart as two metaclasses of OrderGroup,
      so they're entitled to share the same base table (OrderGroup) in database: whenever you place
      an order, it's actually a cart being deleted and a purchase order being created. Two operations
      in same table, you know it's bad for performance, especially there is an index there. It's even
      worse with the bad practice of saving the cart it's changed. Whenever a customer add or remove
      an item from the cart, it's supposed to be saved. Imagine you have hundreds or even thousands
      of customers browsing your website, it'll kill the performance of placing orders.

   · The API:s lack of abstraction. You can't just save the orders to somewhere else. (You can, of
      course, take the order and save it in another place, but it does mean that you are saving to two
      places, and end up loading from two places). And lacking of abstraction also mean it's hard to
      unit test. It's almost impossible to mock those classes. This is a very frequent complain about
      CartHelper - althought it's not in the core API:s. It's like a wrapper for Cart which is, again,
      hard (if not possible) to mock.

   · The quirks of Lineitem-Shipment relation

The new order system is promised to solve those problems. Let's see how those promises are fulfilled.

Everything is an interface.

Well, almost. In previous chapter, you did know about OrderGroup, OrderForm, Shipment, and
LineItem. Those are concrete MetaClass(es) - therefore they are tightly coupled with the database
and it's almost impossible if you want to plug your own implementations.
To allow the required abstraction, new interfaces were introduced: IOrderGroup, IOrderForm,
IShipment, ILineItem, IOrderAddress, IPayment, etc. Let's take a look at IOrderGroup:
    Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                      153

1 public interface IOrderGroup : IExtendedProperties

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets an instance of the class <see cref="OrderReference"/>, which contains t\

5 he unique identity for the order group, and the type of order group it represents (c\

6 art, purchase order, or payment plan)

7     /// </summary>

8     OrderReference OrderLink { get; }

9

10    /// <summary>

11    /// Gets the collection of order forms in the order group.

12    /// </summary>

13    IEnumerable<IOrderForm> Forms { get; }

14

15    /// <summary>

16    /// Gets the order status.

17    /// </summary>

18    OrderStatus OrderStatus { get; set; }

19

20    /// <summary>

21    /// Gets the market.

22    /// </summary>

23    IMarket Market { get; }

24

25    /// <summary>

26    /// Gets the currency used in the order.

27    /// </summary>

28    Currency Currency { get; }

29

30    ...

31 }

    Note IExtendedProperties - it's the core of all other abstractions, as it represents the concept of
    MetaClass for the abstraction. It's a rather simple interface:

                               Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                    154

1 public interface IExtendedProperties

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// Gets the property bag for dealing with custom meta fields.

5   /// </summary>

6   Hashtable Properties { get; }

7}

   That's it. You can store the collection of MetaFields there.

   Note the IEnumerable<IOrderForms> Forms property. You might have guessed - it's an abstraction
   over OrderGroup.OrderForms.

   Like IOrderGroup, IOrderForm, IShipment and other interfaces are implemented in an explicit way.
   So if you have an OrderGroup, which implements IOrderGroup, you can not access its Forms by
   simply calling

1 orderGroup.Forms

    but instead:

1 ((IOrderGroup)orderGroup).Forms

    You might see it as inconvenient, wouldn't it easier to implement it as the property of the
    implementation? If you are mixing old API:s and new interfaces, yes. However, the explicit
    interface implementation will enforce the way you work with API:s. You should only work with
    interface without having to care about implementations. By that, when you want to change your
    implementation, such as adding new storage option, you don't have to change the code you wrote.
    It also enforce the decoupling with the implementation, and makes it easier for you to mock and
    write unit tests for your code.

   IOrderRepository - Goodbye CartHelper

   You might have noticed the OrderReference property. It's not related ContentReference, but it has
   something in common. As its name might suggest, it's a identifier to an OrderGroup. It has four
   properties, but only OrderGroupId (which is the id) and OrderType (which is the type) really matter.
   OrderReference is the way to work with orders in a repository pattern - using IOrderRepository.
   Let's see how OrderReference looks like:

                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                                                             155

1 public class OrderReference

2{

3     public OrderReference(int orderGroupId, string name, Guid customerId, Type order\

4 GroupType);

5

6     public int OrderGroupId { get; private set; }

7

8     public string Name { get; private set; }

9

10    public Guid CustomerId { get; private set; }

11

12    public Type OrderType { get; private set; }

13 }

    This class is, in fact, redundant. OrderGroupId and OrderType are enough to identify an OrderGroup.
    Name, CustomerId can be get from the IOrderGroup.

               With the default implementation, OrderGroupId is supposed be unique across the system, as
               all Cart, PaymentPlan and PurchaseOrder share the same OrderGroup table. However, this
               might change in the custom implementation where you save your carts and your purchase
               orders in different storages. This is one the main points of the new order system - allow you
               to persist the data wherever you want.

    In a way, IOrderRepository can be seen to be inspired by IContentRepository. It's one unified API:s
    to deal with IOrderGroup, regardless of their true types (Cart, PurchaseOrder or PaymentPlan). It's
    the providers, which know how to work with a specific type, handle the leg work. (Remember
    CatalogContentProvider?) IOrderRepository also has some extension methods to make it more
    convenient to work with OrderGroup.

    You still remember CartHelper? You can forget it now, here's the new way to create a cart:

1 var cart = _orderRepository.LoadCart<ICart>(CustomerContact.Current.CurrentContactId\
2 , name);

    This will load the cart in the current market (there is another extension method to load with a
    specific market), or return null if there is no cart existing.

                     It's a best practice to delay the creation of Carts as late as possible. In the old system,
                     whenever you call to new CartHelper(), a Cart will always be called if it has not yet existed.
                     This has a major drawback - every customer visiting your site will have at least one Cart,
                     and you will, in the end, have a lot of empty carts in the system, resulting in a performance
                     hit. In the new system, it is more flexible as you can try to load a Cart and if it is null,
                     simply return an empty mini cart. Only when your customer actually adds something to the
                     cart, a real Cart will be created in database.

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                    156

    When we actually need to have the cart in the database (customer is adding items to cart), it's time
    to call another overload, LoadOrCreateCart. It behaves like new CartHelper() - create a new one if
    there is none.

1 var cart = _orderRepository.LoadOrCreateCart<ICart>(CustomerContact.Current.CurrentC\
2 ontactId, name);

    And you might have guessed how to save a cart:

    IOrderGroupFactory - everything in memory

    New order system comes with a Factory - IOrderGroupFactory allows you to create in-memory
    instances of order classes.

1 public interface IOrderGroupFactory

2{

3     IOrderGroupBuilder BuilderFor(IOrderGroup orderGroup);

4

5     ICart CreateCart(Guid customerId, string name);

6

7     IPurchaseOrder CreatePurchaseOrder(Guid customerId);

8

9     IPaymentPlan CreatePaymentPlan(Guid customerId, string name);

10

11    IOrderForm CreateOrderForm();

12

13    IShipment CreateShipment();

14

15    ILineItem CreateLineItem(string code);

16

17    IOrderAddress CreateOrderAddress();

18

19    IOrderNote CreateOrderNote();

20

21    IPayment CreatePayment();

22

23    ICreditCardPayment CreateCardPayment();

24

25    ITaxValue CreateTaxValue();

26 }

      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                   157

    IOrderGroupFactory represents an important philosophy in new order system: everything should
    be in-memory until you need to persist them. This allows the abstraction to work better.

    One method in the interface seems like out of line with other method : BuilderFor. This was intro-
    duced since 10.2 because a new cart system was introduced. The implementation of IOrderGroupFactory
    will know which IOrderGroupBuilder it should use, and the implementation of that IOrderGroupBuilder
    will be responsible to actually to create the components. IOrderGroupFactory only delegate to the
    builder to do such work.

    In the old system - the instances are tightly coupled with database. When you create a Cart - you
    create it in database and every later operation requires a save to database. With new system, you
    create everything in memory, manipulate everything in memory and only saves when you absolutely
    need. Database access, is, indeed, much slower than memory access. Cutting database saves is the
    reason new order system is expected to be much faster.

                     Previously there was IOrderFactory, however it was obsoleted.

    Now we need to add some items to cart. If the lineitem is already in the cart, if not, create a new
    one then add. In the end, increase the quantity of the lineitem by 1.

1 var allLineItems = cart.Forms.SelectMany(f => f.Shipments).SelectMany(s => s.LineIte\

2 ms);

3 var lineItem = allLineItems.FirstOrDefault(x => x.Code == code);

4

5 if (lineItem == null)

6{

7   lineItem = _orderFactory.CreateLineItem(code);

8   cart.AddLineItem(lineItem, _orderFactory);

9}

10

11 lineItem.Quantity++;

    Wait a minute. Shouldn't we can get all the LineItems by getting them from OrderForm directly?
    No we can't (anymore). With the new abstractions, IOrderForm contains IShipments, and each
    IShipment contains LineItems. There is an existing overload to get all lineitems in the cart -
    cart.GetAllLineItems() which does exactly the same thing.

    Next, we create a lineitem by IOrderGroupFactory. CreateLineItem(code) and that's it. No
    more Entry or heavy database operations. Then we simply add the newly created ILineItem
    to the cart. Wait (another) minute here. The ICart does not own the ILineItems, at least, not
    directly. We need to add the ILineItem to an IShipment, which itself belongs to an IOrderForm.
    IOrderGroup.AddLineItem is an extension method which does that for you, but if you want to know
    what is going on under the hood, then here it is:

                         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                                          158

1 var form = orderGroup.Forms.FirstOrDefault();

2 if (form == null)

3{

4     form = orderFactory.CreateOrderForm();

5     form.Name = orderGroup.Name;

6     orderGroup.Forms.Add(form);

7}

8

9 var shipment = form.Shipments.FirstOrDefault();

10 if (shipment == null)

11 {

12    shipment = orderFactory.CreateShipment();

13    form.Shipments.Add(shipment);

14 }

15

16 shipment.LineItems.Add(lineItem);

    Once the item was added to cart, it's time to save.

1 var orderLink = _orderRepository.Save(cart);

    IOrderRepository.Save(IOrderGroup) is equivalent with Cart.AcceptChanges(), or PaymentPlan.AcceptChanges,
    or PurchaseOrder.AcceptChanges, depends on the actual type of IOrderGroup. The same rule still
    applies - we should only save when it's absolutely necessary. Save your "Save" for last!

      Actually,           IOrderRepository.Save          calls  to  Cart.AcceptChanges,

      PaymentPlan.AcceptChanges, or PurchaseOrder.AcceptChanges if the underlying type is

      concrete class.

  IOrderAddress

    IOrderAddress is the interface extracted from OrderAddress. If you recall from previous section,
    to set the address of a Payment or a Shipment, you first need to add the OrderAddress to
    OrderGroup.OrderAddresses collection, then set the ShippingAddressId or BillingAddressId to
    the name of one of the addresses. A little too many steps, right? Here's the way in new API:s

1 shipment.ShippingAddress = shippingAddress;
2 payment.BillingAddress = billingAddress;

    where shipment is an IShipment and payment is an IPayment. shippingAddress and billingAddress
    are both IOrderAddress, which can be created by IOrderFactory by:

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                   159

1 var shippingAddress = _orderFactory.CreateOrderAddress();
2 //Set values of the fields.

                    However, there is a limitation here: in default implementation, their underlying class
                    must be OrderAddress, otherwise an Exception will be thrown. shippingAddress and
                    billingAddress will be added to OrderGroup.OrderAddress it they are not already existed.

   Simple, huh? I told you the new API:s are to be loved.

   Workflows or non-workflows, that's the question.

   In old versions of Commerce, workflows can be an convenient and straightforward way to handle
   carts and orders. However, not every one likes workflows. Some find it to be non intuitive and
   confusing. Others want to be explicit - calling workflows seems to hide underlying operations. And
   customizing workflows is not everyone's favorite.
   Now with the new abstractions, it's possible to get rid of workflows. Instead of calling a workflow
   to do a bunch of stuffs, now you can call exactly what you want to. If comes with a caveat - you
   must be using new promotion system to be able to follow this way. The old promotion system is
   tightly coupled with workflows.
   IOrderGroup comes with a plenty of extension methods to allow you to do stuffs which previously
   are responsibilities of workflows. Here we are talking about the most simple overloads - there are
   other overloads which take extra parameters, usually the services which needed to do the work
   under the hood. If you want to unit test your code - make sure to use those with dependencies as
   you can mock them in your tests.

      · To apply discounts on the cart:

1  cart.ApplyDiscounts();

   · To validate the cart to see if any items are no longer valid (out of stock, not available in current
     market, prices have changed etc.):

                           Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                                        160

1 var validationIssues = new Dictionary<ILineItem, List<ValidationIssue>>();
2 cart.ValidateOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => validationIssues.AddValidationIssues\
3 (item, issue));
4 cart.UpdatePlacedPriceOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => validationIssues.AddValidat\
5 ionIssues(item, issue));
6 cart.UpdateInventoryOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => validationIssues.AddValidatio\
7 nIssues(item, issue));

· To send requests to inventory system to allocate inventory for items in the cart:

1 var validationIssues = new Dictionary<ILineItem, List<ValidationIssue>>();
2 cart.AdjustInventoryOrRemoveLineItems((item, issue) => validationIssues.AddValidatio\
3 nIssues(item, issue), _inventoryProcessor);

        · To process payments:

1 cart.ProcessPayments();

    Pretty simple, right? If you hate workflows enough - Episerver has something else for you. And of
    course, it looks better.

                     You might ask - why NOT workflows? Well, there are several reasons for not using
                     workflows. Firstly it's one less dependency. Even workflows are built for customization,
                     it's not totally easy to do so. Calling workflows can also be obscure - you might have hard
                     time to understand which CartValidate is doing, while with the code like above, it's easily
                     understandable of which are being done. Workflows or not workflows is a question, and it's
                     up to you to decide. But non workflows should be the way forward.

The new cart system.

One of the main points of the new order system is to allow the extensibility - so you can plug
your implementation into the order system. You don't want to save/load the orders to Commerce
database, but to your ERP system? No problem. You want to have Redis as the backend for the carts?
Easy peasy. But we're not there yet. Order system is big, and replacing it takes a lot of time, if you
remember that every change must be carefully considered because the data is extremely valuable.
So it'll be done in pieces, and the first piece is the cart system.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                                                  161

   Since 10.2, a new cart system has been released. What does it do? And what is the benefit? It's much
   faster! In some tests, it is 10x times faster than the old system. 10x. How?

   To explain why the new system is faster - we need to understand why the old system is slow. As we
   learned in the previous section, the carts and the orders share same tables - OrderGroup, OrderForm,
   Shipment, LineItem, etc. There are two cases which the old cart system perform badly: When you
   have a B2B site where each order can be 100, or even more items. So when you save to a cart, it's
   actually (at least) 100 round trips to data base. And when a customer places an order, aka the cart,
   and everything belongs to it is deleted.

   In later version of Commerce, the saving for meta objects was improved. If a meta object such
   as a lineitem was not changed, it'll not be saved when you save the cart. This dramatically
   improves the performance of saving. However, if you save a new cart with 100+ lineitem,
   performance will still suffer.

   The new cart system solves these two issues by the serialization approach. Except some mandatory
   information such as the name of the cart, customer id or the market id, all other information is
   serialized and stored in a big column. Now no matter how many items you have in your cart, it's
   only one round trip to database. And when a customer places an order, it'll still delete the cart, but
   again it's only one round trip, and it'll not be the same tables with the order.

   You'll get the biggest benefits if you are a B2B site which big cart, or if your site has high concurrent
   when many customers continuously adding/removing items from carts and placing orders (like the
   BlackFriday sale ;) ). If you are in one of the those situations, it makes senses to evaluate the upgrade
   to new cart system.

   The new cart system is not enabled by default when you upgrade your site. You will need to turn it
   on manually by this setting in ecf.app.config. (In the chapter about promotion system, we will have
   the same kind of setting for the promotion system)

1 <Application defaultApplicationName="ECApplication">

2  <Features>

3  <add feature="SerializedCarts" state="Enabled" type="Mediachase.Commerce.Cor\

4 e.Features.SerializedCarts, Mediachase.Commerce" />

5  <Features>

6 </Application>

   This will enable a scheduled job allowing you to migrate the old carts into new ones. Depending on
   your number of carts, it might takes minutes to hours for the migration.

   But that's not everything. You'll need to update your code to make sure it works with the new
   system. How? Simply replace your usages of IOrderFactory with IOrderGroupFactory.

   If you are using Commerce 12 or later, IOrderFactory was removed.

                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 7: New Order system - the new promise                     162

   If you want to have your own implementation of how to create order or shipment. What you can do
   is to implement your own IOrderGroupBuilder. It's more or less IOrderGroupFactory, except with
   two new methods:

1  /// <summary>

2  /// The sort order of the builder.

3  /// </summary>

4  int SortOrder { get; }

5

6  /// <summary>

7  /// The type(s) of order group that this builder will work with.

8  /// </summary>

9  Type[] ForType { get; }

   By specifying ForType property, you'll register your class to be the handler of the implementations
   of ICart, IPurchaseOrder or IPaymentPlan. And in case there is already class registered for those
   implementations, a higher SortOrder will take effect.

   To allow the new cart system to work, several new interfaces were introduced, most impor-
   tant IShippingPlugin and IPaymentPlugin. They are supposed to replace IShipmentGateway and
   IPaymentGateway. You'll have to implement those interfaces for your shipping provider and payment
   provider, respectively. Otherwise a validation during site startup will throw exception if the new cart
   system is enabled but those interfaces were not implemented.

   The new cart system is available in Commerce 10.2, but support for it in Commerce Manager is only
   in Commerce 10.3. As always, you should upgrade to the latest version.

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 8: Taxes

Taxes are essential for every Commerce websites - and you want to get it right. This chapter will be
devoted to explain the concepts - how taxes work and how to calculate taxes (correctly).
There are two approach with taxes - some websites precalculated the taxes and set the prices directly.
Other sites only calculate taxes at check out. I can't say which approach is better - but the later
seems to be more common, and it's a good way to encourage customers to put more items to the
cart. Technically, it's also more flexible and easier to implement. So let's examine it first.

Taxes configuration

                                               Tax configuration in Commerce Manager

Configuring taxes manually can be pretty tiring work - especially when you do it for the first time.
It would be easier to just import a sample tax, as provided here²
Here, after importing above sample taxes, we come with two taxes - Sales tax for California and
Shipping tax for Massachusetts.
The structure of taxes can be confusing at first - so if you are confused, don't worry, it's meant to
be complicated.
For each tax, you can configure a tax value for each Tax Category and each Jurisdiction Group. So if
you want to create two taxes for California - one for general sales and one for luxury items, you will

   ²http://docs.mediachase.com/download/attachments/4685836/TaxCSVSample1.csv
Chapter 8: Taxes  164

have to create two Jurisdiction Group. Each of these Jurisdiction Group will have one Jurisdiction
which all point to California state.

                                              Tax jurisdictions specify when a tax applies

Taxes can be set up to city or even zip code. However, the most common scheme is to states (US)
or countries (Europe). When a shipment is shipped to an address matching a tax jurisdiction, the
tax corresponding with that will be applied. It's important to note that the jurisdiction must match
every value in the shipping address. In example above, we have CountryCode as "USA" and State
Province Code as "California" - if the shipping address has CountryCode as "US" or State Province
Code as "CA", those are not matched and of course the tax will not be applied.

                                                 Tax value for each Jurisdiction Group

Now with the two sample taxes in place - we need to configure the variations for the Tax categories.
This can be set in Pricing tab in Commerce Manager or Catalog UI.
Can taxes be configured programmatically? Yes, of course. There are actually two ways to do that:
TaxManager and JurisdictionManager which can work in a Dto approach. Or TaxImportExport
