where you can import CSV files into the system.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 8: Taxes                                          165

   Taxes calculation

     There are two ways to calculate taxes: using workflows (if you are using the old order API:s) and
     using ITaxCalculator (If you are using the abstractions. However, we usually don't use this interface
     directly, but IOrderGroupTotalsCalculator)
     To calculate taxes using workflow, CartPrepare workflow is used. By default, CartPrepare should
     be called customers reach checkout page. If you don't want to use workflows, you can simply call
     IOrderGroupTotalsCalculator.GetTotals:

 1 var totals = _orderGroupTotalsCalculator.GetTotals(cart);
 2 SubTotal = totals.SubTotal,
 3 CartTotal = totals.Total,
 4 ShippingTotal = totals.ShippingTotal,
 5 ShippingSubtotal = cart.GetShippingSubTotal(_shippingCalculator),
 6 OrderDiscountTotal = cart.GetOrderDiscountTotal(cart.Currency, _orderGroupCalculator\
 7 ),
 8 ShippingDiscountTotal = cart.GetShippingDiscountTotal(_shippingCalculator),
 9 ShippingTaxTotal = totals.ShippingTotal + totals.TaxTotal,
10 TaxTotal = totals.TaxTotal,

     Those requirements are needed for the taxes to be calculated and shown correctly to customers:

         · Shipping address for each Shipment has been set, with all required fields match the information
           in Jurisdiction.

         · LineItems have been added to Shipments correctly.

Tax-included prices

The second approach might not be as popular, but it can work well in some scenarios. The most
common scenario is that when you have a market with flat-rate of Taxes. For example - EU. In such
cases, you can include the taxes in the prices for that market.

This is not possible, at least, not easy to do, in Commerce 11 and prior. You would have to heavily
modify the calculator to not calculate taxes. If you only sell in EU markets for example, you can
make the calculator aware that the prices are already include taxes - so it returns tax values, but
does not add those values to the total (you still need to show how much was taxes). But if you have
multiple markets, some have prices include tax and some do not, then it will be fairly complicated
to know when you should add taxes as normal, and when you should not.

In Commerce 12 that was addressed, there is a new property in IMarket named PricesIncludeTax,
which indicates if the prices in that market already include taxes or not.

                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 8: Taxes                                          166

                                                          New property in Market

The default calculators are already aware about this setting and will respect it in calculating taxes.
You will just need to keep your site updated, change the setting, and off you go!

                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Part 4: Events - Listen and react

One of the key features of Episerver Commerce is the ability to integrate into external systems.
To do that, parts of Commerce need to communicate, to let other parts know that they are
doing something/something have been done. The most common way of doing so is to use events.
Thankfully, Episerver Framework already provides a strong mechanism for firing and catching
events, so Commerce only has to care about when to raise events and what to raise from it.
    Chapter 9: Content events.

    As we all know from Part 1, CatalogContentProvider is the way Commerce uses to plug the catalog
    system into the content system, and then it receives (almost) full benefits from the content system
    - including the events fired when contents are changed. It work pretty well in IContentRepository
    level, as you can simply inject IContentEvents into your class and use it there. Let's assume you want
    to check everytime a content is being moved via Catalog UI, you want to make sure the destination
    is allowed for current user², if not, you will cancel the operation, you can have something like this:

1 using EPiServer;
2 using EPiServer.Commerce.Catalog.ContentTypes;
3 using EPiServer.Core;
4 using System;

5

6 public class CatalogContentEventProcess : IDisposable

7{

8     private readonly IContentEvents _contentEvents;

9     public CatalogContentEventProcess(IContentEvents contentEvents)

10    {

11       _contentEvents = contentEvents;

12       _contentEvents.MovingContent += Content_Moving;

13    }

14

15    private void Content_Moving(object sender, ContentEventArgs e)

16    {

17       if(e.Content is CatalogContentBase)

18       {

19          //Your code goes here

20       }

21    }

22    public void Dispose()
23    {
24
25          //Unregister the event to avoid memory leaks.
26          _contentEvents.MovingContent -= Content_Moving;
27    }
28 }

       ²Currently there is no access control list management in catalog system, it's all or nothing: a user might have access to all catalog contents,

    or nothing. Of course this is a limitation, but it will not be addressed anytime soon. If you want to do some extra check for permissions, you'll
    have to do it your own.
    Chapter 9: Content events.                                                                             169

            Deleting and Deleted. Like common event naming convention, the content events comes
            with pair of events - such as Updating/Updated, or Deleting/Deleted. By convention, the
            Deleting event is supposed to fire before it happens, while the Deleted is fired after. Just
            keep in mind that at the point of Deleted event, you'll no longer be able to get back the
            content. So if you want to do something with catalog content being deleted, make sure to
            use Deleting event.

            There is no "Waste Basket" concept in CatalogContentProvider. So when you delete a
            catalog content, it's gone for good, it is not moving to Waste Basket as normal CMS contents.

    It's convenient to use IContentEvents, but there is a limitation. Those events only get fired if you
    works on CatalogContentProvider level (via IContentRepository). If you work with lower levels,
    such as working with ICatalogSystem directly without involve the content system, those events will
    not be fired. In a perfect world, you should only have to care about IContentEvents, but we are still
    far from it. While you are no longer required to work with ICatalogSystem anymore, ServiceAPI is
    still using it internally. ServiceAPI is increasingly popular, and most sites I've seen are using it, or
    planned to use it.

                     ServiceAPI 5.0 is the first version to use the content APIs to handle catalogs, nodes and
                     entries.

    You'll need another way to get it works, that's when CatalogEventListenerBase comes into place,
    and here's what it looks like:

1 public abstract class CatalogEventListenerBase

2     {

3           public virtual void EntryUpdating(object source, EntryEventArgs args) { }

4           public virtual void EntryUpdated(object source, EntryEventArgs args) { }

5           public virtual void EntryDeleted(object source, DeletedEntryEventArgs args) \

6 {}

7           public virtual void NodeUpdating(object source, NodeEventArgs args) { }

8           public virtual void NodeUpdated(object source, NodeEventArgs args) { }

9           public virtual void NodeDeleted(object source, DeletedNodeEventArgs args) { }

10          public virtual void CatalogUpdating(object source, CatalogEventArgs args) { }

11          public virtual void CatalogUpdated(object source, CatalogEventArgs args) { }

12          public virtual void CatalogDeleted(object source, DeletedCatalogEventArgs ar\

13 gs) { }

14          public virtual void CatalogLanguageUpdated(object source, CatalogLanguageEve\

15 ntArgs args) { }

16          public virtual void AssociationUpdating(object source, AssociationEventArgs \

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 9: Content events.                                          170

17 args) { }

18           public virtual void AssociationUpdated(object source, AssociationEventArgs a\

19 rgs) { }

20           public virtual void AssociationDeleted(object source, DeletedAssociationEven\

21 tArgs args) { }

22           public virtual void RelationUpdating(object source, RelationEventArgs args) \

23 { }

24           public virtual void RelationUpdated(object source, RelationEventArgs args) {\

25 }

26           public virtual void RelationDeleted(object source, DeletedRelationEventArgs \

27 args) { }

28      }

    How does it work? At the time your site starts up, EventContext in Mediachase.Commerce.Catalog
    will look for all registered instances of CatalogEventListenerBase (Episerver Commerce uses struc-
    turemap to get those instance, that's why your custom implementation will need the ServiceConfiguration(typeof(
    attribute), and then register their methods to its events. When certain change is made to catalog
    system, for example when a CatalogEntryDto is saved, EventContext will be called and it will fire
    the event itself, and the methods registered with that event will be triggered.

    Simple, right?

    Now let's consider two cases of processing events with CatalogEventListenerBase:

    The first one is when you listen to the events from the same context (aka local event). For example,
    you have ServiceAPI running in the context of their site, and every time your Windows service
    imports something via ServiceAPI to your system, you want to listen to them and do some special
    indexing.

    The good news is this is the simple case. You can just create your own implementation of
    CatalogEventListenerBase and override the method you want to listen, then register your class
    like this:

    Note that you don't have to override all the methods, just the ones you want to listen to. Build it,
    and it should be ready to go.

    The second one is a more advanced scenario, you want to have ServiceAPI to run in a separate site,
    may be because of performance issues or security concerns. But when you import entries and nodes
    into that ServiceAPI instance, you want your site to listen to those events and act accordingly,
    probably to do some extra processing (clearing cache, indexing, etc. etc.). You want it to be "just
    ServiceAPI", so it means simpler to configure and maintenance.

    In other words, you need to listen to remote events and act.

    In such case, CatalogEventListenerBase can't help you, at least directly. It is supposed to listen to
    local events, and it has to be in same context when you fire the events. You don't have both.

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 9: Content events.                                                                  171

    But luckily, the solution comes from one existing implementation of CatalogEventListenerBase -
    CatalogEventBroadcaster. As its name might suggest, this class can be used to broadcast events,
    even remotely.

                The following section will be an exercise to work with CatalogEventBroadcaster

    CatalogEventBroadcaster uses Episerver Framework Events internally, so it might be worth to learn
    one thing or two about Events. Events use UDP to broadcast the events, with two important data: a
    GUID to identify the event (So listeners only listen to the events they care about), and a byte array
    contains the information from the sender. By default, the remote event for listening and broadcasting
    is disabled, you might need to enable them by un-commenting these lines in web.config:

1 <services>

2 <service name="EPiServer.Events.Remote.EventReplication" behaviorConfiguration="De\

3 bugServiceBehaviour">

4   <endpoint name="RemoteEventServiceEndPoint" contract="EPiServer.Events.ServiceMo\

5 del.IEventReplication"

6   binding="customBinding" bindingConfiguration="RemoteEventsBinding" address="\

7 soap.udp://239.255.255.19:5000/RemoteEventService" />

8 </service>

9 <!-- Enable remote service -->

10 </services>

11 <client>

12 <endpoint name="RemoteEventServiceClientEndPoint" address="soap.udp://239.255.255.\

13 19:5000/RemoteEventService"

14  binding="customBinding" bindingConfiguration="RemoteEventsBinding" contract="EPi\

15 Server.Events.ServiceModel.IEventReplication" />

16 </client>

                     A simple way to check if your setting is correct and the remote events are working, is to edit
                     an entry in Commerce Manager and check if the changes were reflected property in Catalog
                     UI. If it works, then remote events are working and the problem might be in somewhere else.
                     If it does not, then check above setting and try again.

    Now get back to the problem we have. CatalogEventBroadcaster use Events to raise the event re-
    motely, so we need to know its event "Id", which is CatalogEventBroadcaster.CommerceProductUpdated

    The first part is registering a method to listen to this event in your class. Note that this class can be
    an ordinary class - it does not have to inherit from CatalogEventListenerBase:

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 9: Content events.                                           172

1 public void AddEvent()

2{

3     var catalogEventUpdatedEvent = Event.Get(CatalogEventBroadcaster.CommerceProduct\

4 Updated);

5     catalogEventUpdatedEvent.Raised += CatalogEventUpdated;

6}

   You would need to call this method during your site initialization (Strictly speaking, you don't have
   to, you can register when you really want to process the event. But in most of the cases, you should
   register when your site starts, so all the event registration is in one place, and you know where to
   find them.), there are several ways to do that, but here's the pattern I usually follow:

1 //Inside an IInitializationModule

2 public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

3{

4     var listener = context.Locate.Advanced.GetInstance<CatalogContentEventListener>(\

5 );

6     listener.AddEvent();

7}

   It's also important to de-register your method from the event to avoid possible memory leaks, so
   let's create another method to do that:

1 public void RemoveEvent()

2{

3     var catalogEventUpdatedEvent = Event.Get(CatalogEventBroadcaster.CommerceProduct\

4 Updated);

5     catalogEventUpdatedEvent.Raised -= CatalogEventUpdated;

6}

   And this method should be called in Uninitialize:

1 //Inside your IInitializationModule

2 public void Uninitialize(InitializationEngine context)

3{

4     _listener.RemoveEvent();

5}

   The remaining work is to implement CatalogEventUpdated method, which should have a signature
   like this:

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 9: Content events.                                                                   173

1 private void CatalogEventUpdated(object sender, EventNotificationEventArgs e)

    The information carried with the CatalogEventBroadcaster events (stored in EventNotificationEventArgs.Param)
    are byte[] contain serialized data, so let's deserialize it back to object:

1 private static EventArgs DeSerialize(byte[] buffer)

2{

3   var formatter = new BinaryFormatter();

4   using (var stream = new MemoryStream(buffer))

5   {

6      return formatter.Deserialize(stream) as EventArgs;

7   }

8}

   Now depends on the events you want to listen, you'll have to cast EventArgs returned by
   DeSerialize to CatalogContentUpdateEventArgs, and check for its EventType property, which is
   a string.

   These are the events you can listen to:

 1 CatalogUpdatingEventType = "CatalogUpdating";
 2
 3 CatalogUpdatedEventType = "CatalogUpdated";
 4
 5 CatalogDeletedEventType = "CatalogDeleted";
 6
 7 CatalogLanguageUpdatedEventType = "CatalogLanguageUpdated";
 8
 9 CatalogNodeUpdatingEventType = "CatalogNodeUpdating";
10
11 CatalogNodeUpdatedEventType = "CatalogNodeUpdated";
12
13 CatalogNodeDeletedEventType = "CatalogNodeDeleted";
14
15 CatalogEntryUpdatingEventType = "CatalogEntryUpdating";
16
17 CatalogEntryUpdatedEventType = "CatalogEntryUpdated";
18
19 CatalogEntryDeletedEventType = "CatalogEntryDeleted";
20
21 AssociationUpdatingEventType = "AssociationUpdating";
22
23 AssociationUpdatedEventType = "AssociationUpdated";

                                                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 9: Content events.  174

24
25 AssociationDeletedEventType = "AssociationDeleted";
26
27 RelationUpdatingEventType = "RelationUpdating";
28
29 RelationUpdatedEventType = "RelationUpdated";
30
31 RelationDeletedEventType = "RelationDeleted";
32
33 LanguageConfigurationChangedEventType = "LanguageConfigurationChanged";

     There are "only" 17 events - just make sure you only process only events you need to. Listening on
     events which you won't actually process will have negative impact on the performance. It would
     be a good idea, to process the events in an async manner, except if you explicitly want it to be
     synchronous (for example if you want to cancel a change that should not be allowed, because the
     lack of permission handling for catalog content, you have to do it yourself.).

                       Note that unlike the events from IContentEvents which actually allows you to inject your
                       code into the pipeline, CatalogEventBroadcaster only provides a way to react to the events.
                       For that purpose, the ...Updating or ...Deleting events are less than useful - and you can
                       ignore them. In fact, from Commerce 11.8, they will be removed.

     These properties contains the real data, depends on which event you're listening to, they might be
     empty or not.

 1 /// <summary>
 2 /// Gets or sets the list of catalog Id.
 3 /// </summary>
 4 public IEnumerable<int> CatalogIds { get; set; }
 5
 6 /// <summary>
 7 /// Gets or sets the list of catalog node Id.
 8 /// </summary>
 9 public IEnumerable<int> CatalogNodeIds { get; set; }
10
11 /// <summary>
12 /// Gets or sets the list of catalog entry Id.
13 /// </summary>
14 public IEnumerable<int> CatalogEntryIds { get; set; }
15
16 /// <summary>
17 /// Gets or sets the list of catalog association id.

                                                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 9: Content events.                                                                175

18 /// </summary>
19 public IEnumerable<int> CatalogAssociationIds { get; set; }

You might argue that the BinaryFormatter is slow to use in Serialization and
Deserialization. You might want to use something faster - but that does mean you'll need
to implement your own CatalogEventBroadcaster.

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 10: Inventories, Prices and
    Orders events

    Inventories and prices events

    Inventories and prices events were added quite recently, in Commerce 8.15 and was slightly changed
    in Commerce 8.16. (It's not that far away at this time of writing, the current released version of
    Commerce is 9.19. It's just we're moving fast.)

    While inventories are changed frequently and are less likely to get benefit from these changes, price
    events can be really useful - you would want to (and you probably should) re-index an entry when
    its prices are changed. That means when you update prices by other means than in Commerce
    Manager, your entries will be indexed and your facets will be updated. Inventories and Prices events
    share many common characteristics. They are both based on CatalogKeyEventBroadcaster. The idea
    is the same with CatalogEventBroadcaster - when a change is made to inventory or price system,
    the implementations of those systems will fire an event. Listeners will have to listen to that event
    and react.

    Firing events

    As we mentioned about, the default implementations of IPriceService and IInventoryStorage will
    fire events when changes are made. If you have custom implementations of those interfaces - you
    will have to trigger the events yourself. It's simply calling CatalogKeyEventBroadcaster to do the
    work:

1 public class CatalogKeyEventBroadcaster

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// Event raised when one or many prices have been updated.

5   /// </summary>

6   public event EventHandler<PriceUpdateEventArgs> PriceUpdated;

7

8   /// <summary>

9   /// Event raised when an inventory has been updated.

10  /// </summary>

11  public event EventHandler<InventoryUpdateEventArgs> InventoryUpdated;

12
    Chapter 10: Inventories, Prices and Orders events                                                 177

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Raise event when prices or inventories are updated.

15    /// </summary>

16    public void RaiseEvent(CatalogKeyEventArgs e)

17

18    /// <summary>

19    /// Trigger <see cref="PriceUpdated"/>.

20    /// </summary>

21    public virtual void OnPriceUpdated(object source, PriceUpdateEventArgs args)

22

23    /// <summary>

24    /// Trigger <see cref="InventoryUpdated"/>.

25    /// </summary>

26    public virtual void OnInventoryUpdated(object source, InventoryUpdateEventArgs a\

27 rgs)

28 }

          Why CatalogKey? As you might know CatalogKey is a simple class (with ApplicationId
          (which is, luckily, obsoleted in Commerce 11) and CatalogEntryCode) - to identify an
          entry. If there is anything shared between both prices and inventories - then it is they
          are indeed attached to entries, so CatalogKeyEventArgs will be used to let the listeners
          know which entries' prices and inventories were updated. PriceUpdateEventArgs and
          InventoryUpdateEventArgs extends this class by setting the Name of the event.

    And then when you need to trigger an event, for example, when prices are updated (aka after you
    implement your logic in IPriceService.SetCatalogEntryPrices), simply call this:

1     var priceUpdateEventArgs = new PriceUpdateEventArgs(catalogKeySet)

2     {

3         ApplicationHasContentModelTypes = _applicationContext.HasContentModelTypes

4     };

5     broadcaster.OnPriceUpdated(priceValues, priceUpdateEventArgs);

    ApplicationHasContentModelTypes is a special flag to let the listener know if the sending application
    has the content types or not. FindCommerce relies on this flag to decide if it should reindex the
    content. If the current application has the custom content types (the content types you inherit from
    builtin ones), then this should be true, otherwise it's false.

          It's worth noting that a change made by IPriceDetailService does not guarantee that an
          event will be fired. The default implementation of IPriceDetailService will not trigger an
          event in case the default implementation of IPriceService is marked as ReadOnly. Only
          prices make their way to IPriceService will trigger an event.

                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 10: Inventories, Prices and Orders events                                                    178

    Listening to events

    Most of the cases - you will not have to fire events, but listen to them. We can simply follow the
    pattern in previous chapter, but now with new EventId:

1 var catalogKeyEventGuid = Event.Get(CatalogKeyEventBroadcaster.CatalogKeyEventGuid);
2 catalogKeyEventGuid.Raised += CatalogKeyEventUpdated;

         It's worth reminding that this is for registering the event - which you should call in the the
         IInitializableModule.Initialize(InitializationEngine context). You always should
         de-register the event in Uninitialize(InitializationEngine context).

    Now it's time to implement CatalogKeyEventUpdated - we already know how its signature should
    look like:

1 private void CatalogKeyEventUpdated(object sender, EventNotificationEventArgs e)

2{

3     var eventArgs = (CatalogKeyEventArgs)DeSerialize((byte[])e.Param);

4     var priceUpdatedEventArgs = eventArgs as PriceUpdateEventArgs;

5     if (priceUpdatedEventArgs != null)

6     {

7        RemotePriceUpdated(sender, priceUpdatedEventArgs);

8        return;

9     }

10    var inventoryUpdatedEventArgs = eventArgs as InventoryUpdateEventArgs;

11    if (inventoryUpdatedEventArgs != null)

12    {

13       RemoteInventoryUpdated(sender, inventoryUpdatedEventArgs);

14    }

15 }

    Let's try to do something useful in this case: reindex the entries which have updated prices - this is
    important because once the prices are updated, the facets should be updated, too.

                  Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 10: Inventories, Prices and Orders events                            179

1 private void RemotePriceUpdated(object sender, PriceUpdateEventArgs priceUpdatedEven\

2 tArgs)

3{

4   var searchManager = new SearchManager(AppContext.Current.ApplicationName);

5   var itemIds = _referenceConverter.GetContentLinks(priceUpdatedEventArgs.CatalogK\

6 eys.Select(c=>c.CatalogEntryCode), CatalogContentType.CatalogEntry);

7   searchManager.UpdateIndex(itemIds);

8}

  Orders events

   The old way

    It's fairly common to have your orders processed by an external system. You can of course write
    code to periodically call the external system to process orders that were updated or deleted, but there
    is a better way. You can listen to certain events and react when an order is changed. Those events
    are in OrderContext class and are defined like this:

1 /// <summary>
2 /// Occurs when an order group is updated.
3 /// </summary>
4 public event EventHandler<OrderGroupEventArgs> OrderGroupUpdated;
5
6 /// <summary>
7 /// Occurs when an order group is deleted.
8 /// </summary>
9 public event EventHandler<OrderGroupEventArgs> OrderGroupDeleted;

    So if you want to do some extra processing when an order is updated, you can register your listener
    in an initialization module:

1 //Register to the event:
2 OrderContext.OrderGroupUpdated += OrderGroupUpdated;
3
4 //Listen to the event
5 private void OrderGroupUpdated(object sender, OrderGroupEventArgs e)

    Those events are for OrderGroup - when an OrderGroup is changed - via AcceptChanges(),
    the corresponding event will be fired. If you recall from previous chapter, an OrderGroup can
    be a PurchaseOrder, a PaymentPlan, or a Cart. So if you want to listen to changes made to
    PurchaseOrder:s only, you will need a little casting:

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 10: Inventories, Prices and Orders events                   180

1 //Listen to the event

2 private void OrderGroupUpdated(object sender, OrderGroupEventArgs e)

3{

4   var po = sender as PurchaseOrder;

5   if (po != null)

6   {

7      // do your stuffs here.

8   }

9}

    It's worth noting that the orders events, unlike the events we talked about, are local-only. So if you
    have multiple sites and an order is changed in one site, then the listeners will not be called in other
    sites. However, I don't think there is a need for you to fire events in one site and process in another
    one.

    The new way

    The events in OrderContext work very well if you are using the concrete implementation. But if you
    are using new abstraction APIs, there are reasons that they might want to avoid them. Firstly, they
    are attached to OrderContext and all old Mediachase namespace, which might "pollute" your new,
    shiny code. Secondly and more importantly, you might have custom implementation, which does
    not use the old, default OrderGroup or PurchaseOrder, those events will not even be called. That's
    when you need IOrderRepositoryCallback.

    It's an interface which defines the methods to be implemented if you want to them to be called when
    a certain event occurs.

1 public interface IOrderRepositoryCallback

2{

3   /// <summary>

4   /// Occurs when order group is about to be created.

5   /// </summary>

6   void OnCreating(Guid customerId, string name);

7

8   /// <summary>

9   /// Occurs when order group was created.

10  /// </summary>

11  void OnCreated(OrderReference orderReference);

12

13  /// <summary>

14  /// Occurs when order group is about to be updated.

15  /// </summary>

16  void OnUpdating(OrderReference orderReference);

                         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 10: Inventories, Prices and Orders events                 181

17    /// <summary>
18    /// Occurs when order group was updated.
19    /// </summary>
20    void OnUpdated(OrderReference orderReference);
21
22    /// <summary>
23    /// Occurs when order group is about to be updated.
24    /// </summary>
25    void OnDeleting(OrderReference orderReference);
26
27    /// <summary>
28    /// Occurs when order group was deleted.
29    /// </summary>
30    void OnDeleted(OrderReference orderReference);
31
32 }

   If you remember CatalogEventListenerBase, you'll find the similarity here. If you want to do
   something when an order is updated, then add a class which implements IOrderRepositoryCallback
   and add your processing code in OnUpdated method:

1 [ServiceConfiguration(typeof(IOrderRepositoryCallback))]

2 public class MyOrderRepositoryCallback : IOrderRepositoryCallback

3{

4     public void OnUpdated(OrderReference orderReference)

5     {

6        //do stuffs

7     }

8

9     //Other methods

10 }

   The ServiceConfiguration is very important, because that mean you register your class as an
   implementation of IOrderRepositoryCallback (You can select a suitable life cycle, depending on
   how your class holds states). That would allow the default implementation of IOrderRepository to
   get that implementation and call it when it deletes an order.

                       Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 10: Inventories, Prices and Orders events                                               182

Now it's a moment of truth, I think IOrderRepositoryCallback has several design is-
sues. Firstly, it is an interface. Yes, it might look nicer than an abstract class (like
CatalogEventListenerBase is), but that means you have to implement all of the methods,
even you want to override just one. Yes the implementation is easy and you can have an
empty method which does nothing, but it still feels forceful. Secondly, all methods takes an
OrderReference only, as a parameter. That would mean if you want to do extra processing
on the order, you will have to load it again. Also, it means that you can't really do anything
with the order in OnDeleted (because at that point the order has been already deleted).
IOrderRepositoryCallback is still useful in certain scenarios, and I'm not saying you should
not use it, I'm just pointing out it could have been better.

It's also worth noting that at moment of this writing (11.3), Commerce Manager still uses the old
concrete APIs in many of order management parts, so IOrderRepositoryCallback will not work if
you do certain orders operations in Commerce Manager. You'd have to fall back to OrderContext
events for that.

Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Part 5: Search

Search has become more and more important part of any software system. When the data is big
enough, search is essential, you and your customers don't want to, and can't, go through endless
lists to find the product you want. A good commerce site can't be lacking a fast, effective search
and filter solutions to let customers quickly find things they need - and even better - to discover
things they might want. In this chapter, we will explore the searching options Episerver Commerce
provides, and how can you use it in your website implementation.
Chapter 11: Search

Searching for products.

SearchProviders

The first option we are going to talk about is the search provider system in Commerce. It's basically
index-based search system that many important features in Commerce Manager use index-based
search provider to search for entries - it's simply faster and more flexible to work with. They might
not be truly "real-time", but they're close (with the right configuration). The only drawbacks are
they require extra processing (for indexing entries), external files/configurations, but the advantages
easily overweight any of those drawbacks.

                 It's also possible to find entries by SQL-based APIs - we will get to that later.

For search providers to work, they need "index" of the entries. An extra, prerequisite step is required:
each and every entry must be indexed for later search. All of 1st party search providers are built on
Lucene index, which if you are interested, you can read more about how the documents are indexed
here²
There are three search providers provided by Episerver Commerce: LuceneSearchProvider, Solr35SearchProvider,
and FindSearchProvider (four if you count SolrSearchProvider as well. As other "provider system",
you can implement your own search provider.):

   · LuceneSearchProvider comes as the default option with EPiServer.Commerce.Core, and it's
      the only one does not require extra configurations - it can just work out of the box.
      LuceneSearchProvider is based on Lucene.net to index and query "documents", and the index
      files will be stored locally. (Not strictly locally as you can put them on a shared network drive,
      but your IIS instance must have direct access to the files)

   · Solr35SearchProvider is a hosted search provider solution. So you need to configure a Solr
      server to store indexes, and to handle search queries through HTTP requests. General
      understanding is that Solr provides better performance and scalability than Lucene.net, but
      that comes at a cost - more configuration and more maintainance.

   · FindSearchProvider is comparable with Solr35SearchProvider as it works with an external
      service via HTTP requests. Only this time it's Episerver Find. As we will learn later, Find-
      SearchProvider != FindCommerce.

   ²http://lucene.apache.org/core/4_10_2/core/org/apache/lucene/codecs/lucene410/package-summary.html#package_description
Chapter 11: Search                                                                        185

So which provider is right for you? LuceneSearchProvider works pretty well to some points, but after
that, it struggles when the catalog becomes big enough, or when it has to process high number of
queries at the same time. When that happens, it might be the time for you to consider more scalable
search providers - Solr35SearchProvider, FindSearchProvider, or even better, Find Commerce.

Strictly speaking, Find Commerce is not a search provider, and it does not fully replace
one, because it can not be used in Commerce Manager. However, it's usually the front
end site which has to manage the high load of queries. You can have a hybrid solution,
to use FindCommerce on the front-end site while use LuceneSearchProvider in Commerce
Manager. Because Commerce Manager usually has much lower load than front-end,
LucenceSearchProvider can probably handle it just fine.

Where SearchProvider is used

It can be confusing to understand where the SearchProvider is used or how does it distinct from
EPiServer Search? It can be easier to grasp the idea if you remember that SearchProvider concept
came from a Commerce (Mediachase eCF) background. Despite of a big effort Episerver have tried
to integrate Commerce into CMS, they are still two sub-systems working together at this time of
writing (and will be, at least in near future). They are both index-based search system, but with a
very important difference. EPiServer Search works with CMS pages, while SearchProvider works
only with catalog entries.

If you are going to implement your own search provider, you would need to know where your
provider is gonna be used. Search Provider is used heavily in Commerce Manager - As mentioned
above, Commerce Manager uses SearchProvider in almost search-related functionalities, either
when you search to edit an entry, or when you add a lineitem to an order, or when you set an
entry to be a gift in a promotion.

                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search  186

                                         New Line Item dialog - powered by SearchProvider

There is still one exception: Search by code or id in Catalog Management use SQL search internally.

                            Search By Code/Id is the only place which does not use SearchProvider

In Editing UI, Catalog UI uses SearchProvider internally in the quick search box, but returns all

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search  187

entries including inactive (which is different from default searches which hide the inactive entries)

                                                      Search box use SearchProvider

And Global search uses SearchProvider as well, search results from SearchProvider are combined
with results from other searches:

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search  188

                                                                 Global search

                 What happens if you use Find? SearchProvider will works (if properly configured) - they are
                 not mutually exclusive, even if you use FindSearchProvider. FindSearchProvider uses Find
                 internally for storing and querying the indices, but in "its own way", not the "strongly typed
                 contents" as FindCommerce does. The indices for Find and for FindSearchProvider will still
                 be separated, even if you share the same "serviceIndex" from Episerver. That would mean
                 if you use both Find (for front-end site) and FindSearchProvider for Commerce Manager,
                 you index count will be double in size.

Use Search API:s
Now enough of theory. Let's go into some technical detail. The search provider system is build
around SearchManager and CatalogEntrySearchCriteria. In its simplest form, searching using
search API:s can be easy like this:

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search                                                                          189

1 CatalogEntrySearchCriteria criteria = new CatalogEntrySearchCriteria();
2 criteria.SearchPhrase = "panasonic";
3 SearchManager manager = new SearchManager(AppContext.Current.ApplicationName);
4 ISearchResults results = manager.Search(criteria);
5 var ids = results.GetKeyFieldValues<int>()

    We're searching for the entries with the keyword "panasonic", and getting a list of Id:s for matching
    entries. From this ids, you can get the list of contents. Still remember ReferenceConverter and
    IContentLoader/IContentRepository?

                     Note that with the code above, any entry with a "panasonic" keyword in one of the indexed
                     fields will match, no matter it's in the name, the code, the display name or the description
                     (as long as the fields are marked as "indexable"). What if you want to search for entries with
                     "panasonic" in name, specifically - we will get to that soon enough.

Let's explore more complex scenarios - how much can we search with CatalogEntrySearchCriteria
- it is not a simple class, so we will go through most important properties, one by one.

   · CatalogNames: Limits the catalogs you want to search in. If you have multiple catalogs, this
      can be used. Let's reuse the example above. You might have a catalog for consumer products
      and a catalog for professional products - and their names are respectively "Consumer" and
      "Professional", if you want to limit the search for entries in "Consumer" catalog only:

1 criteria.CatalogNames.Add("Consumer");

        · CatalogNodes: Limits the catalog nodes you want to search in. If you want to limit the search
          for entries only in TV and Bluray nodes:

1 criteria.CatalogNodes.Add("TV");
2 criteria.CatalogNodes.Add("Bluray");

    However, you can use Outlines as an alternative

        · Outlines: The "paths" you want to search in, in form of CatalogName\CatalogNodes

1 criteria.Outlines.Add("Consumer\TV\OLED");

        · ClassTypes: The types of entries you want to search for (Product, Variation, Package or
          Bundle). For example, to search for Product only:

                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search                                                                                  190

1 criteria.ClassTypes.Add("Product");

    All properties above are of type StringCollection - and they mean "restrictions". Leave them empty
    mean you don't want to filter by that criteria. Note that you can add multiple criteria to one property.
    For example, you can filter by both "Product" and "Variation":

1 criteria.ClassTypes.Add("Product");
2 criteria.ClassTypes.Add("Variation");

    Those settings are useful, but nothing is particularly interesting - but the next one might be

Specific filters

In our first example we searched for "panasonic", so any items which has an indexable field which
contains "panasonic". What if you want to search for a specific field only. This can be done by a
method in the base class of CatalogEntrySearchCriteria - AbstractSearchCriteria:

1 criteria.Add("displayname", "panasonic");

    Add takes two parameters, one for the field name, and one for the value of that field you want to
    filter by.
    There is also an overload that take the field name and a list of ISearchFilterValue, which mean
    you can filter by multiple values. You can search for "panasonic" and "samsung" in "displayname"
    like this:

1 var filters = new List<ISearchFilterValue>();
2 filters.Add(new SimpleValue { key = "displayname", value = "panasonic" });
3 filters.Add(new SimpleValue { key = "displayname", value = "samsung" });
4 criteria.Add("displayname", filters);

SearchFilter / Facets

Facet or faceted search is an important feature of any eCommerce websites - it allows your customers
to quickly filter the products based on some criteria when they browse your product listing. For
example, here a very simple facet by Quicksilver

                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search  191

                               It might not be the best facet in the world, but it gives you the idea

There is more than 1 way to create faceted searching, but the most easy, flexible and powerful way,
is probably relying on the search functionalities. That means, your product listing page is simply a
search-result display page (pretty common pattern, you know). The facets in Commerce Search API:s
are done by SearchFilter. The idea is pretty simple - you define the filters you want your customers
to see, and for every search, the search results will include the number of matching products for each
filter. When the customer selects a "facet", you'll include the filter mapped with that in the search
to limit the scope.

Here's how facets are done in Episerver Commerce:

   · You define filters you would like to have in Mediachase.Search.Filters.Config, here's an
      example of a filter for field = "size":

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 11: Search                                                  192

1 <SearchConfig version="1.0">

2   <SearchFilters>

3   <Filter field="size">

4   <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

5   <Description locale="en">Size</Description>

6   <Description locale="sv">Storlek</Description>

7   </Descriptions>

8   <Values>

9   <SimpleValue key="s" value="s">

10  <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

11                      <Description locale="en">S</Description>

12  </Descriptions>

13  </SimpleValue>

14  <SimpleValue key="m" value="m">

15  <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

16                      <Description locale="en">M</Description>

17  </Descriptions>

18  </SimpleValue>

19  <SimpleValue key="l" value="l">

20  <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

21                      <Description locale="en">L</Description>

22  </Descriptions>

23  </SimpleValue>

24  </Values>

25  </Filter>

26  </SearchFilters>

27 </SearchConfig>

    Pretty simple, right? You define a filter for the field "size" - which is a searchable metafield. You
    then define the value you want to create filters for, in this case, "s", "m", and "l" as three possible
    values for "size". Of course, depends on your catalog, you might add the value for "xxs" or "XL".

    There are three type of values you can configure for the filters:

    · The simple value as we see above.
    · The price range value:

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search                                                        193

1 <PriceRangeValue key="100-200" currency="USD" lowerbound="100" upperbound="200" lo\

2 werboundincluded="false" upperboundincluded="true">

3  <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

4                   <Description locale="en">$100-$200</Description>

5                   <Description locale="de">100â,¬-200â,¬</Description>

6  </Descriptions>

7 </PriceRangeValue>

     (How prices are indexed will be discussed later, when we talk about implementing customizing
     the search indexer.)
   · The range value, basically everything which is "numberic", but price:

1 <RangeValue key="under-1.5" upperbound="1.5">

2 <Descriptions defaultLocale="en">

3  <Description locale="en">1.5 in &amp; Under</Description>

4  <Description locale="de">1.5 in &amp; Under</Description>

5 </Descriptions>

6 </RangeValue>

The SearchFilters configuration, in fact, is not required - you can define your SearchFilter(s) by
code, however. SearchFilterHelper in Mediachase.Commerce.Website will automatically deserial-
ize this information for you, and having these information in a separated config file should make
the configuration much more easier, without having to recompile your code.

                 It's worth spending some times to consider the facets you will show to your customers.
                 I've seen a site (Luckily, not using Episerver Commerce), to list almost every size of the
                 smartphones they have: 1.2", 1.7", 2.3", 3.0", 3.5", 3.7", 4", etc etc. I consider those to be a bad
                 facet configuration. Nowadays, you should only have something like "Less than 4"", "From
                 4"-5.1"", "From 5.2" to 6"", and "More than 6"" (more or less, you can argue about the exact
                 numbers, but you get the idea). As a developer, you might not be the one to decide which
                 facets will be, but if you see some bad facets like above, make sure to bring some good
                 arguments and make your facets better.

    All the SearchFilter(s) configured will be available in

1 SearchFilterHelper.Current.SearchConfig.SearchFilters

    At first, we can try to add all the filters:

                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 11: Search                                                             194

1 foreach (SearchFilter filter in SearchConfig.SearchFilters)

2{

3   criteria.Add(filter);

4}

    This signals the search provider to return the matching count of all facets, and you can display them
    in your fancy shiny website. When a customer select a facet (which is commonly stored in the URI
    QueryString), we can try to include that filter into the search. First, we need to figure out which
    facets have been selected:

1 var filters = new List<SelectedFilter>();

2 foreach (string key in Request.QueryString.AllKeys)

3{

4   foreach (var filter in SearchFilterHelper.Current.SearchConfig.SearchFilters)

5   {

6      //Get the values from QueryString, here we are assuming | is the separator

7      var values = Request.QueryString[filter.field].Split('|');

8      foreach (var value in values)

9      {

10        if (filter.Values.SimpleValue != null)

11        {

12                      var filterValue = filter.Values.SimpleValue

13                         .FirstOrDefault(x => x.value.Equals(value, StringComparison.Ordi\

14 nalIgnoreCase));

15                      if (filterValue != null)

16                      {

17                         filters.Add(new SelectedFilter(filter, filterValue));

18                      }

19        }

20

21        if (filter.Values.RangeValue != null)

22        {

23                      var filterValue = filter.Values.RangeValue

24                         .FirstOrDefault(x => x.key.Equals(value, StringComparison.Ordina\

25 lIgnoreCase));

26                      if (filterValue != null)

27                      {

28                         filters.Add(new SelectedFilter(filter, filterValue));

29                      }

30        }

31

32        if (filter.Values.PriceRangeValue != null)

33        {

                           Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 11: Search                                                                      195

34                      var filterValue = filter.Values.PriceRangeValue

35                         .FirstOrDefault(x => x.key.Equals(value, StringComparison.Ordina\

36 lIgnoreCase)

37                         && x.currency.Equals(SiteContext.Current.Currency.CurrencyCode,

38                         StringComparison.OrdinalIgnoreCase));

39                      if (filterValue != null)

40                      {

41                         filters.Add(new SelectedFilter(filter, filterValue));

42                      }

43          }

44       }

45    }

46 }

47 return filters;

    The idea is simpler than the code looks (the nested foreach:es and if:s make it looks worse) we
    iterate over all the keys in QueryString (split by "|", you can of course choose another delimiter):

      · If a key is found to be a filter.field, we get its value and split it into values(Assuming you
        want to allow multiple facets selection, for example, filter for both "red" and "blue").

      · If a value matches one of the Values of the filter, we add a new SelectedFilter to the list.
        Depends on the type of filter.Values:
           ­ SimpleValue, the value must be matching a simple field.

           ­ RangeValue, the value must be within the selected range, such as size.

           ­ PriceRangeValue, the price (in the currently selected currency) must be within the selected
             range.

      · After all the keys were tested, we return a list of selected filters.

    The last step is to include the selected filters in your search criteria, to filter the products customer
    wanted to see:

1 foreach (var filter in filters)

2{

3     if (filter.PriceRangeValue != null)

4     {

5        criteria.Add(filter.Filter.field, filter.PriceRangeValue);

6     }

7     if (filter.RangeValue != null)

8     {

9        criteria.Add(filter.Filter.field, filter.RangeValue);

10    }

11    if (filter.SimpleValue != null)

                           Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 11: Search                                                                  196

12    {

13       criteria.Add(filter.Filter.field, filter.SimpleValue);

14    }

15 }

    And then we can pass this criteria to the search APIs. It's easy after all, right?

    Search provider internals.

    Prior to Commerce R3a, catalog entries must be indexed manually. That means, every time you edit
    an entry, you must trigger an index build, so the newly updated fields will be search-able. This can
    be done by manually clicking on the "Build index" button in Commerce Manager, or to run the
    "Incremental Search Index" scheduled job. Of course that is not something you would like to.
    Commerce R3a came with a feature allow the indexing to be fully automated - you are no longer
    required to go to Search Index and click the Build Index button manually.

    Search Index UI in Commerce Manager. However, the status of the processor is jokingly called "The most mysterious
    feature in Commerce Manager"

    The indexer handle all three scenarios:

       · Rebuild the index entirely: which does mean all entries will be re-indexed, regardless if they
          have been changed since the last indexing time. This is an expensive operation, of course, and
          you should not use it for a running production site. However, it might make senses to do that
          when you deploy your site, when the content types (and the the metaclasses) of your entries
          might have changed (metafields have been added or removed). And that is not "Recorded" by
          the system.

       · Build the index: SearchProvider system keeps track of the last time index was built, and it finds
          out and tries to index the entries which have been updated since - aka incremental indexing.
          This is the preferred option as it's reasonable cheap, however, it (theoretically) should not be
          needed anymore, if you enable...

                                                     Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 11: Search                                                                                 197

    · Index by event: The indexer will try to index every time an entry is updated. This was added
      in Commerce R3, and it has been working pretty well, except when it's not. We'll dive deep to
      see how it works and how to make it works a bit later.

    It's important to know that it's not only the entries which were actually changed. The
    SearchProvider system has to take the relation into account as well. So if you move a node
    to a new location, all of its entries - either true children ("Primary" node) or linked will need
    to be reindexed.

    SearchProviders are configured by Mediachase.Search.config, in Configs folder of both front-end
    and Commerce Manager site, here's an example of how it looks like:

1 <?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8"?>

2 <Mediachase.Search>

3 <SearchProviders defaultProvider="LuceneSearchProvider">

4   <providers>

5   <add name="Solr35SearchProvider" type="Mediachase.Search.Providers.Solr35.Solr\

6 SearchProvider, Mediachase.Search.Solr35SearchProvider" queryBuilderType="Mediachase\

7 .Search.Providers.Solr35.SolrSearchQueryBuilder, Mediachase.Search.Solr35SearchProvi\

8 der" url="http://localhost:8080/solr" shareCores="true" facetLocalizedFieldValuesOnl\

9 y="true" commitWithin="10000" maximumBatchSize="50" />

10

11  <add name="LuceneSearchProvider" type="Mediachase.Search.Providers.Lucene.Luce\

12 neSearchProvider, Mediachase.Search.LuceneSearchProvider" queryBuilderType="Mediacha\

13 se.Search.Providers.Lucene.LuceneSearchQueryBuilder, Mediachase.Search.LuceneSearchP\

14 rovider" storage="<path-to-index-folder>" simulateFaceting="true" />

15

16  <add name="FindSearchProvider" type="EPiServer.Commerce.FindSearchProvider.Fin\

17 dSearchProvider, EPiServer.Commerce.FindSearchProvider" serviceUrl="http://<find-ind\

18 ex-url>" defaultIndex="myindex" />

19  </providers>

20  </SearchProviders>

21  <Indexers basePath="<path-to-index-folder>">

22  <add name="catalog" type="Mediachase.Search.Extensions.Indexers.CatalogIndexBuil\

23 der, Mediachase.Search.Extensions" />

24 </Indexers>

25 </Mediachase.Search>

    There are a couple of things you want to care about:

    · The defaultProvider - this is the search provider you're going to use. The required at-
      tribute is the type, because Mediachase.Search needs to create an instance of it, other

                         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search                                                            198

  attributes then will be used by the search provider. The configured type must inherit from
  Mediachase.Search.SearchProvider.

· The basePath - this is the path to store the indices, it's only used by file-based indexers, in most
  of the case cases, LuceneSearchProvider. HTTP-based indexers, such as Solr35SearchProvider
  or FindSearchProvider, do not care about this setting. There one thing to remember if you are
  using file-based indexers: make sure the paths in both Mediachase.Search.config point to
  same directory, and both AppPool for both frontend site and Commerce Manage have correct
  access to this folder. They should have at least read access, and if you want to index on that
  site, you would need to give delete/write access.

· The type of Indexer. This is where you register how your providers index the entries, it
  must implement ISearchIndexBuilder interface. For the first party LuceneSearchProvider and
  Solr35SearchProvider, it's CatalogIndexBuilder, which inherits from BaseCatalogIndexBuilder.
  Normally if you want to implement your own Indexer, this class will be very important.

When the indexing is performed, BaseCatalogIndexBuilder calls to this method

1 protected virtual int IndexCatalogEntryDto(IndexBuilder indexer, CatalogEntryDto.Cat\
2 alogEntryRow entryRow, string defaultCurrency, string[] languages)

    IndexCatalogEntryDto creates an ISearchDocument from the entryRow, and then finally call to this
    method in the provider:

1 public override void Index(string applicationName, string scope, ISearchDocument doc\
2 ument)

    FindSearchProvider works a bit different, it registers FindSearchIndexBuilder, which overrides
    IndexCatalogEntryDto directly to create ISearchDocument in its own way, but at the end it also
    calls to SearchProvider.Index to do the index.

    override only BuildIndex and UpdateIndex. Or you can override only how each entry is indexed,
    by overriding IndexCatalogEntryDto.

· Register your implementations as the defaultSearchProvider and the indexer.

Demystifying the index processor

With proper configuration, Episerver Commerce will index the changed entries automatically,
without your manual interactions. To allow this, Commerce relies on the ChangeNotification
concept from Episerver Framework. It's a database-backed queue. When one or more entries are
updated, Commerce will send those updates to the queue. That also happens when a node is updated,
a node-entry relation or a catalog association is updated, all related entries will be sent to the queue.

                 If you wonder what is actually sent to the queue - it's the code of the entry. As we learned
                 from Part 1, Code is unique across the system and can be used to identify the entry.

Meanwhile, Commerce has one class registered to process such change - CatalogEntryChangeProcessor.
ChangeNotificationManager will call CatalogEntryChangeProcessor when it finds anything in the
queue that CatalogEntryChangeProcessor registered to. By default, every 5 seconds (a "heartbeat"),
ChangeNotificationManager will get remaining items from the queues (It will get the Code, then use
ICatalogSystem to get the CatalogEntryDto) and call the processors to process those.

There are several statuses of the processor:

   · Valid: the processor is working and it'll update the indices when there are changes. This is of
      course the status you want to see.

   · Invalid: This is the status when you click on Invalidate button. That means you don't want the
      processor to continue processing the queue. You'll be able to build the index manually.

   · Recovering: It's usually when the status of the processor when you click "Recover" when it was
      "Invalid". The processor is trying to recover consistency - and by that does mean it is rebuilding
      the index.

                    This can take quite long time, depending on your catalogs
                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search                                                                          202

· Closed: This is the status you don't want to see - normally it means something went totally
  wrong, and the processor is no longer to process the queue. Your only option is usually to
  restart your site.

· Opening: This usually happens at time the site starts, it's so fast that you hardly see the
  processor in this state.

The automatic indexing processor is a great feature, well, at least in theory. It works really well if you
have small, frequent updates. Not so much if you have big chunk of updates, frequently. However, it
comes with a fairly big con - it's not that stable. It is possible to become Closed once in a while, and
no really knows what is wrong ². As I said, it requires a site restart to make it works again. Your
better/safer option is to use the incremental indexing. If you have small, frequent updates, you can
set up the "Incremental indexing job" to run automatically every 5, 10, or 15 minutes.

One of the reason that you might consider the eventual indexing is that is faster. The
incremental indexing can be slow in specific scenarios. Performance-wise, it can be quite
problematic if you have a big catalog, many relations between node and entries, and a very
big log - time to get the . Worry no more, Commerce 11.4 comes with a new queuing system,
which would make that problem goes away.

Search by Search API:s

In previous section we talked about the index-based search, which should be great choice for
implementing search features. But SearchProviders are not without limitations: they are not real-
time, and they are read-only. When you need to update data, or if you need strictly up-to-date
information, you might need to Search API:s

Episerver Commerce provides a SQL-based API:s for you to search for entries which match a certain
criteria. They works in real-time without any delay like the solution provided by Search Providers,
one data is committed, it is immediately searchable.

But they are not that fast. Because they hit database every time, and in many, to query on a large
set of data. Search API:s are mostly used in Commerce Manager, and you can use them for some
batching updates, but they definitely should not be used in any customer-facing features. For such
features, use Search providers instead.

                 I would even say that if you are building search features that end users can access via Search
                 API:s, you are probably doing it wrong.

   ²I feel the obligation to tell you this: No I don't like having that problem hanging around version after version. But sometimes it's something

you have to accept the reality. There are "Heisenbug" that you can't really trace it, debug it, or fix it. The priority is not just there. I tried, but
I just didn't succeed.

                    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 11: Search  203

                                  Catalog Batch Update is one example of how Search API:s can be used

    Search API:s are built around CatalogSearchParameters and CatalogSearchOptions. Here's the
    simplest example of using Search API:s

1 CatalogSearchOptions options = new CatalogSearchOptions();
2 CatalogSearchParameters searchParams = new CatalogSearchParameters();
3 int totalCount = 0;
4 CatalogEntryDto entryDto = catalogSystem.FindItemsDto(searchParams, options, ref tot\
5 alCount);

    In case you don't specify CatalogSearchOptions.RecordsToRetrieve, entryDto will contain every
    entry in the system - and you don't want to use such code in your production system. A more
    realistic approach is to iterate over variations, so we can process in batches. This is a snippet to load
    and process every 200 entries per batch:

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 11: Search                                                                               204

1 CatalogSearchOptions options = new CatalogSearchOptions();

2 CatalogSearchParameters searchParams = new CatalogSearchParameters();

3 int totalCount = 0;

4 options.Classes.Add("Variant");

5 //We don't want to get any entry, we only need the totalCount

6 options.RecordsToRetrieve = 0;

7 catalogSystem.FindItemsDto(searchParams, options, ref totalCount);

8 int recordsCount = 0;

9 while (totalCount > 0 && recordsCount < totalCount)

10 {

11    options.RecordsToRetrieve = 200;

12    options.StartingRecord = recordsCount;

13    var catalogEntriesDto = CatalogContext.Current.FindItemsDto(searchParams, option\

14 s, ref totalCount, new CatalogEntryResponseGroup(CatalogEntryResponseGroup.ResponseG\

15 roup.Info));

16    //Do some processing here

17    recordsCount += 200;

18 }

         There is no "best" value for batch size. Speaking from experience, you can choose anything
         between 100 and 500 for batch size. Smaller or bigger sizes can have negative impacts on
         performance, but your mileage might vary.

    CatalogSearchOptions contains settings of how the search should behave - which classes to search
    for, how many rows should be returned, which is the start row to get, or should the cache
    result be cached. CatalogSearchOptions inherits from SearchOptions with the Namespace is set to
    "Mediachase.Commerce.Catalog":

2     {

5        /// </summary>

10       /// </summary>

12

13       /// <summary>

14       /// The start record to return.

38     ...

39  }

    CatalogSearchParameters contains some filter you can apply to your search:

2   {

3      /// <summary>

    If you recall about CatalogEntrySearchCriteria, you can see the similarity here - same properties
    for filtering results. Personally I think this is a place where Mediachase eCF got it right - the
    consistency in API:s is very important to make them easy to remember and use. However, if you
    want to expose the true powers of Search API:s, you would have to look into the base class -
    SearchParameters.

1 public abstract class SearchParameters

2   {

3      /// <summary>

4      /// Gets or sets the type of the join used in the query.

5      /// The example types are: inner join, outer join, left join and right join.

6      /// </summary>

7      public string JoinType { get; set; }

8

9      /// <summary>

10     /// Gets or sets the join source table. This value has to be a table name th\

11 at exists in original query.

12     /// </summary>

13     public string JoinSourceTable { get; set; }

14

15     /// <summary>

16     /// Gets or sets the join target query. This can either be a table name or a\

17 complete query.

18     /// </summary>

19     public string JoinTargetQuery { get; set; }

20

21     /// <summary>

22     /// Gets or sets the join source table key.

23     /// </summary>

24     public string JoinSourceTableKey { get; set; }

25

26     /// <summary>

                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Chapter 11: Search                                          207

27     /// Gets or sets the join target table key.

28     /// </summary>

29     public string JoinTargetTableKey { get; set; }

30

31     /// <summary>

32     /// Gets or sets the SQL where clause. You can specify filter conditions aga\

33 inst the tables returned by the query.

34     /// </summary>

35     public string SqlWhereClause { get; set; }

36

37     /// <summary>

38     /// Gets or sets the SQL meta where clause.

39     /// </summary>

40     public string SqlMetaWhereClause { get; set; }

41

42     /// <summary>

43     /// Gets or sets the keyword text search phrase.

44     /// </summary>

45     public string KeywordSearchPhrase { get; set; }

46

47     /// <summary>
