16 axLength, bool isUnique)
17 public MetaField CreateEmail(string name, string friendlyName, bool isNullable, int \
18 maxLength, bool isUnique)
19 ...

     The final step is to save the builder and then save the entire scope.
     Simple, right?

The pipeline

Business Foundation is the only place in Episerver Commerce where everything works in a
"pipeline", so you can inject your custom processing to an operation. While you can do more or
less the same with, for example content system via events, but they are not the same thing.

Customer Contact

Probably the most used Business Object of all - customer contact receives special treatments - it has
it own predefined class - CustomerContact. At its core, CustomerContact is still EntityObject, and

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Chapter 14: Business Foundation overview  283

it's still managed by BusinessManager, but it's important enough for Episerver to provider some
convenient methods to make CustomerContact handling easier.

                                                 Creating a new contact in Commerce Manager

    CustomerContact extends EntityObject by adding a bunch of properties, such as FirstName,
    LastName, or Email, etc. It also has two collections, ContactAddresses and ContactCreditCards,
    which contains the contact's addresses and credit cards, respectively.
    CustomerContact is handled by CustomerContext - which can be used to get CustomerContact(s) or
    Organization(s). For example:

1 var customerContact = CustomerContext.Current.GetContactById(contactId);
2 customerContact.PreferredLanguage = "en-US";
3 customerContact.SaveChanges();

    The full list of methods and properties of CustomerContext can be found here³²

  Customer Address

    Almost always tied to Customer Contact, Customer Address is something that worth your attention.
    There are two addresses that special enough - PreferredShippingAddress and PreferredBillingAddress
    Episerver Commerce has multiple methods to convert a customer address to an order address, and
    vice versa. This is very convenient when a registered user wants to check out an order:

         ³²http://world.episerver.com/documentation/Class-library/?documentId=commerce/9/21F44EB1

                                                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   Chapter 14: Business Foundation overview                                          284

1 var customerAddresses = CustomerContext.Current.CurrentContact.CustomerAddresses;

2 foreach(var customerAddress in customerAddresses)

3{

4   var orderAddress = new OrderAddress();

5   CustomerAddress.CopyCustomerAddressToOrderAddress(customerAddress, orderAddress);

6   order.OrderAddresses.Add(orderAddress);

7}

   And when a customer decides to register for an account after placing an order:

1 var customerAddresses = CustomerContext.Current.CurrentContact.CustomerAddresses;

2 foreach(var orderAddress in order.OrderAddresses)

3{

4   var customerAddress = new AddressEntity();

5   OrderAddress.CopyCustomerAddressToOrderAddress(orderAddress, customerAddress);

6   customerAddresses.Add(customerAddress);

7}

   And of course we can set PreferredBillingAddress and PreferredShippingAddress for the
   CustomerContact.

   Performance optimization

   One of core concepts in Business Foundation is "dynamic". The table to store data, and the queries
   to access them are built dynamically. That approach allows great flexibility, but it comes at a cost:
   because everything is dynamic, it's hard for the system to put optimizations when that's needed.
   That's when you have to step up and do the work, because only you know which fields are frequently
   accessed.

   An example is for CustomerContact - it's not uncommon for Swedish sites to store "person number"
   as a field there. (If you wonder what "person number" is, then it's like US's Social Security Number,
   just much less "secret". This number is built on your birthday and some unique number added to
   identify you, it is used across systems.). In such case, because customers are frequently accessed via
   their person number, it would make sense to add an index for that column in cls_Contact table (I
   would go further and say a clustered index might be relevant here). The framework does not know
   about this column and how it's used, but because you know, you can add such indexes to improve
   performance.

   It's worth noting that adding indexes are not without drawback. While it speeds up queries, it slows
   down insert/update/delete, so make sure to proceed with cautions. Careful profiling should be done
   to make sure you are adding the most effective indexes, and nothing is useless.

    Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Part 8: Extending the system.

Episerver Commerce is not just a software. It IS a framework. Out of the box, Episerver Commerce
works well in basic scenarios (and it should). But by adding magic to your implementation, you
might want to talk with a CRM system to manage customer information, or to use a PIM to update
catalog, or to get your ERP to handle the orders for you. Good news: Episerver Commerce allows that.
Most of the important parts of the system can be replaced/overriden with your implementations.
    Plug your implementations -
    Episerver Framework way.

    There are many ways to plug your implementations into Episerver Commerce. Mediachase eCF has
    its ways, but we'll be talking about the method provided by Episerver Framework, first.

    Extending Episerver framework by plugging in your implementation via dependency injection is a
    well known, well documented technique. So this should be the preferred way - if you can do it this
    way, then you should (do it this way).

    There are two initialization modules from Commerce you might want to care about - the CommerceInitialization
    from Mediachase.Commerce.Initialization, and InitializationModule, from EPiServer.Commerce.Initialization.
    The latter depends on the former (so it's guaranteed to run after). Why should you care?
    CommerceInitialization will handle almost every dependencies with core stuffs, such as ICatalogSystem,
    IInventoryService, IMarketService, etc. InitializationModule will handle the stuffs related to
    catalog content and routing. You will know which module to depend on (of course if you depend on
    InitializationModule, you will automatically depend on CommerceInitialization as well).

    However, their names do not tell the entire truth, in facts, they inherit from IConfigurableModule,
    which itself inherits from IInitializationModule

    Here's how you can implement your IConfigurableModule:

1 [ModuleDependency(typeof(EPiServer.Commerce.Initialization.InitializationModule))]

2 public class InitializationModule : IConfigurableModule

3{

4   public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

5   {

6      //Do your stuffs here, such as register for events.

7   }

8

9   public void Uninitialize(InitializationEngine context)

10  {

11     //Undo your stuffs, such as unregister the events.

12  }

13

14  public void ConfigureContainer(ServiceConfigurationContext context)

15  {

16     //Configure the dependencies

17     context.Services.AddSingleton<ICurrentMarket, MarketStorage>();
    Plug your implementations - Episerver Framework way.  287

18    }

19 }

    In the example above, we add MarketStorage as the singleton instance for ICurrentMarket, that
    means if you call this in your code:

1 var currentMarketService = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<ICurrentMarket>();

    You'll get the same instance of MarketStorage every time. AddSingleton is just one of 5 ways to
    register the life-cycle of your dependencies - ServiceConfigurationProviderExtensions provides
    plenty extensions methods for IServiceConfigurationProvider, but in short you can choose
    between:

        · AddSingleton<TService, TImplementation>, as above
        · AddTransient<TService, TImplementation>: new instance of TImplementation will be created

          every time TService is requested.
        · AddScoped<TService, TImplementation>: registers the dependency to be "hybrid" - If Http-

          Context is available, then the life-cycle of TImplementation is per-HttpContext, otherwise it's
          per-thread when TService is requested.
        · AddHttpContextScoped<TService, TImplementation>: new instance of TImplementation will
          be created for every HttpRequest.
        · AddAddHttpContextOrThreadScoped: this should be equivalent with AddScoped.

    Remember to choose the life-cycle widely, especially when you class has states stored. Having wrong
    life-cycle can messed up with states and can cause nasty problems, especially in concurrent cases.

    The second way to register dependencies is to use ServiceConfigurationAttribute, for example:

1 [ServiceConfiguration(typeof(ApplicationContext), Lifecycle = ServiceInstanceScope.S\
2 ingleton)]
3 [ServiceConfiguration(typeof(IApplicationContext), FactoryMember = "Instance")] // i\
4 nstance (otherwise the container will keep one singleton for each interface/type)
5 public class ApplicationContext : IApplicationContext
6{
7}

    Here the list of scope you can choose from (There are other scopes, such as HybridHttpSession,
    however they will be obsolete soon):

         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Plug your implementations - Episerver Framework way.                288

1 public enum ServiceInstanceScope

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// New instance per usage. Equivalent to AddTransient.

5     /// </summary>

6     Transient,

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// One instance per container. Equivalent to AddSingleton.

10    /// </summary>

11    Singleton,

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// New instance per request. Equivalent to Add

15    /// </summary>

16    HttpContext,

17

18    /// <summary>

19    /// New instance per thread or request. Equivalent to AddScoped.

20    /// </summary>

21    Hybrid,

22 }

    Normally, you should use ServiceConfigurationAttribute to register your dependencies, it's more
    convenient way. However, there are cases when you need to use IConfigurableModule instead: to
    control the order of dependencies registration. If you want to override a certain implementation of
    a system interface, and you know it is registered in CommerceInitialization, for example - you can
    make your IConfigurableModule depends on that module, therefore your dependency registration
    will be guaranteed to run after the system one. In the world of DI, the later registration wins.

    There are two notable IConfigurableModule you should know about Episerver Commerce:

      · CommerceInitialization in Mediachase.Commerce namespace. This can be considered as the
        "main" module where almost important stuffs from Commerce are registered.

      · InitializationModule in EPiServer.Commerce.Initialization. This is where content and rout-
        ing stuffs are registered.

    Unless you have strong reasons to have dependency on CommerceInitialization, it's usually
    safer to make your IConfigurableModule depends on InitializationModule to make sure every
    Commerce registrations have been done. It's is important to note that there is no guarantee about
    the order of execution for two modules which have dependency on same other module. So if your
    module depends on CommerceInitialization, it's undetermined that it will run before or after
    InitializationModule.

                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Plug your implementations - Episerver Framework way.  289

OK that's enough of theoretical information. Let's get our hands dirty with some code.

ICurrentMarket is a small interface with only two methods: to get and to set the current market. The
class MarketStorage in Enoteca and Commerce Sample site simply allows you set current market
(selected from a dropdown) to store in cookie, and load it back. In real world, you might not want
customers to do that, the market must be automatically assigned from customers GeoIP (customers
might be able to select the market afterward, but the market is assigned to them for the first time
they visit)

Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   The unusual ways.

   As mentioned in previous chapters, Mediachase eCF has its own ways to extend the framework. It's
   not as unified or easy to understand as the way Episerver Framework provides, but it still does its
   job when you needs it. We will discuss case by case to see how you can extend the framework - but
   a word of warning - they are less known and not so well documented features, so only use them
   when you don't have any other better way.

   Customize order processing

   If you are using a newer version of Commerce when the abstraction APIs are out of Beta, you should
   be using them for customize how orders (including carts, payment plans and purchase orders) are
   handled. But that's not always the case. You might be using an older version (you should really
   upgrade, and I mean it), or you might not have the time and resource to move to the new abstraction
   APIs. Is plugging custom processing code impossible? Not quite. In facts, it's quite easy to do so, with
   the support from the order configuration system.

   Let's assume you want to do extra thing whenever you save a purchase order. You might want to
   call an external API:s to update some information, for example.

   Let's first create your custom class:

1 public class MyPurchaseOrder : PurchaseOrder

2{

3   public override void AcceptChanges()

4   {

5      //Your fancy code goes here.

6      base.AcceptChanges();

7   }

8}

   You can even remove base.AcceptChanges(). That means you don't want to save the order into
   database in normal way. You might save it to your custom persistent backend, a document database
   might be. Note that if you goes that way, the static methods in PurchaseOrder class to search and
   load for orders, such as LoadByCustomer(Guid CustomerId) will no longer works and some features
   depend on it, like the OrderManagement in Commerce Manager, will break (unless you go even
   further and extend them as well). You're on your very own now.

   And now the magic parts: To let the system know that you want to use MyPurchaseOrder instead of
   PurchaseOrder. Just go to Configs\ecf.order.config, and update the line in MappedTypes/Purchase-
   OrderType with your new type, note that it must be the fullname of the type:
The unusual ways.  291

1 <PurchaseOrderType name="MySite.Order.Classes.MyPurchaseOrder, MySite" />

    Now build it and you're done. Try to place an order and see the magic happens.
    The above step applies for front-end site. If you want to use it in Commerce Manager as well, make
    sure to deploy your assembly to its bin folder, and update the its config file as well.
    This method also works with other metaclasses, such as Cart or PaymentPlan, and even OrderForm
    and LineItem. In the end, you have the power all over the order system!

                     Again, this is only a workaround. It is supported, but no longer documented or recom-
                     mended. The best way to customize the order system is to use the new abstraction APIs and
                     plug your implementation there. You get better APIs, new features and bug fixes. It's the
                     way forward.

Work with "workflows".

No I lied, at least partly. It's not the workflows anymore. Episerver Commerce has a special, open-
sourced project (Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow) ³³, which was based on Windows Workflow
Foundation (WF) 3.5 in Commerce 8 and earlier. In Commerce 9, the engine has been rewritten
to remove the dependencies on WF, and the "workflow" has now been renamed to ActivityFlow,
to indicate that it now longer depends on WF. Still, the term workflow is more familiar and we will
use that term in this book.

The reason Episerver Commerce open-sources Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow is to allow you
to customize the workflows to fit with your business requirements. The choice is yours - either
attach the project into your solution and modify it directly, or inject your "activity" into the existing
workflows replace the default one.³

How workflows are registered

If you worked with Windows Workflow Foundation before, you'll probably know about XOML
(Extensible Object Markup Language) files. They were used to define the workflows in Commerce
8 and prior, and here's how an XOML would look like

   ³³Can be downloaded from here
   ³It was a known limitation with Commerce 8 and prior that if you reference to the workflow assembly, you will have to stick with

EPiServer.Framework package which your EPiServer.Commerce.Core package was built with . This was caused by the build engine of WF as it
skips the assembly binding configured in web.config. Such limitation was removed in Commerce 9. One more reason to upgrade your solution
to the latest Commerce version and stay on top of your game?

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    The unusual ways.                                                   292

1 <SequentialWorkflowActivity x:Class="Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow.CartCheckoutWorkfl\

2 ow" x:Name="CartCheckoutWorkflow" xmlns:ns0="clr-namespace:Mediachase.Commerce.Workf\

3 low.Activities" xmlns:ns1="clr-namespace:Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow.Activities.Car\

4 t" xmlns:ns2="clr-namespace:Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow.Activities.PurchaseOrderAct\

5 ivities" xmlns:x="http://schemas.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml" xmlns="http://schema\

6 s.microsoft.com/winfx/2006/xaml/workflow">

7 <IfElseActivity x:Name="ifElseActivity1">

8     <IfElseBranchActivity x:Name="ifElseBranchActivity1">

9                      <IfElseBranchActivity.Condition>

10                     <CodeCondition Condition="RunProcessPayment" />

11                     </IfElseBranchActivity.Condition>

12                     <ns1:ProcessPaymentActivity Warnings="{x:Null}" OrderGroup="{ActivityBind CartChec\

13 koutWorkflow,Path=OrderGroup}" Payment="{x:Null}" x:Name="processPaymentActivity1" /\

14 >

15    </IfElseBranchActivity>

16 </IfElseActivity>

17 <ns0:CalculateTotalsActivity x:Name="calculateTotalsActivity1" OrderGroup="{Activity\

18 Bind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path=OrderGroup}" Warnings="{x:Null}" />

19 <ns1:AdjustInventoryActivity Description="Adjusts Inventory." x:Name="adjustInventor\

20 yActivity1" OrderGroup="{ActivityBind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path=OrderGroup}" Warning\

21 s="{ActivityBind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path=Warnings}" />

22 <ns1:RecordPromotionUsageActivity UsageStatus="Used" Warnings="{x:Null}" OrderGroup=\

23 "{ActivityBind CartCheckoutWorkflow,Path=OrderGroup}" x:Name="recordPromotionUsageAc\

24 tivity1" />

25 </SequentialWorkflowActivity>

    You might guess - this is the CartCheckout workflow, and it has a quite simple logic. If the conditon
    RunProcessPayment is true, then run ProcessPaymentActivity. Then run CalculateTotalsActivity
    and AdjustInventoryActivity. And finally RecordPromotionUsageActivity.

    Not too complicated, right? But there are several major drawbacks:

      · It's overly complicated for a simple logic. If it was written in C#, it would be on 4-5 lines of
        code.

      · It's hard to know an activity belongs to which workflows. You can only search, instead of using
        the Go to implementation or Find all references provided by Visual Studio.

      · It's hard to edit and even worse, hard to debug. The workflows had always puzzled me when
        it came to breakpoints and so.

    Lucky for us (myself included), Commerce 9 removed the dependencies of Windows Workflow
    Foundation, and moved to a new, almost "POCO" (Plain Old C# Object) approach. Here's how the
    above activity looks like now:

                       Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    The unusual ways.                                                  293

1 using Mediachase.Commerce.Engine;

2 using Mediachase.Commerce.Orders.Managers;

3 using Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow.Activities;

4 using Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow.Activities.Cart;

5

6 namespace Mediachase.Commerce.Workflow

7{

8     /// <summary>

9     /// This class represents the Cart Checkout workflow

10    /// </summary>

11    [ActivityFlowConfiguration(Name = OrderGroupWorkflowManager.CartCheckOutWorkflow\

12 Name)]

13    public class CartCheckoutActivityFlow : ActivityFlow

14    {

15         /// <inheritdoc />

16         public override ActivityFlowRunner Configure(ActivityFlowRunner activityFlow)

17         {

18            return activityFlow.If(() => ShouldProcessPayment())

19                             .Do<ProcessPaymentActivity>()

20                             .EndIf()

21                             .Do<CalculateTotalsActivity>()

22                             .Do<AdjustInventoryActivity>()

23                             .Do<RecordPromotionUsageActivity>();

24         }

25    }

26 }

    Tell me how long did it take you to understand what it intends to do? I even included the using
    statements and the doc comments and it's still shorter than the XOML version. We can now see
    where an activity is used easily by features provided by Visual Studio, and you can debug without
    sweating and grinning. Life just got easier!

    OK, so let's break down how the new "workflows" are registered.

    At first, it's supposed to be inherited from ActivityFlow (as we mentioned earlier, it's the way
    Episerver used to distinct this from Workflow term - so we know it's the new engine). By convention,
    the class will have the suffix -ActivityFlow, you are free to choose otherwise, but it's easier to stick
    with the pattern.

    The class must be decorated with an ActivityFlowConfiguration attribute. It has two important
    properties:

      · Name, which is mandatory. In this example, the name of the workflow is "CartCheckout", which
        is supposed to be a system workflow.

                               Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   The unusual ways.                                           294

      · Priority, which is optional. In case there are multiple wokflows with a same name, the
         workflow with highest priority will take effect. By default, priority = 0, then any priority higher
         than take will take effect.

   So if you want to have your own CartCheckout but don't want to mess with the default one, it's
   simple as this:

1  /// <summary>

2  /// This class represents the Cart Checkout workflow

3  /// </summary>

4  [ActivityFlowConfiguration(Name = OrderGroupWorkflowManager.CartCheckOutWorkflow\

5 Name, Priority=10)]

6  public class MyCartCheckoutActivityFlow : ActivityFlow

7  {

8  }

   And finally, a method to configure how the workflow acts:

1 public override ActivityFlowRunner Configure(ActivityFlowRunner activityFlow)
2{
3}

    You can use a fluent API:s on activityFlow to configure the condition an action, with three basic
    elements: If(Func<bool>), EndIf() and Do<T>(). If will be the conditional branching, while Do<T>
    will execute an activity.

    There is another attribute for ActivityFlowConfiguration you might need to know about -
    AvailableInBetaMode. Only workflows decorated with this attribute are available in BETA mode
    (VNextFeatures enabled). However, at the time this book completes, I would expect this attribute to
    be removed (along with all BETA tags in the API:s)

    If you have customer workflows from Commerce 8, make sure to check out the workflow migration
    tool³. It's pretty cool and will convert your workflow project into new format in (almost) just one
    click!

    Let's go through an example on how you can customize the workflow project. Normally, when a
    customer adds items to the cart, the system will have to check the inventory to see if there are
    enough goods in the store, and then when he or she actually places an order, the system will have to
    adjust the quantities. However, this is not necessary if your website only sells downloadable games.
    You can remove those steps altogether for simpler processing.

    There are three "Activity" which relate to such actions:

        · GetFulfillmentWarehouseActivity to get the warehouse which can fulfill the order.

         ³http://world.episerver.com/download/Items/EPiServer-Commerce/episerver-commerce-9-tools-and-code-samples/

                       Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    The unusual ways.                                                              295

    · CheckInstoreInventoryActivity or CheckInventoryActivity to check if the inventory can
      fulfill the order.

    · AdjustInventoryActivity to actually adjust the inventory according to purchasing number.

    Also, ActivityFlow has a property named ShouldAdjustInventory, to indicate if the activity flow
    should adjust the inventory. Because in this case it's always false, to we will remove it as well.

    We can start by removing above activities form the project. Because some ActivityFlows use them,
    they need to be updated as well. For example, here's how PORecalculateActivityFlow looks like
    before

1   public override ActivityFlowRunner Configure(ActivityFlowRunner activityFlow)

2   {

3      return activityFlow.Do<ValidateLineItemsActivity>()

4                      .Do<GetFulfillmentWarehouseActivity>()

5                      .If(() => ShouldAdjustInventory())

6                      .If(() => ShouldCheckInstoreInventory())

7                      .Do<CheckInstoreInventoryActivity>()

8                      .Else()

9                      .Do<CheckInventoryActivity>()

10                     .EndIf()

11                     .EndIf()

12                     .If(() => ShouldAdjustInventory())

13                     .Do<AdjustInventoryActivity>()

14                     .EndIf()

15                     .If(() => ShouldRecalculateOrder())

16                     .Do<ProcessShipmentsActivity>()

17                     .Do<RemoveDiscountsActivity>()

18                     .Do<CalculateTotalsActivity>()

19                     .Do<CalculateDiscountsActivity>()

20                     .Do<CalculateTotalsActivity>()

21                     .Do<CalculateTaxActivity>()

22                     .Do<CalculateTotalsActivity>()

23                     .EndIf()

24                     .Do<CalculatePurchaseOrderStatusActivity>();

25  }

    And after:

                       Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    The unusual ways.                                                              296

1   public override ActivityFlowRunner Configure(ActivityFlowRunner activityFlow)

2   {

3      return

4      activityFlow.Do<ValidateLineItemsActivity>()

5              .If(() => ShouldRecalculateOrder())

6                      .Do<ProcessShipmentsActivity>()

7                      .Do<RemoveDiscountsActivity>()

8                      .Do<CalculateTotalsActivity>()

9                      .Do<CalculateDiscountsActivity>()

10                     .Do<CalculateTotalsActivity>()

11                     .Do<CalculateTaxActivity>()

12                     .Do<CalculateTotalsActivity>()

13             .EndIf()

14             .Do<CalculatePurchaseOrderStatusActivity>();

15  }

    If you are using the new promotion system - aka having VNextFeatures flag enabled, it is important
    to enable AvailableInBetaMode when you register your ActivityFlow:

1   [ActivityFlowConfiguration(Name = "CartCheckout", Priority=10, AvailableInBetaMo\

2 de = true)]

3   public class MyCartCheckoutActivityFlow : ActivityFlow

4   {

5   }

    Otherwise, your customized ActivityFlow will not be available and the default one will be used.

    Workflows optional parameters

    As we can see above, you can use If-Else statements to control workflows. Instead of having two
    or more worflows, you can have only one, and when you call it, depending on the conditions, you
    might want the flow to act differently. This can be done by passing one or more optional parameters
    to the workflow.

    To define a parameter in a workflow, simply add a property with a special attribute to it:

                         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   The unusual ways.                                          297

1  [ActivityFlowContextProperty]

2  public virtual bool SkipRecalculateOrder { get; set; }

3

4  public bool ShouldRecalculateOrder()

5  {

6     return !SkipRecalculateOrder;

7  }

   By default, this property has value of false, therefore ShouldRecalculateOrder return true and the
   acitivities will be executed. If we want to skip recalculating order, we need to set the flag to true, by
   sending a Dictionary<string, object> to the workflow:

1  var param = new Dictionary<string, object>();

2  param.Add("SkipRecalculateOrder", true);

3  OrderGroupWorkflowManager.RunWorkflow(purchaseOrder, OrderGroupWorkflowManager.P\

4 ORecalculateWorkflowName, false, param);

   If your workflow has more than one flag, simply add additional parameters to the dictionary param
   before calling the workflow.

                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   The legacy continues

   Coming from a long history even before the acquisition, Commerce Manager can be considered as
   the legacy part of Episerver Commerce. It might not have the shiny, responsive design with widgets
   like the new Catalog UI, but it does it job quite well. Some of its users even liked it, for it efficiency.
   Truth be told, Commerce Manager has not been actively developed. Important bugs are fixed, but
   no new features will be added. With the dawn of ASP.NET core (Formerly ASP.NET 5), which does
   not support WebForms, the foreseen future of Commerce Manager will be clinging on life-support,
   and eventually phrased out. It has had it days.

   But that will not happen anytime soon. Commerce Manager will continue to run, even silently in
   hundreds of implementations of Episerver Commerce. It's not dead website. And its architecture,
   which might be confusing at first, allow you to extend the functionalities when you need. It might
   not be the most customizable, or powerful framework out there, but it does it job.

   Let's us look in a specific case as I get from a question in Episerver World forums: How to add the
   wish-list feature to the Order management system of Commerce Manager. We will go through the
   steps needed and also examine the structure of Commerce Manager.

   At first, we have to add a new menu to the navigation tree of Commerce Manager. Commerce Man-
   ager define that tree by multiple parts, each part represents one of the main features of the system
   (Customer, Catalog, Order and so on). Because we're looking to add wish-list menu, it should be in
   the Order subsystem. We then should edit Apps\Order\Config\View\Navigation\LeftMenu.xml.

   Don't let its content scare you. It can be confusing at first, but in the end, you only need to
   replicate the Carts menu. This can be added right after Order_Carts Link in <Navigation>/<Tabs
   id="mainTag">/<add> section:

1 <Link id="Order_Wishlist" text="Wishlists" order="45" iconUrl="" iconCss="treeIcon" \

2 type="Carts" command="cmdWishlists"

3  permissions="order:mng:view"/>

   And magic happens, we added a menu to the left tree:
   The legacy continues                                                       299

                                                                 Wishlists, woohoo

   Wait a second, we don't have the extra submenus. That the extra benefit from TreeSource.aspx. We
   might add those later, but let's keep it simple for now.

   The menu does not really do anything now - we need to add a command for it: <Command
   id="cmdWishlists"> <CommandType>ClientAction</CommandType> <ClientScript>CSManagementClient.Change
   `Wishlist-List', `')</ClientScript> <EnableHandler type="Mediachase.Commerce.Manager.CommandHandlers.Perm
   Mediachase.ConsoleManager" /> </Command> Node that here, we set the EnableHandler to be
   PermissionEnableHandler. That mean, Commerce Manager will only allow users which granted
   order:mng:view permission to access the view - same as the menu for ShoppingCart does.

   So now it's actually doing something: When you click on it, it will try to load the Wishlist-List.xml
   view (in same folder as Orders-List.xml). So let's go to Apps\Order\Config\View\Grids and add
   a file there. We, again, want to keep it simple, therefore we will just copy the Orders-List.xml file,
   except the last section needs to be changed:

1 <ViewConfig>

2  <setAttributes id="Wishlist-List" isNameDynamic="true" controlUrl="Order/Wishlist.a\

3 scx" permissions="order:mng:view" help="Order+Management"></setAttributes>

   Note the Wishlist.ascx part - it's something we have to implement ourselves. Open your Visual Stu-
   dio and create an empty webform project. You'll need to add reference to Mediachase.WebConsoleLib
   and Mediachase.CommerceManager, then add a new user control like this:

    The code should explain itself. We are loading the shopping carts which has the name of `Wishlist'.
