It's not that hard, right? Extending Commerce Manager means dealing with legacy code, WebForms,
ViewState, IsPostBack, etc. etc.. The old glorious days - but I hope you don't have to deal with too
often.
We barely scratched the surface of Commerce Manager - and there are many other stuffs you can
do. It might not be the most fun/pleasant way to work with - but it can get the job done.

                 Extending Commerce Manager has one caveat you need to know: Everytime you update
                 your Commerce Manager website, your changes are likely to be overwritten by the files from
                 Episerver. Keep them in separated folder and then apply the changes when you upgrade.
                 Also, you are on their own now, the support from Episerver will be pretty limited when you
                 run into problems with extending Commerce Manager, you'll likely have to ask for Expert
                 Services, which costs money.

In the previous example we talked about adding a new view. How's about extending an existing
view? It's even easier, but let's take a look at an example: how to add a new column to the order list
view. Perhaps, adding Track Number column?

Commerce Manager Access rights.

                 Security is a big subject and I highly recommend you to not take it lightly. However, this
                 book is not about security, and I am, sadly, not a security expert. If you are looking to secure
                 your site, follow the best practices, and consult an expert on the topics.

In this section, we will explore how to grant access to specific components in Commerce Manager.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   The legacy continues                                                                                 303

   In a sense, Commerce Manager is highly modularized, access (permission) to a specific function of
   a specific feature can be granted to an specific user or a group.

             In Commerce 8.15 and earlier, the permissions settings (a.k.a Role Management) can be
             handled entirely in Commerce Manager. However, from Commerce 8.16, the roles have been
             moved entirely to CMS. For obvious reasons we will only talk about CMS style permissions.

   If you go to CMS Admin/Config/Security/Permission for Functions, you can see a list of permissions
   which can be set to Commerce Manager's functionalities. These permissions can be granted to
   certain users or groups.

                         By default, users in Administrators and WebAdmins group can edit catalog items

   Granting permissions can be done manually by administrators, or programmatically by code. Let's
   take an example on how it can be done.

   Let's say we want to add permission to the new wishlist view we just added in previous section
   (Normally it would make senses for it to share the same permission as the order view, but we are
   taking it as example). We need to add a permission type for it, and then register that permission type
   for the wishlist view.

   First, let's change the permission for wishlist view from order:mng:view to wishlist:mng:view

1 <Column width="80" allowSorting="true" dataField="OrderGroupId" headingText="{Share\

2 dStrings:ID}" columnType="CustomTemplate" permissions="wishlist:mng:view">

3  <Template id="LinkTemplate" controlUrl="GridTemplates/OrderLinkTemplate.ascx"></\

4 Template>

5 </Column>

   By adding permissions attribute, we are saying that only users or groups with that permission can
   access to that function. We then need to define this new PermissionType, to make that permission
   appear in the Admin view (so it can actually be granted to users):

                         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    The legacy continues                                                 304

1 PermissionType wishlistView = new PermissionType(Permissions.GroupName, "wishlist:mn\
2 g:view");

    Mediachase.Commerce.Permissions.GroupName is a constant defined by Commerce - we'll use it so
    our new permission type will be grouped with other one. Of course, you can define another group
    name for custom permission types. Now the final step is to register this PermissionType into system -
    via PermissionTypeRepository.Register(PermissionType). PermissionTypeRepository.Register
    will return true if the PermissionType was successfully registered, and false if it is already existed -
    so we can put the code either in an IInitializationModule or a MigrationStep:

1 var permissionTypeRepo = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<PermissionTypeRepository\
2 >();
3 permissionTypeRepo.Register(wishlistView);

    And that's it. Our new permission type is now accessible via Admin UI:

                                                   Our new permission type is now available

    It's now possible to add to assign to users or groups to grant access to wishlist.
    But that's not all. The text is not very friendly - so you might want to have a nice description of the
    permission type. Good news - those can be localized - just add this to your language file:

1 <?xml version="1.0" encoding="utf-8" ?>

2 <languages>

3   <language name="English" id="en">

4   <admin>

5   <permissiontype>

6              <groups>

7                <EPiCommerce>

8                         <permissions>

9                         <wishlistmngview>Allows access to view wishlist (wishlist:admin:view\

10 )</wishlistmngview>

11                        </permission>

12               </EPiCommerce>

13             </groups>

14  </permissiontype>

15  </admin>

16  </language>

17 </languages>

                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
The legacy continues                                          305

So basically you will need to add localized strings to of <languages>\<language>\<admin>\<groups>\<EPiCommerce>
The actual element for string would be the name of the permission, without ":" separator. In this
case, because the permission is "wishlist:mng:view", the string would be "wishlistmngview".

And that's it.

Catalog permissions

One of the most frequently asked question on Episerver World Forums is if the catalog contents
support access rights the way CMS contents do. The answer is sadly, no. There are only two level
of access rights for catalog contents: All or nothing. Either you have access to entire catalog system,
or you don't (Strictly speaking, "all" means the access rights affect the whole catalog. There are still
levels of access rights (including read-only, and edit), but it only applies to Catalog Management in
Commerce Manager, not the new Catalog UI.).

The catalog permission handling/access right was actually implemented in Commerce 11.6. The first
iteration was a MVP - minimal viable product - and the handling is limited to catalogs and categories
only. The entries will inherit settings from their true (direct) parents. At this point, there is very few
reason, if at all, for you to use Catalog Management in Commerce Manager. You should use Catalog
UI instead.

                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Payment providers

As we promised in Order system part, we now discuss payment providers - the essential part of any
Commerce sites.

                 There are several ways to call the "payments" - they can be called either Payment providers,
                 Payment methods or Payment gateways. In this section we will use term "Payment
                 providers" or "Payment gateways" for the payments themselves. DIBS, Paypal, for example,
                 are Payment providers. Once we configured them to work with Episerver Commerce, we
                 have "payment methods". In short, payment methods are configured payment providers.
                 This is our terminology in this book, it might or might not be the most correct way in real
                 world.

Episerver provides some payment providers as example, which you can download from here³ (login
required). The sample ones include:

   · DIBS
   · Paypal
   · Datacash

In the past, there was also Klarna payment provider, but it was retired with Klarna released first-
party Klarna payment provider addon for Episerver Commerce.

   ³http://world.episerver.com/download/Items/Episerver-Commerce/episerver-commerce-9-payment-providers/
Payment providers  307

                                     A payment provider configuration in Commerce Manager

Configure payment methods.

A payment provider has plenty configuration - and most of them are rather important. Let's go
through them one by one.

   · The Name and Description are to show to end-customers.
   · System Keyword once set, is readonly and can be changed. This is used by the system to

      identify your payment provider, so it must be unique. Commerce Manager will expect an
      user control (.ascx file) in Apps\Order\Payments\Plugins\<System Keywork> with name of
      ConfigurePayment.ascx - it will load that user control to allow configuring the payment
      provider.
   · Language decides where the payment provider belongs to - as we mentioned before the payment
      providers are categorized by languages, not markets. So if you want to have a payment provider
      for both "English" and "Svenska", you'll have to create two payment providers, with System

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Payment providers  308

  Keyword as "MyPayment-En", and "MyPayment-sv", which are available in "English" and
  "Svenska", respectively.
· Class Name - is the full name of your payment provider class - which implements IPaymentGateway
  interface.
· Payment Class - the class inherits from Payment which your payment provider class supposes
  to return. We will talk more about Class Name and Payment Class in next section.
· Sort Order - this can be used to determine the order of payment providers show to customers,
  for example, you can have "Credit Card", "Cash On Delivery", "Gift Card" - in that order at
  checkout page.
· IsActive shows if the payment is active, however it's not used by other parts of the system.
  You can use this flag to hide a payment method in checkout page - but again it's up to you to
  handle that.
· IsDefault and Support Recurring decides if it is default or if it supports recurring payments
  (to be used for PaymentPlan). Like IsActive, it is not really used by other parts of the system.
· A payment provider can be configured to be used only with certain shipping methods, or in
  certain market.

                                    This payment provider is only available in DEFAULT market

IPaymentGateway

For a payment provider to be available for selection, it must implement IPaymentGateway interface,
which is a quite simple interface:

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Payment providers                                                                 309

1 public interface IPaymentGateway

2{

3       /// <summary>

4       /// Returns the configuration data associated with a gateway.

5       /// Sets the configuration gateway data. This data typically includes

6       /// information like gateway URL, account info and so on.

7       /// </summary>

8       IDictionary<string, string> Settings { get; set;}

9

10      /// <summary>

11      /// Processes the payment. Can be used for both positive and negative transactio\

12 ns.

13      /// </summary>

14      bool ProcessPayment(Payment payment, ref string message);

15 }

    Settings is simply an object for configuration variable, while ProcessPayment is an interesting
    method. It is called both for payment (when customers place order) and refund (when customers
    return items, for example). To determine which action to take, check Payment.TransactionType
    property. Here's the values available (Note that Payment.TransactionType is string type, so you
    will need to convert this enum to string for comparison):

1 public enum TransactionType

2{

3       /// <summary>

4       /// Requests to charge a cardholder.

5       /// An approved Authorization transaction reduces the cardholderâTMs open-to-buy am\

6 ount (the amount that can currently be charged against the account), but does not ac\

7 tually capture the funds.

8       /// </summary>

9       Authorization = 1,

10      /// <summary>

11      /// Used to complete an Authorization transaction that was successfully authorized \

12 through the payment gateway.

13      /// Note: An Authorization and Capture together are considered one complete transac\

14 tion. Once the Capture is submitted, the transaction will be sent for settlement.

15      /// </summary>

16      Capture = 2,

17      /// <summary>

18      /// Charges the specified amount against the account, and marks the transaction for\

19 immediate funds transfer (capture) during the next settlement period.

20      /// </summary>

21      Sale = 4,

                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Payment providers                                               310

22     /// <summary>

23     /// Used to cancel an original transaction that is not yet settled and prevents it \

24 from being sent for settlement.

25     /// </summary>

26     Void = 8,

27     /// <summary>

28     /// Returns the specified amount (posts a credit) to the cardholderâTMs account.

29     /// </summary>

30     Credit = 16,

31     /// <summary>

32     /// Other transaction type.

33     /// </summary>

34     Other = 32,

35     /// <summary>

36     /// Used to capture the remaining balance.

37     /// </summary>

38     /// <remarks>

39     /// In some payment provider, only a single Capture transaction can be submitted\

40 against an Authorization, even if we do not capture the full amount with our Captur\

41 e.

42     /// If there is an available amount remaining, we may use the Capture Only trans\

43 action type to capture the remaining balance.

44     /// </remarks>

45     CaptureOnly = 64

46 }

    ProcessPayment takes a Payment instance - with the certain type as you configred the payment
    provider. Here's the payment types available out-of-box:

       · ExchangePayment
       · InvoicePayment
       · OtherPayment
       · GiftCardPayment
       · CashCardPayment
       · CreditCardPayment

    Of course, you can create your customized types of payment, they just need to implements Payment
    abstract class.

                     Commerce uses reflection to load the payment providers (which are supposed to inherit from
                     Payment class). It is, however, possible that when you try to create or update your payment
                     providers, there are no selection available in the Class Name dropdownlist. If that happens,
                     it usually means there are something wrong with your Commerce Manager bin folder. A
                     dependent assembly might be missing or has wrong version.

                            Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Payment providers                                          311

Manage payment methods programmatically.

In previous section we talked about managing payment providers manually - you can create, update
(some properties, but not all) or delete payment providers in Commerce Manager. Of course that
can be done programmatically through code. The class responsible for that is PaymentManager - and
it will act on the presentation of payment providers in memory PaymenMethodDto.

                 Yes, you can see the pattern here, all data objects from Mediachase era are named "Dto".

  How payments work

    In Part 3 we talked about how to add payment(s) into an OrderGroup (or IOrderGroup). But how
    does it work internally?

    When the customer checks out an order, our payment module - which implements IPaymentOption
    - will be called. IPaymentOption.PreProcess(OrderForm form) is expected to return a Payment
    instance - which can be one of the builtin types (OtherPayment, CreditCardPayment, etc.), or your
    own implementation. Here, your payment provider should set the correct TransactionType for the
    Payment - it can be Sale, Authorization or Transaction.

    We then set the required information to this Payment, and add it to OrderForm.Payments:

1 payment.PaymentMethodId = paymentMethodRow.PaymentMethodId;
2 payment.PaymentMethodName = paymentMethodRow.Name;
3 payment.BillingAddressId = orderForm.BillingAddressId;
4 payment.Amount = orderForm.Total;
5 orderForm.Payments.Add(payment);

    Then we need to process the payments - either via workflow (By CartCheckout workflow, which will
    call ProcessPaymentActivity, or by DefaultPaymentProcessor). The workflow/processor will try to
    create an PaymentGateway from Payment.PaymentMethodId, and then call PaymentGateway.ProcessPayment(Payment,
    ref string).

    PaymentGateway.ProcessPayment is what your payment provider needs to implement. Depends of
    the way your payment provider works, it's important to check for Payment.TransactionType for
    relevant action. For example:

                   Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Payment providers                                                               312

1 public override bool ProcessPayment(Mediachase.Commerce.Orders.Payment payment, ref \

2 string message)

3{

4     if (payment.Parent.Parent is PurchaseOrder)

5     {

6        if (payment.TransactionType == TransactionType.Capture.ToString())

7        {

8           //Capture the amount

9        }

10

11       if (payment.TransactionType == TransactionType.Credit.ToString())

12       {

13          //Refund the amount

14       }

15       message = "TransactionType is not valid";

16       return false;

17    }

18 }

    It's worth noting that Episerver Commerce also supports split payments - if you payment method
    implements ISplitPaymentGateway instead of IPaymentGateway:

1 public interface ISplitPaymentGateway : IPaymentGateway

2{

3     bool ProcessPayment(Payment payment, Shipment shipment, ref string message);

4}

    This method will be called when you process the payment. This allows your customer to pay for
    each Shipment, instead of the whole order at once.

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
  ProfileModule

     Several features of Episerver frameworks in general and Episerver Commerce in particular - are
     provided by IHttpModule implementations. When you need to register an action as soon as the site
     starts - this is probably the best place to be. Here are the default modules in a standard Commerce
     installation:

 1 <modules runAllManagedModulesForAllRequests="true">
 2 <add name="BusinessFoundationInitializeModule" preCondition="managedHandler" type=\
 3 "Mediachase.Commerce.Core.Modules.BusinessFoundationInitializeModule, Mediachase.Com\
 4 merce" />
 5 <add name="InitializationModule" type="EPiServer.Framework.Initialization.Initiali\
 6 zationModule, EPiServer.Framework" preCondition="managedHandler" />
 7 <add name="UrlRewriteModule" type="EPiServer.Web.RoutingUrlRewriteModule, EPiServe\
 8 r" preCondition="managedHandler" />
 9 <add name="ShellRoutingModule" type="EPiServer.Shell.Web.Routing.ShellRoutingModul\
10 e, EPiServer.Shell" />
11 <remove name="WebDAVModule" />
12 <add name="ProfileModule" type="EPiServer.Business.Commerce.HttpModules.ProfileMod\
13 ule, EPiServer.Business.Commerce" />
14 <add name="CatalogNodeModule" type="EPiServer.Business.Commerce.HttpModules.Catalo\
15 gNodeModule, EPiServer.Business.Commerce" />
16 </modules>

                       You can ignore CatalogNodeModule because it's obsoleted and does not really do anything
                       (future release of Commerce will get rid of that). And you should not touch other modules
                       because they provide critical functions in Episerver Framework and Commerce. The (only)
                       one we can change here is the ProfileModule.

     So what we can do with ProfileModule? ProfileModule registers its methods to AuthenticateRequest
     event, so it will kick in when a customer logs in. It will try to assign the last order (placed when
     anonymous) to the current user profile, and merge carts (if not empty) to the existing carts (there
     might be multiple carts in a multi markets set up, and wishlist is also counted as one), if the carts
     are not empty. However, that might not be the behaviors we want, for example, you might need
     the items to be overwritten entirely - so if there was any item in the logged in cart, they will be
     removed. We're going to replace that.

     First, we need to implement IHttpModule. IHttpModule is just a simple ASP.NET interface:
    ProfileModule                                                                                                  314

1 public interface IHttpModule

2{

3     // Summary:

4     //           Disposes of the resources (other than memory) used by the module that imp\

5 lements

6     //           System.Web.IHttpModule.

7     void Dispose();

8

9     // Summary:

10    //           Initializes a module and prepares it to handle requests.

11    void Init(HttpApplication context);

12 }

    Init(HttpApplication context) is the place where we want to register our customer implementa-
    tion, while Dispose can be used for cleaning up garbage - such as de-register the method from the
    event, to avoid any possible memory leaks.

1 public void Init(HttpApplication context)

2{

3     if (context == null)

4     {

5          return;

6     }

7     context.AuthenticateRequest += Application_AuthenticateRequest;

8}

    Now you need to implement your Application_AuthenticateRequest method, and then simply
    replace the registration of ProfileModule in <modules> section with your type - the type value
    should be the full name of your type.

                   Technically it's possible to have both the default and your custom "profile" module. Just keep
                   in mind that the module higher in the list will be processed first, if both of them register
                   to same event. If your custom module also listens to AuthenticateRequest event, think if it
                   should execute before or after ProfileModule.

    Although the replacing of ProfileModule in web.config is quite easy approach, but it's not trivial,
    and it's not inline with the normal approach of Episerver Framework in general. A new approach
    was introduced in Commerce 10.4. It is based on the new IProfileMigrator interface, which is quite
    simple one:

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    ProfileModule                                                       315

1 public interface IProfileMigrator

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Migrates the orders.

5     /// </summary>

6     void MigrateOrders(Guid anonymousId);

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Migrates the carts.

10    /// </summary>

11    void MigrateCarts(Guid anonymousId);

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Migrates the wishlists.

15    /// </summary>

16    void MigrateWishlists(Guid anonymousId);

17 }

    You can simply write your own implemenation, and register it in one of your initialization modules,
    and that's it. By default, the behaviors are:

      · The orders placed by anonymousId will be migrated to the logged in user. If you want to change
        this, implement MigrateOrders(Guid anonymousId)

      · The carts and the wishlists will be merged - the items will be added to the carts and the wishlists
        of the logged in users.

    You might inherit from ProfileMigrator to reuse some of the default behavior and only change the
    one you need. Mind you ProfileMigrator can be considered as internal and can be changed.

                                Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    CustomerContact events

    You might notice the lacking of events in some parts of the system. We have events for catalog
    system, for order system, for prices and inventories changes, but that's not enough. You might want
    to have events - or at least - the ability to know when something happens. For example, when a
    customer contact is changed, or edited, or deleted, it would be very nice to do some extra actions.
    Sending emails, updating external systems, etc.

    Such events are not available out-of-box, so we have to implement our own. How? We don't have
    ICustomerContactService (or something similar) interface where we can write our implementation
    to replace the default service (and even if there is, it would be a big task to do so). So there's no
    "ordinary", framework-way to do that.

    However, CustomerContact is built on Business Foundation system, and BF, at its core, is all above
    extensible and plug-able. We don't have ICustomerContactService interface, but we have IPlugin
    who can do the same, and even more.

    As we learned in previous chapter, CustomerContact is just another EntityObject and all operations
    are still done via BusinessManager.Execute(Request) - even we have some nice wrapper methods
    to make working with it easier. And when Execute(Request) is called, it also runs all registered
    IPlugin modules. That's it, we can simply write an IPlugin implementation to inject our code
    when some changes are done to a customer contact.

1 public class CustomerContactHandler : IPlugin

2{

3     public void Execute(BusinessContext context)

4     {

5        var request = context.Request;

6        if (request.Target.MetaClassName != "Contact")

7        {

8           return;

9        }

10       if (request.Method == RequestMethod.Delete)

11       {

12          //Do something

13       }

14    }

15 }

    IPlugin has only one method Execute(BusinessContext) - which we implement here. We care
    about context.Request: if the target object being "handled" is of actual CustomerContact type -
CustomerContact events                                          317

otherwise we just skip it to avoid any performance overload, because our module will be called for
every Business Object of any types. Here's we are "listening" to Delete action, but you're free to
listen to other actions, here's the full list:

 1 public const string InitializeEntity = "InitializeEntity";
 2
 3 public const string Create = "Create";
 4 public const string Update = "Update";
 5 public const string Delete = "Delete";
 6 public const string Load = "Load";
 7 public const string List = "List";
 8
 9 public const string Import = "Import";
10 public const string InitializeMappingDocument = "InitializeMappingDocument";

     Normally, the CRUD operations are the ones we care about. Now build your project contains the
     class above and drop the assembly into bin folder of Commerce Manager, then try to delete a contact.

     Wait a minute. Nothing happens.

     Well, yes, because we haven't registered it. BusinessManager only cares about the plugins registered
     in Configs\baf.data.manager.config. Let's go there and add this simple line to <businessMan-
     ager><plugins> section:

1 <add method="*" metaClass="Contact" eventStage="PostMainOperation" type="EPiServer.C\
2 ommerce.Sample.CustomerContactEvent, EPiServer.Commerce.Sample" />

    The attributes speak for themselves - here we are listening to all methods for Contact meta class.
    Of course, you can change the method to listen to, "Create" only, for example. You can also change
    to listen to all meta classes, by changing "Contact" to "*", or listen to another Business Foundation
    MetaClass.

    evenStage is an interesting attribute - here we define when we want our plugin executed. There are
    4 possible values:

1 PreMainOperation = 1
2 PreMainOperationInsideTranasaction = 2,
3 PostMainOperationInsideTranasaction = 8,
4 PostMainOperation = 16,

    As the names might suggest, the stages are before the main action, before or after the main action,
    but within same transaction (if your module does some data modification, it should be here), or after
    the main action. In our example, we just want to have some notifications when a contact is deleted,
    so we register our plugin to be PostMainOperation.

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
CustomerContact events                                          318

There's a caveat here, we don't really know the result of the main operation. The only thing we
know is there was no error (no exception thrown). So we just assume that it completes successfully.

The final attribute is only the full name of our little type, with the assembly name.

And now, try deleting a contact again. And this time, magic should happen!

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
The Migration Steps

Migration Step is one of the hidden features of Episerver Commerce. It's been there since Episerver
Commerce 7.10 (I remember it better than anything else because it can be considered as my
baby). It's not public, it's even in a separated assembly with "Internal" part in its name (EPis-
erver.Commerce.Internal.Migration). And if you browse its API:s, it has document clearly states
that these API:s should only be used for Episerver infrastructure. Episerver also does not support
any backward compatibilities on the API:s, so if they want to change the interfaces or classes, to add
or remove a method, they are free to do so without having to obsolete anything or have to wait for
a major version. You're on your own and will need to update your class if that happens.

Then should you use it?

Well, the answer, as always, is: it depends. A Migration Step is supposed be one-time only operation.
The initialization modules provide one-time operation per every site startup, but here, it's one per
website. Episerver has been using Migration steps as the data-modifications for updates (it's not
necessary to have one migration step per update, in facts, the chance you run into migration steps
for a minor update is relatively small) . They have the upgrade SQL files to do DDL operations,
such as update stored procedures, add/remove columns, etc, and Migration steps for migrating data,
fixing something up. It's not strictly separated, thought. Sometimes upgrade SQL files can be used
to migrate data if it's easier and faster that way, but you can see where each one should fit.

The idea of Migration Step is quite simple. You implement a special interface (Can you guess it
name? Well, IMigrationStep), which has one method named Execute. The method will return true
or false to indicate its result. Every time your site start, MigrationManager will check if there is any
steps which are not run, and depends on your setting, it might redirect you to a special page to run
remaining steps, or start them automatically. So, migration steps are run after initialization modules,
and before anything else. There's a mechanism to make sure you have to complete migration steps
(for safety reasons, of course. You won't want to run your site with incorrect data). When a step
returns true to indicate it ran successfully, MigrationManager will store the information in the
database to remember for the next time. Otherwise, it does mean some errors happened, you might
need to correct them before trying again. When all migration steps are done, you can return to
normal operations.
    The Migration Steps                                                               320

                                                                Migration steps view

    OK let's try to implement a Migration Step.

1 public interface IMigrationStep

2{

3     /// <summary>

4     /// Gets the order of the step. The one with lowest order executes first.

5     /// </summary>

6     int Order { get; }

7

8     /// <summary>

9     /// Gets the name of the step

10    /// </summary>

11    string Name { get; }

12

13    /// <summary>

14    /// Executes the step.

15    /// </summary>

16    bool Execute(IProgressMessenger processMessenger);

17

18    /// <summary>

19    /// Gets or sets the description.

20    /// </summary>

21    string Description { get; }

22 }

    The two things you need to care about is Order and Excecute. Order defines which migration step

                              Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
   The Migration Steps                                                            321

   to run first - the lower Order, the higher it is in the queue. Execute simply runs the code, it takes an
   IProcessManager to let you display the progress in Migration view.

   Migration settings:

   You can configure the Migration Steps to automatically run when the site starts (and if there
   are steps which haven't run successfully, of course). You can set it either by setting (Add a key
   named AutoMigrateEPiServer to your appSettings section, with value as true), or by code, in your
   IInitializationModule:

1 public void Initialize(InitializationEngine context)

2{

3   context.Locate.Advanced.GetInstance<MigrationSettings>().AutoMigrate = true;

4}

   If the setting by configuration will always override the one by code.

   The migration steps reside in EPiServer.Commerce.Core package, but the migration view re-
   sides in EPiServer.Commerce.UI one. This can be an issue if you have installation without
   EPiServer.Commerce.UI (in a load-balancing environment for example). If you configured the
   migration steps to automatically run, then it will run without any issue, but if you didn't, an
   exception will be thrown during site startup. To avoid that issue, always start the installation with
   EPiServer.Commerce.UI first.

   The migration steps cannot run in the context of Commerce Manager. However, there is a safe
   check which will throw an exception if you start your Commerce Manager website without having
   the migration steps completed.

                        Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
The Migration Steps                                          322

                                                      It is not scary as it seems to be

In such case, simply run the migration steps in the front-end site, then restart Commerce Manager.

                     Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Part 9: Advanced stuffs

Finally, some advanced stuffs
Caching

      There are only two hard things in Computer Science: cache invalidation and naming
      things.

­ Phil Karlton

My boss likes this quote so much that he usually quotes it when we have a discussion about naming in
our reviews. But of course, cache invalidation is even harder and it's critical to your code correctness.

For a high performance site, you will sooner or later have to use cache (Fact: the sooner, the better).
Getting cache right is hard - it's an art of balancing between performance, memory and ease (Of
course, correctness is a non-compromise requirement).

There are very little few guidelines for caching in a Episerver Commerce site, it depends a lot in your
implementation. However, those following best practices are something you should really follow:

   · Use ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache. This interface comes from Episerver Framework
      and it's basically the barebone of caching system in entire Commerce. Unlike its parent
      interface - IObjectInstanceCache, ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache will work in system
      which is scaling over servers or sites. Some older classes, such as CatalogCache, was updated
      to use ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache internally. It's a quite simple interface with some
      methods to play with.

                 One thing to keep in mind is that ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache, at least the default
                 implementation, only guarantees that the cache validation is synchronized. So if you call
                 Remove(key) in your class, you're guaranteed that other instances of your site will not read
                 the obsolete cache value anymore.

   · Think about the cache key. Keep it short (while still being unique). The cache engine will have to
      match a lot of keys and long keys will give more pressures on your CPUs, and they themselves
      consume memory. I've seen cachekey which can be 200 characters long (!). Cache keys are for
      the computer, not for human, so you don't have to be verbose. Even if you want to debug it, a
      concise key which well define pattern will still easy to understand.

   · Don't cache too much, only cache things that you know for sure was not cached. Episerver
      Commerce already caches (almost) everything it needs, from contents to carts. You should
      only cache your own stuffs when you really know it'll hit the database if you don't. Having
      too many objects floating around in cache is a bad thing - it will consume memory (to some
Caching                                          325

      points, your system will throw OutOfMemory exception, or even worse, start saving RAM to
      page file, which slows the entire site), and having too many keys will slowdown the cache
      validation (key matching should still be very fast, but it's the master keys and dependent keys
      which is the issue).

You might ask what are actually cached in the system, well, more than you'd think:

   · Catalog content are cached by ContentProvider, except when a content it was loaded with
      ContentReference.GetPublishedOrLatest is true. This cache is managed by CMS system.

   · DTO:s are cached by default.
   · MetaObjects are cached by default
   · Carts are cached by default.
   · IPriceValue:s are cached by default.
   · Inventory information are cached by default.
   · Think carefully about the dependent keys, master keys, and the caching mode. It's best when

      you can fire-and-forget. Having an obsolete cache item might harm your code correctness, and
      in some situations it's even worse than no cache at all. Internally, Episerver applies a pattern
      like this, and it works quite well:

        ­ Define a cache key prefix which is short and unique, following the defined convention,
           for example: EP:EC:Catalog, where Catalog is the area we want to have cache.

        ­ Define a master key from the cache key: EP:EC:Catalog:*.

        ­ Define a cache key by the prefix + unique id, for example, EP:EC:Catalog:1, and when
           add the cache, add the master key to the list as well.

        ­ To clear the cache structure, simple call ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache.Remove(masterKey)
           - this will remove all cache items depend on that master key.

   · Think about the master keys to use - so you cache will automatically be invalidated when
      a specific object is invalidated. Episerver Commerce has this special class MasterCacheKeys -
      which can be used to get the master keys for almost everything which is cached:
        ­ RootKey: Is the master key for all Commerce caching, so if you call ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache.Re
           you effectively remove all Commerce cache.

        ­ AnyEntryKey and AnyNodeKey are the master keys for all nodes, and entries, respectively.

        ­ If you want your cache to be dependent on a specific node or entry, you can use
           MasterCacheKeys.GetEntryKey and MasterCacheKeys.GetNodeKey.

   · Think carefully about the expiration time of the cache. Most of the times, it's bad idea to cache
      indefinitely. Only when you know for certain that something needs to be in cache the entire
      time of the application, and/or the cache object is expensive to build, it's better to specify an
      expiration time in your CacheEvictionPolicy - a null TimeSpan parameter in constructor means
      that the CacheEntry will stay in the cache forever - well - until when you explicitly remove the
      cache, or the cache trimming thread kicks in, which (almost always) a bad thing. It's better to
      let the cache management knows when to trim cache when it's no longer needed. If you thing
      a cache entry is likely to be accessed often, just set the CacheTimeoutType to Sliding instead
      of Absolute - then when your cache object is read, its lifetime will be reset. Therefore cache
      entries which high cache hit level will remain in cache longer.

         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Caching                                                                            326

· Think about the race condition when you load from database and insert an item to the cache.
  I've seen this mistake more than once - and it can seriously affect your site's performance
  when it hits. Either no lock, or too restrictive lock can be a problem: No lock results in
  race condition when two or more threads simultaneously read or write to cache, causing
  waste (multiple database reads instead of just one) or even incorrectness. Too restrictive
  locks can cause serious deadlocks when other threads loading different data being blocked
  unnecessarily. More detailed discussion can be found here on my blog post³. In short, try
  to use ISynchronizedObjectInstanceCache.ReadThrough with ReadStrategy.Wait if possible
  (you're using CMS 10+, and therefore Commerce 10+, which you should), which solve both
  problems for you. It provides necessary locking on the reading cache key. Otherwise, a little
  custom implementation will be needed - ConcurrentDictionary<,> can be used so the lock
  object will not be shared when loading different objects.

· As we mentioned earlier - it's a best practice to make the data immutable before inserting
  to cache - this will help the correctness of cache and avoid cache item being changed
  unintentionally.

³http://vimvq1987.com/2017/02/episerver-commerce-performance-optimization-part-2/

         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Routing

Routing is important for any sites, and it's even more important for a Commerce site - it helps
driving traffic, which usually result in sales. People nowadays search for everything, and ranking
higher in the search results yield much better chance of your products being viewed and purchased.
(One of) The keys for higher ranking in search engine? Having the best URI you can have!
There are two routing systems in Episerver Commerce (as always!). The old one is based on SEO
URI, has been there since Commerce R1, and the new one, HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter, which
is a partial router, was introduced in Commerce 7.5.

The SEO Uri approach

This approach is based on the SEO Uri, so the link to your product will be in form of http://<yourdomain>/your-prod
Note that .aspx is the default, but you are free to choose another extension, or even no extension at
all. The SEO Uri can be edited in both Commerce Manager and Catalog UI

                                                 SEO Uri editing in Commerce Manager

Those information are stored in CatalogItemSeo for both Nodes and Entries, which each row
represents the information of one node or one entry in one language. Note that only the Uri is
unique and is used by the system, it's up to your site implementation to utilize other information
such as Title, Description - just follow best SEO practices.
Routing                                          328

The SEO Uri regeneration

The Uri is unique per language, and if you don't set it explicitly, the catalog system will
automatically generate them based on the entry/node's name while still ensure the uniqueness.
The default implementation will work like this:

   · If the SEO Uri is empty, generates it from the name by replacing any invalid characters, possibly
      adds the language code, and appends the .aspx extension.

   · If the Uri being saved is unique, save it.
   · If it is not unique, generate again by adding a random string to the end. (it's the 8 first characters

      of a newly created GUID)

This behavior can be overridden by inheriting from Mediachase.Commerce.Catalog.Data.UniqueSeoGenerator,
and then register your implementation as the default instance.

UniqueSeoGenerator is a simple class and all of its methods and properties are virtual, to override
the Uri generation, you can override three following:

1 protected virtual string UriExtension { get; }

2
3 public virtual string GenerateSeoUri(string name, string languageCode, bool includeR\
4 andomToken)

5
6 protected virtual string GetRandomToken()

    Usually, you will have the Uri set by some other external systems, or manually, but there are still
    cases when the Uri:s are automatically generated. There are several tips to make the Uri generation
    more effective:

        · Change the default UriExtension. It was kept ".aspx" for the backward compatibility, but I
          don't think that kind of extension is popular anymore.

        · Make sure your entries, especially your variations, to have unique names. This will generate
          better SEO-friendly URIs - no matter how you override GetRandomToken - having some artificial
          random string append to your Uri can't be a good thing.

        · When you made your Uri public, stick with it. Nothing worse than a hoping customer sees
          an 404 error for a product they planned to buy, because the Uri has been changed. Episerver
          Commerce has no mechanism to detect such changes to redirect customers to the new Uri, so
          don't change your Uri:s until absolutely necessary.

How it works

SEO Uri:s are handled by SeoUriRouter, which is an implementation of ISimpleAddressRouteHandler.
ISimpleAddressRouteHandler has only one method:

         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Routing  329

1 SimpleAddressRouteValues GetRouteValues(string urlSegment, ContentReference routeRoo\

2 t, SegmentContext segmentContext);

    SeoUriRouter implements this, and it will only handle the urlSegment without any slash (/) in
    between - so /this-can-be-a-seo-uri/ is processed, but /this/cannot/be/a/seo/uri/ is not. It
    then try to get the content from the SEO Uri, and if you are wondering, there are methods in
    ICatalogSystem to do that - GetCatalogEntryByUriDto(string seoUri, string language) and
    GetCatalogNodeDto(string seoUri, string language) ³. If a matching content to be found, then
    a SimpleAddressRouteValues value will be returned, indicating this router has processed successfully
    the urlSegment.

    Simple enough, right? However, the design of SeoUriRouter has some limitations: It's registered by
    default, and with the routing system, the first router to be able to "catch" the uri wins, so it's be a
    challenge if you want to replace the behavior. We'll look for more detail in the next section.

The hierarchical approach:

This "new" approach (today it's not new anymore, perhaps we can call it newer?) was introduced
in Commerce 7.5 to make the routing of Catalog contents inline with CMS content.

The core concept of this approach is to reflect the structure of contents to the structure of the Uri,
for the discoverability. With SEO Uri approach, customers will never know how to get to the parent
node, to see the other items in same category. With the partial routing, they can. To allow that,
every catalog content has a piece of information - RouteSegment. This is saved in CatalogItemSeo
as UriSegment, but is hidden for editing in Commerce Manager³

                                       RouteSegment, known as "Name in URL" in Catalog UI

   ³I know, I've been telling you to avoid ICatalogSystem as much as possible and embrace the new content API:s. This is almost always

true. However, there are some functions provided by ICatalogSystem which are not replaced by new content API:s. In such case, you'll have
to use ICatalogSystem. It's been improved a lot internally anyway.

   ³Except for the UriSegments of catalogs, which are saved in CatalogLanguage table.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Routing                                                                           330

RouteSegment is also supposed to be unique, but only within its sibling. Same as SEO Uri,
RouteSegment is generated by UniqueSeoGenerator, but by another method:

1 public virtual string GenerateUriSegment(string name, bool includeRandomToken)

    It works pretty similar with GenerateSeoUri, except without extension part.

How it works

HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter implements ICommerceRouter, which itself inherit from IPartialRouter<TCont
TRoutedData>, IPartialRouter has two methods for incoming and ongoing URL:

1 PartialRouteData GetPartialVirtualPath(TRoutedData content, string language, RouteVa\

2 lueDictionary routeValues, RequestContext requestContext);

3 object RoutePartial(TContent content, SegmentContext segmentContext);

    For incoming URL, the router will parse the URL from the left to the right in RoutePartial. The
    first segment is supposed to match the "root content" (not the RootContent, but the configured root)
    - and the next segment is supposed to match the RouteSegment of one of the children of the content
    match the previous segment, recursively. If a content matches the entire route, it'll be returned.

    For outgoing, GetPartialVirtualPath will build the URL form right to left. Starting with the content
    is being routed, it'll append the RouteSegment of its parent content to the left of the URL, until it
    reach the root content.

    HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter has three important parameters to pass to its constructor:

        · RouteStartingPoint, which is a Func<ContentReference>. It is supposed to return a ContentReference
          to the starting point of the route. If you call CatalogRouteHelper.MapDefaultHierarchialRouter(RouteCollect
          routes, bool enableOutgoingSeoUri), it'll be the ContentReference.StartPage (aka SiteDefi-
          nition.StartPage). Because this is a Func<T>, it'll be evaluated everytime an URL being routed,
          then it's really dynamic. If you have multiple start pages, it can detect the language the
          customer is on, and use the right segment. If you set the Shortcut setting for your start page,
          it can use that, too. Just remember to use a fast Func<ContentReference>, otherwise it'll be hit
          bad.

        · CommerceRoot: if you call CatalogRouteHelper.MapDefaultHierarchialRouter(RouteCollection
          routes, bool enableOutgoingSeoUri), it'll be the "true" Commerce Root, but you are not
          limited by that. You can construct an instance of HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter which
          takes a CatalogContentBase and then register it directly. This can be done in your initialization
          module:

         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Routing                                                                      331

1     var referenceConverter = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<ReferenceConverter>(\

2 );

3

4     var contentLoader = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<IContentLoader>();

5

6     //Assuming you want to use your only catalog

7     var commerceRootContent = contentLoader.GetChildren<CatalogContent>(referenceCon\

8 verter.GetRootLink()).First();

9

10    //RegisterPartialRouter is an extension from EPiServer.Web.Routing

11    RouteTables.Routes.RegisterPartialRouter(new HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter(st\

12 artingPoint, commerceRootContent, enableOutgoingSeoUri));

    This is useful in case you want to hide parts of your catalog structure - because it might be sensitive
    or boring to show it to customers. For example, using the default CommerceRoot means your catalog
    RouteSegment will be included in the url, like this: http://<your-site>/en/departmental-catalog/Departments/Fa
    If you have only on catalog and don't want it to be shown, simply point it to your catalog. Your URL
    will appear to be http://<your-site>/en/Departments/Fashion/ only. Of course, nothing prevent
    you from pointing your RouteStartingPoint to a category, if you want to. In this example, I can set
    the Departments category to be the RouteStartingPoint and remove it from the URL.

      · enableOutgoingSeoUri, which is a boolean - if it's true, then HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter
        will work with SEO URI instead. Any hierarchical URL which matches a catalog content will
        be redirected to its SEO URI.

    Redirecting

    A very good thing about Episerver Commerce is that even you enabled the partial routing system,
    the old SEO Uri:s will continue to work (of course, as long as you keep the Uri:s unchanged). So they
    can coexist in same website - but that might not what you want - you might want to stick with only
    one routing system - it's been told that the more popular your URL is, the higher rank it gets in the
    search engine results.

    From hierarchical URL to SEO URI

    This one is pretty easy, just call this during your site's initialization, the true parameter means you
    want to enable Outgoing Seo URI, and the next statement configures the redirect:

1 CatalogRouteHelper.MapDefaultHierarchialRouter(RouteTable.Routes, true);
2 CatalogRouteHelper.SetupSeoUriPermanentRedirect();

             Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Routing                                          332

From SEO URI to hierarchial URI:

This one is tricky, there is no such built-in method allows us to do so. We can't even override
SeoUriRouter because it's registered automatically and there is no way to guarantee that our router
will be able to run before it. (If a matching router is found, the processing will stop). Let's explore
other options.

EPiServer.Web.Routing.ContentRoute has two events which might be interesting - RoutingContent
and RoutedContent. As their names might suggest, the former is fired before any routers take place,
while the latter is fired after that. We might not want to listen to RoutingContent because it's too
early, we'll have to process all URI:s sent to our site, which will slow it down. It's better to only
process after the URI has been routed successfully, we prefer it is processed by SeoUriRouter. We
can do some checks and redirect if necessary.

First, we need to register to RoutedContent event:

1 ContentRoute.RoutedContent += Routed_SeoUri;

    And then implement it. It's another void method which takes an object as sender and an RoutingEventArgs
    as the EventArgs. RoutingEventArgs has SegmentContext property named RoutingSegmentContext,
    this is what we need. A successfully routed route will set the RouteObject of RoutingSegmentContext
    to the content it found. So we can check if that content is a catalog content or not, to see if we should
    redirect.

    But how can we know if the URI was SEO URI, or hierarchial URI? By checking for slash in the
    between? Not really effective, because the top level content only has one segment. We can mimick
    SeoUriRouter and try to load the Entry/Node by Uri? Yes that might work but it is not really fast. If
    we are lucky and the URI was SEO URI, the DTO should have been cached, but if it was hierarchial
    URI, we will have to hit the database to find nothing!

    Solution, well, HierarchicalCatalogPartialRouter will process to the last segment, and for each
    segment, the LastConsumedFragment is assigned to next segment, so it'll be empty at the end.
    SeoUriRouter, in other hand, does not really process any segment, so the LastConsumedFragment
    it returns will be the same path as requested.

    With that information, we can have this as our method:

         Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
    Routing                                                                       333

1 private static void Routed_SeoUri(object sender, RoutingEventArgs e)

2{

3     var context = e.RoutingSegmentContext;

4     //RoutedObject is supposed to not be null here

5     if (!(context.RoutedObject is CatalogContentBase))

6     {

7            return;

8     }

9

10    if(string.IsNullOrEmpty(context.LastConsumedFragment))

11    {

12           return;

13    }

14    var urlResolver = ServiceLocator.Current.GetInstance<UrlResolver>();

15    context.PermanentRedirect(urlResolver.GetUrl(context.RoutedContentLink));

16 }

    And now we can redirect the SEO URI to Hierarchical URI in an effective way.

                      Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Appendix

Performance optimizations

Performance is an important aspect of any websites, and even more important with an Commerce
website - Amazon studies have shown that slower responses result in a loss in revenue, and the
customer satisfaction. However performance can be a complicated topic that can be difficult to
measure, and even more difficult to achieve. So how can you build fast Episerver Commerce website:

   · Keep performance in mind when you develop your website. It does not mean that you have
      to optimize prematurely. But whenever you do something that are data-heavy, think if you
      should do it that way (or if you should do it at all). If in doubt, check, or ask. There might be
      a better way to do it, that's why deep understanding about the framework is very important
      (And I hope when you reach this page, your understanding/knowledge of Episerver Commerce
      has improved. My intention with this book is nothing more than making you use Commerce
      better). Keeping performance in mind from the start will always yield better result than "make
      it work, we can optimize that later". Even better, consider performance is a must-have feature.
      Define your key measurement that you would like to see in your website (response times,
      latency, ... ) and stick with them, measure them regularly to avoid steep drop in performance
      when a new feature is added.

   · Use the latest version possible. I can't emphasize this enough. Based on feedback from hundreds
      of partners and from internal findings, Episerver Commerce development team will incorporate
      the performance improvements in newer version. (We at Episerver take performance seriously,
      and there are even some people who can be described as "obsessed with performace". Well,
      that includes me). You can be good at performance tuning, but Episerver has the decication
      and resources to do that better, and they can do it at core level (something that you can't do
      yourself in many cases). No matter how much effort you can throw at it, Episerver can still do
      better. I was once asked for advices for optimizing an Episerver Commerce 8.16 website, and my
      answer is: upgrade it to latest version, or at least Commerce 9.24.1. The performance gain of just
      upgrading the website will almost certainly be better than any "tuning" or "optimizing" results
      they can get from an old version. Time and effort can be used to optimize your implementation.
      A fast website is a combination of fast framework (our part), a fast implementation (your part),
      and a fast hosting solution (DXC/Azure... part). You can't be fast without any of them.

   · Profilers are your friends. When something is slow than you would like, uses a profiler
      (jetBrains dotTraces, ANTS Performance Profiler, SQL Server Profiler, etc.) to find where the
      bottle neck is. Once you know what is wrong, it's half way to resolve the problem. If it's in
      your code, fix it. If it's in the framework, report to Episerver. As I just said above, Episerver
      will address the problem as soon as possible and an issue affecting customer's business will
Appendix  335

always get a high priority treatment. This is another reason why you should to stay up to date.
If you want a bug fix, you can simply update to the next version (in most case it's an easy and
hassle free task) when it comes out. Things will be uglier if you are in a major version behind.

These are pretty much some very high level guidelines. Performance is a topic I usually write about
on my blog, if you are interested, check out my posts at https://vimvq1987.com

How to ensure a successful upgrade.

I, as an Episerver employee, always recommend our customers to upgrade to the latest version
possible. Well, of course that is constrained by a number of things - time (you might be in a deadline
next Tuesday), money (upgrading might mean license renewal and that costs money. I'm not allowed
to discuss about the licensing, but one thing for sure it's not free), uncertainty (our system is working,
why upgrade), etc. But if you can, you always should:

   · Newer version is better. More features. More bugs fixed. More performance. Period. Try to
      import your catalog in Commerce 8. Then try with Commerce 9. It's not even close.

   · Fresh code is better. Says, you have on problem and you ask Episerver developers for help in
      World forums. They (aka me and my colleagues) will try to help you the best they can, but they
      need to understand the problem first. If you are in very recent version, they can fire up Visual
      Studio, look at the code and say "OK, it works this way". But if you're in a very old version,
      it'll be like this "Anyone remember how did it work in <dinosaur> version. I think we changed
      it last time, right? No one?" and then they will have to dig through a very long history, just to
      find an answer. You can wait? (A moment of truth, I take breaks to answer questions on World.
      I answer quickly if I can, but if the problem is complicated, and/or the version is old, I usually
      have to forward the original poster to developer support service, because I just can't dig into
      that much code in 5 minutes.)

   · Bug fixes come sooner. Episerver developers don't fix bug, they write perfect code. No I lied.
      Despite their best effort to avoid bugs and QA's best effort to catch bugs, it still happens,
      sometimes. If you have a bug in a very recent version, it'll be easier to fix and you can get the
      fixed version in 2-3 weeks (the time it takes for you to report the bug, then it's triaged, fixed,
      and tested). If you were in last major version, or even older, there will be no hotfixes for you.
      And in the end you'll have to upgrade. Why not stay ahead in your game?

Of course, upgrading is not an hassle-free task. (But it has been much more easier in recent years).
If you have gone through all the way to upgrade your site from Commerce R1/R2 to latest version,
then Congratulations! Next time you're in Stockholm, I will buy you one beer or two. If you are
planning to do that, here's something that might help you:

   · Backing up is the key. Always back up your site and your databases before you start. I know
      you've done it, but I never feel that I can urge this enough.

                                                 Creating powerful eCommerce solutions.
Appendix                                                                                                336

· It's better to work with small steps than a big one. You will have more control. (If you play
  games, you will know why this is so important. It's better to replay the last 15 minutes when
  you made a wrong decision, because you saved before an important checkpoint than throwing
  your controller because you would have to replay the entire day). Always upgrade to next
  major version, instead of upgrade to the latest major version in one run. (And in term of major
  version, Commerce 7.5 counts as one). Upgrade, check if everything, including basic features
  in both front-end and back-end sites work normally. Back up your sites and databases. Then
  continue. You can try to upgrade to 2-3 major versions above, and it might work but I would
  not recommend that. When something happens (and it always happens), you won't know what
  went wrong.

· In most cases, you should upgrade your front-end site first. Database changes are done
  there. Migration Steps are done there. When everything is good, you're free to upgrade your
  Commerce Manager. Unless you have a heavily customized Commerce Manager, upgrading
  Commerce Manager is usually much easier.

· Watch the migration steps closely. All of them should be run successfully before you start using
  your sites. Note that from Commerce 8.16, Migration steps have been mandatory, you can't no
  longer, intentionally or accidentally, prevent them from starting.

          I've seen a site where migration steps appear to be completed, but they did not successfully
          run, resulting in some very weird and serious issues, which could not be diagnose until I
          looked the databases closely. Simply put: Migration steps are to be run and you should not
          tamper with them in any ways.

   · If you upgrade from an older version where catalog content versions do not exist (Commerce
      R3a or earlier), it does make sense to export your catalogs before upgrading, then import them
      after upgrading. Truth is, the catalog import/export is much more robust than upgrade scripts
      and migration steps. This can also apply for newer versions, if you do not care for draft versions
      and such.

Now what

Phew. It's been a long run. Writing this book is probably the biggest side project I've had. It took me
hundreds of hours to think, to write and to rewrite, and I sincerely hope I made it worth the time
you spent reading it (and the money you spent buying it, of course).
I tried to cover the most, in the best ways I can, to help you understand the system, how it works, how
to use it and use it properly and efficiently. But truth is, Episerver Commerce is a big framework,
and with the extensibility, no book can cover everything. This book intends to be a beginning, when
you can start your long but exciting and rewarding road with Episerver Commerce development.
I wish you luck in your projects with Episerver Commerce! And I'll meet you on the field.
